My dear Mr. Secretary:
A situation has recently arisen in Bering Sea that may easily be-
come of serious import to the salmon fishery in Alaske waters, partic-
ularly in Bristol Bay where the most important red salmon fishery of the
2
world is located.
894,628/
On June 28, 1930, the Conmissioner of Fisheries, who is now enroute
to Alaska, wired from Seattle that he had been advised that a Japanese
steamer of about eight thousand tons accompanied by a smaller steamer of
about nine hundred tons, a large sailing ship, and twenty-five or thirty
large power launches were fishing off Port oller, Bering Sea, with gear
out for thirty-five or forty miles. Fishing operations were apparently
directed toward cod, halibut and crais.
The Bureau of Fisheries took up the matter with the U. S. Coast
Guard which issued telegraphic instructions to the Conmander of the Ber-
ing Sea Patrol Force to investigate fully the activities of the Japanese
vessels operating off Port moller. A telegraphic reply received by the
Coast Guard from the Conmander of the Berin Sea Patrol Force is as fol-
lows:
9901 your 9930 1400 Early vodey/CHELA! boarding officer along-
side Japanese cannery steamer TAI OYU MARU of Fuchu from
Hakodate period Oma Master Nippon Kosen Kaisha owner period

Alaska fisheries service of the Bureau of Fisheries. He is now at Naknek,
Bristol Bay, Alaska.

=== Page 2 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
The building up of an extensive fishery by the Japanese in waters of
Bering Sea close to the coast of Alaska is fraught with serious possibili-
ties to American fisheries. While it appears that operations are at pres-
ent being confined to taking and canring crabs, the extension of these
efforts to other fishery products is uch more than a possibility. An ar-
ticle appearing in the October, l929, issue of the Pacific Fisherman, pub-
lished at Seattle, Washington, is as follows:
JAPANESE FLOATING CAINERY PROJECT
A Japanese floating cannery project of noteworthy size and
of more than usual interest to North Anerican fishery operators
is being formed in Tokyo. The fact that the company plans to
operate in the Bering Sea, as well as the Sea of Okhotsk, and
that it has connections with Crosse & Blackwell, Ltd., of London
gives it particular signiricance in the eyes of American and Ca-
nadian interests.
The company, known as the Kokusai Floatin Cannery company,
will have a capital of 10,000,000 yen, approximately $4,670,000.
The promoters of the company expect to be operating in the Bering
Sea during their first business year with two trawlers of 80 tons
each, one 11,000-ton floating cannery, five x64-ton trawlers and
a floating salmon factory of 6,600 short tons.
The Nichiro Fishery company will have 80,000 of the 200,000
shares of stock, the pronoters of the company will take 70,000
shares and the remainin: 50, 000 shares will o to interested in-
dividuals.
The output or the new company is said to have been contracted
to Crosse & Blackwell, Ltd., wich will make an auvarce payment of
1,260,900, it is reported. This 'und, witn the paid-in capital
of $1,167,500, will be devoted to defraying initial expenses.
The company's prospectus estinates a nual income at 2,568,500,
with a profit of $887,300. Allowing $233, 500 for depreciation of
fixed property and ll6,75o for fis ine ri hts, it is estinated a
20 per cent dividend can be paid.
If the Japanese extend their activities to salmon fis ing in the Bristol
Bay region of Lerine Sea, they will no doubt work havoc wit the salmon in-
dustry of that region. his recion supports the largest red salmon fishery
and the annual output is worth millions of dollars to Aerican industry.
This fishery within territorial waters is  rotected by Federal law and regu-
lations which are strictly enrorced. Even if the Japanese fishery operations
are not extended to include salmon, it is entirely within the realm of possi-
bility that present activities may interce t and deflect reat numbers of red
salmon while on their annual igratory movenent from orfshore or high-sea
waters to the teiritorial vaters of Bristol Bay.

=== Page 3 === (confidence 0.94)
- 3 -
If operations of the Japanese are extended to halibut it is evident
that the benefits that arise fron the conve tion between the United States
and Canada for the protection of the alibut lis eries of the North Pacific
may ultimately be largely if not al to ether nullified.
The situation as it exists is bro it to the uttention of the Depart-
ment of State for its information ad with the hope tat consideration of
the matter may su  est some course of action to be pursued in the event
that the Japanese operations under consideration become a detriment to the
American fishery industries of the Pacific coast.
The Bureau of Fisheries and the Coast Guard are keepin the matter
under carerul observation. m furt.er im ortant developments will be com-
municated to the De ert ent of State.
Very truly yours,
EfMryan
Acting Secretery of Conmerce.

My dear Mr. Secretary:
August 2. Y980.
The receipt is acknowledged of the Department of State's lotter.
of the 19th instant, File FE894.628/40, advising that the American
力
Embassy at Tokyo has been requested to obtain and forward any further
available information concerning the activities and plans of the com-
pany which has dispatched the Japanese cannery steamer Taihoyu Maru
9
to waters of Bering Sea adjacent to the north side of the Alaskan
peninsula. The Department of Commerce will be very much interested
2
in the further developments along this line.
As bearing upon the subject of the invasion of these waters by
Japanese fishing vessels, there are transmitted herewith copies of two
reports sent to the Commissioner of Fisheries by the Acting Commandant
of the United States Coast Guard. These reports are from the Command-
er of the Bering Sea Patrol Force, one being dated July 2 and the
other July 7, l930, and refer to observations made by the Commander of
the Bering Sea Patrol Force in respect to the operations of the Japa-
nese fishing vessels under consideration.
It is of interest to note that the master of the Japanese steamer
Taihoyu Maru, although freely giving information in regard to the ves-
sel, would not, when requested, lower a ladder and permit the boarding
officer from the Coast Guard Cutter Chelan to go aboard the Taihoyu
Maru. It is said that the master of the latter vessel stated that he
had orders from his owners not to permit any foreigners on board.
The Department of State will be advised promptly of any further
developments of interest in this matter.
Very tryly yours,
i
Acting Secretary of Commerce.

Valdez, Alaska.
7 July 1930.
From:
Commander, Bering Sea Patrol Force.
To :
Commandant.
Subject:
Japanese Steamer TAIHOYU MARU; fishing in Bering Sea.
Reference:
(a) Commander, Bering Sea Patrol Force letter 2 July 1930
(611).
(b) Commander, Bering Sea Patrol Force despatch 9904-2025
(July 1930 - C..Code).
(c) CHLAN despatch to Dennis Winn, Naknek, Alaska, 4 July
1930.
(d) Despatch from Dennis Winn to CHELAN 7 July 1930.
Inclosures: (1) Copies of References (c) and (d).
1.
Supplementing my report contained in reference (a), relative
to Japanese Steamer TAIHOYU MARU, fishing in Bering Sea, Headquarters is
informed that the CHkLAN while en-route from Port Heiden, Alaska, to
Valdez, Alaska, 4 July l930, steered a course so as to pass through the
position of subject vessel as given in reference (a). The weather was
thick and foggy, and it was expected that the position would be reached
about 4:00 p.m. 4 July 1930. However, at 3:00 p.m. that day, the lookout
roported a large steamer close aboard on port bow and the CHkLAN was
immodiately stopped. It was found that the steamer was the Japanese
floating cannery TAIHOYU MARU of Fuchu, and from our dead reckoning posi-
tion it was seen that the subject vessel had moved about nine miles east-
ward of her former position, to Latitude 560-20' N, Longitude l610-17' W.
These facts were reported to Headquarters by reference (b). In the
vicinity of the Japanese steamer five small tenders were seen hauling in
nets and catching spider crabs. Large piles of crabs could be seen on
the forward and after decks of the steamer. The Japanese steam beam
trawler MYOGI MARU reported in reference (a) as seen in the same locality,
was not observed in the vicinity, probably on account of the thick fog
prevailing. Particular efforts were made on the part of the Commanding
Officer of the CHkLan and the officers on the bridge to observe whether

=== Page 8 === (confidence 0.99)
mot any fur seals were being taken by the Japanese steamer, by steeming
elose to that vessel and the small tenders. No signs of seals or sealing
gear whatsoever were seen and only large piles of crabs were noticed, on
the steamer, and the tenders hauling in nets were fishing for spider crabs.
In this conneetion Headquarters is advised that the writer has never seen
any fur seals in the waters of Bristol Bay. Usually the fur seals come
from the Pacific Ooean through Unimak Pass, Akatan Pass and other passes,
to the Westward, and proceed directly to the Pribilof Islands.
8. As stated in reference (a), a conference was held at Naknek, Alaska,
with Mr. Dennis Wimn, Agent of the Bureau of Fisheries at that place, rela-
tive to subjeet floating cannery. At this conference it was suggested by
the writer that the Bureau of Fisheries maintain a guard in the locality of
the Japanese steamer, which Mr. Winn said he would do, if at all possible.
Reference (e) is a copy of CHkLaN despatch to Mr. Winn at Naknek advising
him that the Japanese cannery vessel had changed her position nine miles
to the Eastward. Referenoe (b) is a copy of a despatch from Mr. Winn to the
CHMAN, received 7 July l930, stating that he will observe their activities.
Enroute to Valdez, Alaska, with the floating court, the Foree Commander has
been endeavoring to get in communication with Mr. O'Malley, Commissioner
of Fisheries, in order to give that officer first hand information of the
situation off Port Moller, Bristol Bay. From information received, it is
believed that Mr. O'Malley is enroute to Juneau on U.S. Bureau of Fisheries
Steamer BRaNT. Should the Commissioner of Fisheries arrive in the vicinity
of Valdez or Seward within the nert few days, full information will be
given to him by the Foree Commander, and transportation furnished him to
Bristol Bay on the CHAN, should he decide to proceed to the present loca-
tion of the TAIHOYU MRU. Should the Force Commander be in a position to
obtain any further information in this matter, it will be forwarded to
Headquarters by despatoh, and by letter in detail.
R.W. DEMPWOLF.

Subjeet: Japanese steamship TAIHOYU MARU; fishing in Bering Sea.
Reference: (a) Headquarters' despatch 9930-1400 (June 1930).
(b) Bering Sea Patrol Force despateh 990l-0l05 (July 1930).

(64-611) copy to Headquarters and CHELAN.
(a) Cruise report UNALGA, 24 June 1930 (601-611) paragraph 3.
1.
In accordance with instructions of reference (a), the following
report on subject is submitted:
On 20 June 1930 the UNALGA reported to Force Commander by
despateh that the Japanese Government Training Ship HAkUYO MARU of Imperial
Fisheries Institute, Tokyo, Japan, was anchored off Port Moller, on Baird
Bank, 560-08'N l6l -32'w, fishing for crabs; also that another unidentified
steamer was anchored about five miles further off shore (reference (d).)
As reference (e) which contained a copy of despatch from the Governor of
Alaske, accounted for the presence of the Japanese Government Steamer
HAKUYO MARU in Bering Sea, no action was taken or deemed necessary.
2. The CHkLAN arrived in Unalaska with Floating Court 25 June
19se and on 26 June 1930 the above mentioned Japanese Government vessel
HAKUYO MARU anchored in Dutch Harbor. Captain J. Nakakawa called on Foroe
Commander who returned the call and extended the usual courtesies of the
port to officers, students and crew. Inquiry was made of the Japanese
Captain as to the identity of the large steamer anchored North of his posi-
tion on Baird Bank. He stated he did not know, in the presence of Foree
Commander and Commander Starr of the UNALGA, who was present in cabin of
CHELAN when Captain Nakakawa called. On 28 June 1930 U.S. Bureau of
Fisheries steamer CRank reported passing a large four masted steamer
anchored off Nelson's Lagoon with three steam tenders and one deisel tender
in attendance with fishing gear spread over a radius of so miles. The
CRANE could not identify the steamer account of bad weather. On same date
American steamship STARR reported a Japanese steamer, evidently a floating
cannery anchored in same locality.
On 88 June 1930 the CHILAN with Force Commander and Floating
Court proceeded to St. Paul, Pribiloff Island, landed court on 29
June 1930 to seleet and appoint U.8. Commissioner for St. Paul. The
CHELAN left same date fer reported position of Japanese steamer anchored
efr Welson Lagoon on Baird Bank, arriving O6s0 on 30 June, prior to
receipt of reference (a).

=== Page 11 === (confidence 0.98)
The CHmaN stopped elose aboard the Japanese steamer anchored
l8'w lel-Sa'w. Lieutenant (j.s.) L.B.Tollaksen, as boarding
ficer, went alongside, and obtained the following information from
The steamship is the Japanese Cannery steamship TAIHOYU MARU of
Fuehu, from Hakodate, Japan and bound to return same port about 1 August
1930. Y. Oma 1s Master and the steamer is owned by Nippon Kosen Kaisha.
The vessel is a four masted, one funnel steamer of 7834 tons, 500 feet
long, 52 feet beam, having a crew of about 300 men. She was formerly of
Canadian registry, the master stated. Three power launches hauling in
nets and a stean beam trawler MYOGI MaRU engaged in trawling were seen in
same locality, besides numerous markers and floats, spread over a large
aroa.
The Japanose steamer TAIHOYU MARU with its outfit of launches and
trawler is engaged in fishing for spider crabs and canning same. The
boarding officer reported that although the Master gave him all the above
information freely, he would not when requested lower a sea ladder and
permit him to go aboard the TAIHOYU MARU. He notified the CHILAN'S
boarding officer that he had orders from his owners not to permit any
foreigners on board. As there was no evidence that the vessel was or had
been engaged in pelagic sealing, the Force Commander did not resort to
foreeful boarding and search, inasmuch as the TAIHOYU MARU was outside
torritorial limits and had not violated any of the Articles of the Con-
vention dated July 7, 19ll to protect Seal Herds and Sea Otters in the
North Pacific Ocean including Bering Sea, or Department of Commerce
Circular No. 251.
5. The position in which the Japanese Steamer TAIHOYU MARU was
found anchored in 37 fathoms water, is 21 miles from shore off Nelson
Lagoon, Alaska, on Baird Bank - Latitude 560-18'N - 161º-32'W. The
position was checked by dead reckoning, two observations of.sun and by
taking a sounding of 37 fathoms, close aboard the steamer.
6. On 1 July 1930 the Force Commander conferred with Mr. Dennis
Winn, Agent of Alaska for Bureau of Fisheries, on board the CHELAN at
Naknek Anchorage and furnished that official all of the above information,
which he will no doubt transmit to the Commissioner of Fisheries in the
Department of Commerce.
7. The Japanese Government Steamer HAkUYO MaRU is a training ship of
the Imperial Fisheries Institute, Tokyo, Japan. When Force Commander visited
that vessel in Dutch Harbor, it was found to be a modern steel vessel of
about 2000 tons, Deisel Drive, well equipped with all modern appliances. The
personnel consisted of 30 crew, 32 students, and 10 officers and instructors.
▲ regular class room with tables, benches, and blackboards was located aft
on main deck and forward on main deck, a minature canning outfit of mchinery.
Although reference (c) would indicate this Japanese Government vessel is in
Bering Sea to observe the route followed by the seal herd and the mothods
employed in enforcing the regulations against poaching, it is believed they
are and have been this season and previous seasons (vessel was reported in

=== Page 12 === (confidence 0.97)
ttu, Atka and Bristol Bay l929) engaged in locating fishing banks; also that
sp1der crabs were located on Baird Bank last season by the HAkUYO MaRU, the
discovery being commercialized this season, by the subject Japanese Cannery
Steamer TAIHorU MARU, now engaged in fishing for and canning these crabs in
large quantities.
R.W. DDMPWOLF.

The Honorable
The Secretary of state,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to Instruction o. 81
of July 19, 1930, in wlich was enclosed a copy of a letter
from the Department of Commerce dated July 8, 1930, re-
lating to the activities of the Japanese camery steamer
TAIOKU MRU in the Bering Sea. The name TAI OYU NARU
appearing in the Instruction is apparently an error.
The Department directs me to obtain and forward any
available information concerning the activities and
plans or the Kokusai Floating Cannery Company, referred
to

=== Page 17 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
to in the letter of the Department of Conmerce.
I imediately took the matter up with the Foreign
Office and was slown a report that the Japanese Government
had received from the Japanese consul in Seattle. The report
contained substantially the facts as outlined in the letter
of the Department of Commerce enclosed in the Instruction
under reference. The of.icials informed me orally that the
TAIfOKU LARU and the recently formed Kokusai Floating Canne ry
Company were interested only in crabs, and that, so far as was
known, tnere was no intention on their part to engage in
halibut or salmon fishing. I was also informeu t at the
Japanese fisnery interests had been advised t.at their
operations in waters adjacent to the Alaskan litoral were
interfering with the flow of salmon to the coasts, and that
they would be warned not to conduct their operations in that
region when the salmon were making their way to the rivers
for spawning.
In this connection, I may state that Japanese fisning
interests have for a number of years had contracts for the
sale of canned crab to Cross and Blackwell and to other London
suppliers. So far as I know, the Japanese do not engage in
the extensive export of other forms of sea food. Fish in
sufficient quantities for the Japanese market are available
in Japanese waters or from the fishery concessions which the
Japanese have along the Russian coast. I am further informed
that the only fishing profitable to Japanese in the Bering Sea
is crab, as other forms of sea food are available in nearby
waters and it is not remunerative to bring fish from a distance
to

to Japanese ports
Respectfully yours,
Edui T. muilh

the Secvetury of State,

Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Department's
894.628 +1
instiuction No. 85 of August 2, 1930, unt to the
Embassy's reply thereto regarding the uctivities of
Japanese crab canming steamers in Bering Sea. I desire
also to invite attention to despatch No. 259 of August
22, 1930 and other co respondence in regard to Japanese
difficulties with the Soviet authorities over fishing
in waters near the Siberian coast. The Japanese consume
large quantities of fish of all kinds. It is & stable
article

=== Page 22 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
artiole of diet in this country. Certain fisheries
products like canned crab, whale products and dried
abalone are exported. These represent really a small
portion only of Japanese fisheries activities. By far
the greatest part of the Japanese catch each year is
consumed at home. The exports are an insignificant
portion of the fisheries as a whole.
The development of refrigeration in the past few
years has enabled the Japanese fishery companies to
send their ships farther afield. Modern cannery prac-
tice is another factor aiding deep sea or distant
fishing activities. I feel that we must expect a con-
tinuance of Japanese fishing in Bering Sea, assuming
that an abundance of fish is to be had there. While
at the moment their activities in the Alaska coast
region are confined to crabs, I have no doubt that
they will be extended to include other sea food if
and when improved methods of refrigeration and canning
at sea make it profitable.
These questions, it seems to me, should be
studied and threshed out by experts familiar with
the matters in hand.
For the moment the Fur Seals
Treaty governs the sealing industry in North Pacific
waters.
This treaty, however, can be denounced at
any time now by its own terms. I do not say that the
Japanese have any immediate intention of denouncing it.
I do not think they have, but they desire a modifica-
tion of its terms.
While the Fur Seals question is under control for
the

=== Page 23 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
the moment, the fishing industry proper is under no
control at all, so far as we and the Japanese are
concerned. As I indicated in my despatoh under re-
ference, the Japanese authorities have warned their
orab fisheries to keep away from areas near the
Alaska coast where they are likely to interfere with
the movements of salmon. There is, however, no law
under whioh the Japanese Government can compel them to
do so; nor can we do so, so long as they remain outside
of strictly territorial waters.
All things considered, therefore, I am inclined
to the belief that the only remedy or preventive is a
comprehensive fishery treaty. As matters now stand,
neither the Japanese nor ourselves have any law to
711.948
proteot salmon, halibut or other fish in non-terri-
torial waters. If such protection is necessary, the
action of all interested govermments should be ap-
proximately identical or at least similar. A matter
of this kind cannot be arranged without concerted
action.
Respectfully yours,
Edin I. hoitk
Edwin L. Neville
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim.

My dear Mr. Secretary:
The receipt is acknowledged of the Department of State's lettef of
GgC.
.62
September 12, 1930, file FE 894.628/43, with inclosure of a despatch
dated August 20, 1930, from the American Embassy at Tokyo in regaru to
28/46
the activities of the Japanese can ery steamer Taihoku aru in Alaska
waters.
The information thus conveyed is of particular interest to the
Department of Connerce. In the despatch the followine statement appears:
I was also informed that the Japanese fishery interests
had been advised that t.eir operations in waters adjacent to
the Alaskan litoral were i teriering wit the flow of salmon
to the coasts, and that they would be warned not to conduct
their operations in that region Wien the salmon were making
their way to the rivers for spawning.
It is not entirely clear from the roregoing extract as to whether
the Japanese Government would warn fishery interests to rerrain from
interfering with salmon in waters aujucent to the Alaska coast. It is
assumed that such is the case and that the Japanese Government will in-
struct and warn nationals of that country that there is to be no inter-
ference at any time or place with the runs of salmon ap roaching the
Alaska coast. Verification of this understanding will be appreciated.
It is respectfully requested that a furt er more detailed report be
secured fron the American Enbassy at Tokyo after the retuin of the Taihoku
Maru to that country this season. It is desired that such report convey
information particularly as to the (a) character and quantity of fishery
products prepared, (b) fishing rear and boats employed, (c) persons en-
gaged, (a) places and duration of operations, and (e) any other pertinent
matters deemed of importance. A statenent also is desired, if procurable,
as to plans for future operations.
This information is needed not only in regard to the Taihoku Maru but
also in respect to the Japanese trawler Kakusai Maru, which put into Dutch
Harbor, Alaska, on September 1, 1930, for fresh water while enroute from
the Bristol Bay region of Alaska to Tokyo. The vessel was detained by the
Coast Guard Cutter Haida, but was released September 2 when the ensuing
investigation disclosed that it had been fishing in waters of Bristol Bay

=== Page 26 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
outside a fifteen-mile line and had aboard three tons of salt cod, three
hundred pounds of crabs, and thirty halibut. This vessel was ll8 feet
in length, manned by a crew of twenty-five, and belonged to the International
Fisheries Company of Tokyo.
The master reported to the Boarding Officer from the Haida and the
Deputy Collector of Customs that the vessel was equioped with four trawls.
He said that this was an experimental vessel launched in July, and would
return next year with two others. He stated that the purpose this year
was to get samples of fish for the owners of the vessel. The Kakusai Maru
left Dutch Harbor on September 3 after taking water.
Information has been received that there is considerable talk in
Seattle to the effect that two Japanese steamers canned about 50,000 cases
of salmon off the Alaska coast this summer in addition to their pack of
canned crab meat. It is also said that quantities of salmon were frozen
and salted. It is suggesteu that the American Embassy at Tokyo make a
special and cautious inquiry as to the truth of the discussions at Seattle
that salmon were canned by Japanese interests off the coast of Alaska this
season.
Occasion is hereby taken to reiterate and emphasize what vas said in
this Department's letter of July 8, 1930, to the effect that any extension
or development of Japanese fishery interests in waters off the Alaska
coast which in any way menaces or t reatens the runs of salmon, will re-
sult in great damage to American industry. The Bristol Bay region of
Alaska produces more red salmon than any other body of water in the world,
with an average annual value of ap roxi ately ten million dollars. It is
urgently felt that every possible means must be taken by the Governnent
of the United States to protect this valuable fishery from foreign invasion.
Very truly yours,

€9.c
Depertment of State
October
My dear Mr. Secretary:
The receipt is acknowledged of your letter of the lst instant, file
FE 894.628/45, inclosing copy of a despatch from the American Embassy at
Tokyo, dated September 8, 1930, in which the Charge d'Affaires ad interim
expresses further views in regard to the activities of Japanese vessels
fishing in Bering Sea.
554.
As this Department has expressed in previous correspondence, it is
felt that the valuable salmon fisheries of western Alaska may be very se-
riously injured and American industry suffer great loss if there is any
C
extensive exploitation of this fishery by Japanese vessels operating in
心
international waters. If such vessels would stay on the banks twenty or
more miles offshore, as in the case of the Taihoku Maru in the summer of
1930, and confine their operations to the taking of crabs, there appears
to be no cause for apprehension in respect to the salmon fisheries.
There has never been any effort upon the part of American citizens
to engage in crab fishing in Bering Sea; therefore no logical reason ap-
pears to exist as to why we should object to the taking of crabs in those
waters by Japanese, as long as they do not interfere with the fisheries
conducted by our nationals. As a matter of fact, it seems entirely pos-
sible that a considerable part of the pack of crabs prepared by Japanese
vessels off the Alaskan coast may ultimately find its way to the United
States, as quite extensive imports of canned crab meat are made from Japan.
A difficult situation is presented if Japanese vessels undertake the
capture and utilization of salmon in international waters off the coast of
Alaska. Apparently the only possible remedy appears to be along the line
of a treaty whereby Japanese vessels would be restrained from engaging in
such operations. So far as the fisheries in general are concerned, it is
not clear that we have anything to offer the Japanese Government in return
for prohibitions which might be made effective under any treaty against
Japanese vessels operating off the coast of Alaska. American vessels do
not fish in Japanese waters nor is there any likelihood of their doing so.
It may be the course of wisdom to keep the entire situation under
close observation and await developments in the fishing season of l931.
While very likely Japanese vessels will appear off the Alaskan coast for

=== Page 31 === (confidence 0.96)
- 2 -
the purpose of packing crabs, it may be that, as has been presumed to be
the case in l930, there will be no interference with the runs of salmon.
The American Embassy at Tokyo may be able to develop further information
from time to time bearing upon the situation before the beginning of the
fishing season of 1931.
Very truly yours,
Actihg $ecretary of Commerce.

Mr. Neville and Mr. Miller, in company with
8
6
INDEX
Mr. Moser of the Bureau of Foreign and Domestic
Commerce, called by appointment at ten o'clock en
の
28/49
Mr. Radcliffe and Mr. O'Malley of the Bureau of
44.628
Fisheries. Mr. Neville and Mr. Miller explained that
they were calling to discuss certain questions which
had been raised by the Bureau of Fisheries in regard
to fishing by Japanese vessels in waters adjacent to
the Alaskan coast.
In letters which had been received by the State
119
Department, and which had been forwarded by the De-
partment to the Embassy at Tokyo, the Bureau of Fish-
eries had raised the question as to whether there was
any means of p venting or restraining the Japanese
from conducting  shing operations in that region.
The Embassy in replying to these letters had pointed
out that there was i. domestic Japanese legislation
whereby the Japanese Government could prevent Japan-
ese ships from carrying on these fishing operations;
that practioally the only means whereby any restraint
could be imposed would be the conclusion of a treaty.
After some discussion of the correspondence, Mr.
O'Malley

=== Page 36 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
O'Malley was asked if the Bureau of Fisheries was in a
position to make a clear statement of what measures were
considered necessary to protect American fisheries in
view of the rapid development of refrigeration and can-
nery methods on ships on the high seas. Mr. O'Malley
said he thought he could do this but was in doubt as
to what inducement the United States could offer the
Japanese in return for abstention on their part.
Mr. Neville said that all the information he had
indicated that the Japanese at the moment were in-
terested in orab fishing only; that he had been so in-
formed by the Japanese authorities but that he had also
been informed that it was possible that in the future
there would be Japanese ships sent to the waters of
the Bering Sea for other types of sea food. Mr. O'Malley
said that the crab fisheries were of no particular in-
terest to the United States at the moment because there
were no Americans engaged in crab canning to any extent.
Mr. Neville suggested that if anything were to be done
to protect our fisheries in Alaskan waters, the time
to do it was now before the Japanese had built up
any larger financial interest in fisheries in that
region.
Mr. Miller and Mr. Neville then said that perhaps
the Japanese would seek compensation in some alteration
of the fur-seal treaty. They added that they did not
711.948
know

=== Page 37 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
know what modification the Japanese might wish. Mr.
Neville was inclined to guess that the Japanese de-
sired a larger kill of fur seals because the Robben
Island herd was said to have reached large propor-
tions. Mr. Neville said further that the Japanese
olaim that the fur seals were consuming edible fish
probably meant that it was the Japanese herd. He
thought that the Japanese desired to reduce their
herd perhaps or stabilize it and that they would
like to retain the 15 per cent of the furs which
they now give to us. He and Mr. Miller were in-
olined to think that compensation in this form or
some analagous arrangement might meet the Japanese
wishes. Mr. O'Malley stated that in such case he
believed we would be justified in relinquishing our
claim to a share of the Japanese catch and might
even also somewhat increase the share of our catch
which we turn over to Japan.
Mr. Moser suggested that another "trading
point" might be a reduction of our import duty
on canned orab meat inasmuch as that industry was
of more importanoe to the Japanese than to us.
Mr. O'Malley said that he was anxious to pre-
serve the fur seals and the fisheries and to pre-
vont indisoriminate fishing in the open sea which
might interfere with the salmon runs in Alaskan
waters.

=== Page 38 === (confidence 0.89)
- 4 -
waters. He said that he was prepared to take the ques-
tion up with the Secretary of Commerce and to suggest
some action which might be taken by the State Depart-
ment through the Embassy at Tokyo.

DEPARTMENT OF COMMERCE
Index Bureau
Bureau ofFisheries
Washington
IAN
Dept. of State
January 17, 1931.
Subject: Japanese fishing vessels in waters off the
coast of Alaska.
MEMORANDUM TO THE SECRETARY OF COMMERCE:
During the past summer Japanese fishing vessels
visited Bristol Bay and Bering Sea about 2l miles off the
Alaskan coast. The largest vessel of 7,844 tons, 500
feet in length and manned by a crew of about 300 was en-
gaged in the canning of crabs. Accompanying this vessel
was a steam beam trawler and a number of power launches.
Another commercial trawler belonging to the International
Fisheries Company of Tokyo, was also in Bristol Bay on a
prospecting trip. The presence of this latter vessel was
unknown until she put into Dutoh Harbor on September 1,
for fresh water while enroute to Tokyo. A training ship
of the Imperial Fisheries Institute of Tokyo, a modern
steel vessel of about 2,O00 tons, also visited Dutch
Harbor in June, the personnel consisting of 30 in the
crew, 10 officers and instructors, and 32 students. The
ship was fully equipped for giving instructions in
fisheries work, including canning, et cetera.
The presence of Japanese vessels in these waters so
close to the American coast in proximity to areas passed
through by some of the schools of salmon enroute to their
spawning grounds in Alaska, raises questions which merit
most serious consideration by our Government. Under
existing international law it would appear practicable
for such vessels engaged in fishing for salmon there to
partially, if not wholly, nullify the efforts of our
Government to conserve this great resouroe.
Not long ago I had a very interesting discussion of
this entire subject with Mr. Edwin L. Neville, Counsellor
at our Embassy in Tokyo. It was the consensus of opinion
at

=== Page 55 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
this meeting that if the Department of Commerce were to
express an interest in the desirability of having the
State Department give consideration to this whole ques-
tion, Mr. Neville might be able to contribute, in time,
valuable information which might be used as a basis for
treaty negotiations with Japan to solve this difficult
situation in a manner agreeable to both parties.
HENRY O'MALLEY
Commissioner.

Washington, D. C.
My dear Mr. Secretary:
894.628/
The receipt is acknowledged of your letters of January 29 and
January 30, 1931, inclosing communications in regard to the subject of
Japanese vessels fishing in waters off the coast of Alaska.
In regard to your request particularly for the views of this De-
partment as to "the limitations desired to have imposed on fishing
trawlers and the conditions under which it would be considered desir-
able for Japanese trawlers and crab fishers to operate", I desire to
5
submit the following:
3
1. The Department views with disfavor any movement having
for its objective the lending of encouragement to foreign in-
terests to fish off the coast of Alaska, particularly for the
reason that if foreign interests were to engage in fishing for
such species as salmon, such action might result in entirely
nullifying the efforts of this Department to properly conserve
the supply of salmon which breed only in fresh water.
2. It is the Department's belief that any efforts to ob-
FEB 11 1931
tain concessions such as those mentioned in the despatch from
FIT.ED
Ambassador Forbes, along the line of procuring empty cans and
the delivery of the pack in Alaska, would be vigorously opposed
by American fishery interests.
3. As a possible concession to Japanese industry, the De-
partment would look with favor upon an effort to reduce the
duty on canned crab meat imported into this country.
4. Should it become necessary this Department would not
oppose a concession to the Japanese Government whereby we would
relinquish the share of fur-seal skins which the Government of
the United States now receives from the Japanese fur-seal herd
on Robben Island, as stipulated in the fur-seal convention of
711.417
1911.
5. The Department has issued regulations prohibiting the
use of any trawl for the taking of fish in Alaska. This prohibi-
tory action has been necessary in order to avoid a type of fishing
operations considered to be destructive in character.

=== Page 57 === (confidence 0.97)
-2 -
Were it possible, this Department would like very much to see the
entire withdrawal of all Japanese fishing vessels from all high sea waters
off the Alaskan coast, particularly in the eastern half of Bering Sea.
Very sincerely,

The Department of Commerce notes with great satisfaction that the
decision has been reached not to send any Japanese floating cannery to
Alaskan waters this year. It is observed that both crab and trawling
operations near Alaska last year resulted in loss and that, wnile trawlers
are licensed to continue such operations, their owners are not in finan-
cial position to proceed to Alaska this year. The Departnent of Comerce
hopes that there will be no resumption at any time of these operations off
the Alaskan coast.
The Department of Commerce is greatly interested in the progress of
the negotiations which are being conducted with Japanese authorities and
fishery interests by the American Embassy at Tokyo. This Department
wishes to express its appreciation of the very competent manner in which

February 27, 1931.
This despatoh from Tokyo (No. 134),
February 9, 1931, confirms the Embassy's
telegram of February 9 relating to Japa-
nese fishing aotivities in Alaskan waters.
It summarizes the results of an informal
meeting with various Japanese officials
and representatives of the fishing inter-
ests.The discussion dealt largely with
the equipment and methods used by the
Japanese fishermen in Alaskan waters and
brings out the fact that their operations
had not been profitable owing to the fact
that the cost of each case of crab packed
last summer in Alaska was yen 42 as
against yen 35 of the Kamchatka pack.
The President of the Crab Canners'
Guild stated that no floating cannery nor
trawler would be sent to Alaskan waters
this summer by that Guild and it was
thought that the owners of other trawlers
licensed to operate in Alaskan waters
would not be able to operate on account
of financial difficulties. Itwas sug-
gested that "nothing offioial be done
until there were tangible signs of an
expansion of Japanese fishing interests
in the direction of Alaska"; the Japanese
officials would be glad to study any con-
ditions which the American authorities
would like to have applied to Japanese
trawlers and floating canneries.

Confirming my telegram No.18, February 9, 11 A.M., I
have the honor to report that I had an informal meettng on
Saturday, the 7th instant, with Baron Iwakura, President
of the Crab Canners' Guild, Mr. Taketomi, Chief of the
Bureau of Commercial Affairs in the Foreign Office, and
Mr. Nagase, Chief of the Bureau of Marine Produots in the
Department of Agriculture and Forestry, to disouss Japanese
fishing operations near Alaska.
Mr. Nagase gave an outline of the extent of the
operations

=== Page 79 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
operations last year by the floating cannery TAIHOKU MARU
and various trawlers, which I shall not repeat as it was
substantially the agcount presented in the Embassy's
5
890.628/5
despatohes No.115, of January 23, 1931 and No. 128
January 27, 1931. He then proceeded to explain the type
of net used by the trawlers. This net is said to have
a width of about 15o feet, but the arc between the two
ends of the net is about loo feet when dragged at the most
effeotive speed of about three knots. The depth is about
30 feet. The lower edge of the net is dragged along the sea
butgvosdling salonon
aee ho. 16
bottom in order to catoh halibut, sole, cod, and other types
Febg20,1911
of fish whioh remain near the surface. Mr. Nagase stated
that the trawling speed is so low that salmon and other types
of fish which move with high velocity are able to avoid
the nets with ease. He confirmed my understanding of the
requirement in the Japanese regulations, that the nets
used for catching orab should have a mesh of at least
l8" in diameter. He then showed me a map of Bristol Bay
on whioh were marked the points where Japanese trawlers
and floating cannery had operated last year, and ex-
plained that, according to the reports presented to his
Bureau, these vessels had not approached the Alaskan coast
to a distance of less than 13 nautical miles, except when
they were entering an American harbor to procure supplies.
The reports further indicated, he added, that these vessels
had caught no salmon in commercial quantities.
I then repeated in outline the reports transmitted
to the Embassy with the various instructions from the
Department. I endeavored to make it olear that there was
no

=== Page 80 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
no complaint of the Japanese having acted illegally, and
said that the only reason for complaint thus far had been
the fact that the presence of the floating cannery near the
mouth of a river had interfered with the salmon run. I
repeated the substanoe of my two conversations with Baron
Iwakura, and expressed the opinion, for the benefit of Mr.
Nagase and Mr. Taketomi, that it would seem wise to regulate
the activities of the Japanese trawlers as calculated to
facilitate an arrangement between Japanese crab canners and
American interests for the di stribution of the pack in the
United States.
Mr. Taketomi asked me to explain why I should suggest
that the crab canners should enjoy more liberal privileges
than the trawlers. Baron Iwakura interposed and said
that the explanation lies in the fact that the high wages
prevailing in the United States would make it impossible for
American interests to engage in the crab industry as it
depended almost entirely upon manual labor. I added that
this explanation presumably accounted for a disposition
in the United States not to objeot to the presence of
floating canneries so long as they did not interfere with
the fishing. Mr. Taketomi then asked the nature of the
conditions and restrictions which it was proposed to apply
to the trawlers. I answered that I did not know, but that
in any event it would not seem possible for the Japanese
orab interests to make any satisfactory arrangements with
American interests until the trawlers were subjeot to some
regulation; that I had understood that the trawling operations
last year had been highly unprofitable, and that so long
as there is some promise of the crab canners deriving
advantages in this quarter, it seemed advantageous for the
Japanese to make some concession in respeot of the trawlers.

=== Page 81 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
Mr. Nagase said that there were a number of
trawlers which are licensed to operate in Alaskan waters,
and that, entirely apart from the possibility of these
trawlers going to Alaska this year, he did not believe
it would be possible to cancel the licenses without
good reason. Mr. Taketomi added that he could not see
how the Japanese Government could restrict the operations
of Japanese fishers unless some compensatory privileges
aocrued to the Japanese.
After considerable discussion which consisted largely
of a repetition of the arguments above outlined, Baron
Iwakura observed that the matter was obviously a com-
plioated one, and not capable of yielding an arrangement
without further study. He said that there had been a
meeting of the directors of his Guild, and that he had
suggested the advisability of suspending operations in
Alaskan waters this year, in view of the apprehension
which apparently exi:ted in the United States with respeot
to the Japanese fish  s. According to Baron Iwakura,
the cost of each case of crab packed last summer in
Alaska was Yen 42., as against Yen 35. for the Kamohatka
pack. He said that this difference made it impossible
to do anything with the Alaska orab unless some satis-
factory arrangement could be effected along the lines
which he had outlined to me. Furthermore, the Guild
had decided to reduce the total pack this year by
2o0,o00 cases in order to meet Soviet competition and
to be prepared against the quantities which the Soviets
would presumably dump in the United States and England.
In view of these ciroumstances, the directors had au-
thorized Baron Iwakura, in his judgment and in the light
of the conferenoe which was then being held, to assure
me

=== Page 82 === (confidence 0.98)
-5-
me that no floating cannery nor trawler would be sent to
Alaskan waters this summer by that Guild.
Mr. Nagase stated that the owners of the trawlers
licensed to operate in Alaskan waters were in a very
unfavorable financial situation due to the fact that the
British interests which had hitherto been financing them,
suoh as Sale and Company of London, had withdrawn their
support; that the trawlers had lost considerable sums of
money last year, and that he was certain that none would
go to Alaska this year.
I then said that, in view of these last statements,
there seemed to be no urgent need of coming to an agree-
ment.
. Both Mr. Taketomi and Mr. Nagase assured me that
the Amerioan fishery authorities would have no reason for
alarm this year, and that, if by any chance a Japanese vessel
is to be sent to Alaska this year, they would issue strict
orders to do nothing to whioh the American authorities
could objeot. They suggested that nothing official be
done until there were tangible signs of an expansion of
Japanese fishing interests in the direction of Alaska,
and that, if I could disclose to them the conditions
whioh the American authorities would like to have applied
to Japanese trawlers and floating canneries, they would be
glad to give the matter further study.
Respectfully yours,
Eugue Noomay

Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the mbassy's
despatoh No. 134 of February 9, 1931, and to previous
correspondence in regard to the operations of Japanese
fishing vessels in Alaskan waters. It appears that
there will be little or no fishing there by the Japanese
this year. I am convinced, however, that this is only
a temporary condition, due to special oiroumstances.
In succeeding

=== Page 88 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
In succeeding years it seems probable that Japanese
will resort to the Alaskan crab fields if there promises
to be any profit in it.
It would seem advisable to work out, in the mean-
time, a plan for the proteotion of our fishing resouroes
whioh would permit reasonable exploitation of deep sea
fishing in the Alaska waters of Bering Sea. It might
be advisable to discuss such plan with the Japanese au-
thorities who are, I am sure, deeply concerned with
fish conservation as a permanent souroe of food.
I am inolined to the belief, from what I have
recently heard on the subject, that the Japanese seal
herd on Robben Island has reaohed the limit of profit-
able expansion. The seals, it is stated, are becoming
a menaoe to the fishing industry ofr the Japanese coast.
The Japanese would like, I think, to reduce it, or at
least stabilize it. They do not desire a return to
pelagic sealing, but they probably desire to control
their rur seals on their own terms without obligation
to deliver part of the skins or accounting to foreign
governments. If our authorities could work out a plan
for a comprehensive handling or fisheries and fur seals
in Bering Sea on a humane and scientific basis, it seems
likely that the Japanese would be willing to consider
it. As I have remarked previously it would be better
to do this before a vested business interest intervenes
to make it difrieult and expensive.
Respeetfully yours,
Eduin t-Truilk
Kawin L. Neville
Chargé d'Arfaires ad interim.

Dear Mr. Neville:
I am pleased to acknowledge the receipt of your etter of the 24th
ultimo. I had anticipated a further talk with you regarding fishery
work 1n Japan before your deperture for that country. It is realized,
however, that you had many matters requiring attention before you left
here.
I have been interested in the communications which have been re-
ceived from time to time from the State Department as to the action
being taken by the Embassy at Tokyo with regard to Japanese activities
in the fishery off the coast of Alaske. I am of the opinion that the
negotiations have been handled in most excellent fashion. My mind has
been much relieved by reason of the probable decision of Japanese in-
terests not to resume fishing operations in Bering See off the laskan
coast 1n the season of l93l. I am glad to note your opinion that the
Japanese authorities are pronounced conservationists. This fact, to-
gether with the improbability of financial success of operations off
the Alaskan coast,'may'mean that such activities are not likely to be
undertaken.
Of course, I realize that we must be on the alert so far as the
situation is concerned and must be prepared to meet any development
along this line which would threaten the salmon fisheries of Alaska.
As yet I have not seen your latest despatch, in which you say men-
tion is made of the subject of fur seals. I can appreciate that as
Robben Island 1s of very limited extent the Japanese herd at that place
has now grown as large as available space will accommodate. I do not
quite see how the seals are becoming a decided nuisance.
If there are more seals than Robben Ialand can take care of, it
seems to me that the Jepanese authcrities can resort to the very simple
expedient of increasing the number of seals killed each year. Years ago

=== Page 96 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
fur seals were known to have breeding grounds on some of the Kurile
Islands. There is a possibility that if there is a lack of breeding
ground on Robben Island some of the fur seals might reestablish them-
selves in the Kurile Islands. I gather, however, that there is oppo-
sition in certain Japanese cireles to any extensive development of that
nation's fur-aeal herd because of the alleged more or less extensive feeding
of these animals upon fish. Perhaps, though, this may not have important
bearing upon the matter.
I am interested in your suggestion that it would be advisable for
me to come to Japan or send some one to go over the question of Pacifio
fisheries with the Japanese. This will be kept in mind with a view to
serious consideration should further action along this line become ad-
visable. I realize that, as you say, further useful information might
thus be developed in respect to our fishery interests. I am glad to
note that you think the Japanese will be willin to cooperate with a
vlew to conserving the fisheries of the high seas.
Very truly yours,
Henry  Maney
Commissioner.

DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
Conversation:
May 20, 1931.
Mr. Bowers, of the Bureau of Fisheries;
28
Mr. Dooman and Mr. Miller.
MB
Sub ject:
Japanese fishing act ivities in Alaskan
π
894
6
waters.
Taking advantage of the presence of Mr. Dooman at the
894.628/6
Department, Mr. Bowers of the Bureau of Fisheries was
asked to come to the Department to talk over the situation
in relation to Japanese fishing activities in Alaskan waters.
Mr. Bowers began by summarizing the situation as he
had reported it to Mr. O'Malley following telephonio con-
6
versations with Mr. Miller on April 29, which in turn were
based on the recent reports from the Embassy at Tokyo re-
newing the suggestion that Mr. O'Malley come to Japan to
discuss the question with the Japanese authorities.
Mr. Bowers stated that Mr. O'Malley's conolusion
after hearing these reports was that he deemed it better
to have the Japanese authorities come to Washington than
JUN 1TTU 1931
for him to proceed to Tokyo for any discussion of the sub-
FILA
jeot.
Mr. Dooman then reviewed the course of the discus-
sions in Tokyo which he intimated had been undertaken in
February last as a result of communications received
through the Department from the Department of Commerce
during

=== Page 115 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
during the preceding six months relating to Japanese fish-
ing activit ies in Alaskan waters. and intimating that the
preservation of our important salmon industry in those
waters appeared to call for some understanding on the sub-
jeot with the Japanese authorities and interests.
Mr. Dooman stated that the Japanese authorities had
met the Embassy in a friendly and cooperative spirit.
They had intimated, however, that they could not with pro-
priety restrain Japanese vessels from lawful fishing on
the high seas but that strict instruotions had been given
to the Japanese vessels packing crabs off the Alaskan
coast to in no wise engage in commercial salmon fishing.
Mr. Dooman stated that crab fishing was an important
industry to the Japanese, in which they were especially
fitted to engage because of the manual dexterity of the
hands employed in the packing of crabs; and that, although
they were not at the present time actively interested in
any other form of fishing in the Alaskan waters, it must
be borne in mind that the Japanese nation largely depended
upon fishing for their food supply and that in proportion
as they were driven out of the Russian fisheries the
pressure to seek their food supply in other waters would
be inoreased, and that we could not expect that they would
overlook the Alaskan fisheries as a source of such supply.
He

=== Page 116 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
He stated that the Embassy felt that if an under-
standing on this subject were to be reaghed with the
Japanese authorities now is the time to do it - if indeed
it were not already too late. He therefore thought it
important that if Mr. O'Malley were not able himself to
go to Japan some other member of the Bureau of Fisheries,
who had a technical knowledge of the subjeot, should go
to Japan to talk the matter over with the Japanese
authorities, adding that naturally such conversations
would not be for the purpose of making any commitments on
the subject but of exploring the situation and of obtain-
ing information which might clear the way for an under-
standing.
He further stated that, inasmuch as the crab industry
was of importance to Japan, provided they could conduct
it on an economically profitable basis, and was not of
any especial importance to us, we might well assume a
friendly attitude toward their efforts to sell their
produts in the United States, which was their great market.
He referred to the fact that Mr. Frank Booth, representing
the combined selling agencies of the various firms
Japane se
interested in the crab industry, was now in the United
States discussing the matter with American packing
interests; and that it might be well to ascertain what
suooess

=== Page 117 === (confidence 0.93)
- 4 -
sucoess he was having and to assist him in such ways as
might be consistent.
Mr. Bowers stated that he would report the results
of the conference to Mr. O'Malley when the latter returned
to Washington, and Mr. Dooman intimated that he would be
willing to return to Washington for further conferences
if it should be deemed desirable.

The present attitude of the Bureau of Fisheries
COPDS
ely
toward the repeated suggestion of the Tokyo Embassy that
8
Commissioner O'Malley come to Japan to talk over with the
MB
Japanese authorities the question of Japanese fishing
aotivities in Alaskan waters appears to be that we have
8
nothing in particular to worry about at present; and that,
894.628/70
as the Japanese appear to want something, it would be
better if they came to us than that we go to them.
In a recent conversation with Mr. Bowers, of the
Bureau of Fisheries, to which the memorandum hereunder re-
lates, it was pointed out by Mr. Dooman that the disous-
sions in Tokyo relating to this subject were begun as a
result of a series of communications from the Department
of Commerce, through the Department of State, intimating
that the Japanese fishing activities were becoming a men-
ace to our salmon industry and that something should be
JUN 10 1931
FILED
done about it.
It was also pointed out that fish were an important
item in the Japanese food supply; that in proportion as
the Japanese were driven out of the fishing in Soviet
Russian waters they would be forced to seek other sources
of

=== Page 120 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
of supply; that, while the Japanese authorities were at
present disposed to discourage any fishing by Japanese in
Alaskan waters, other than for crabs, they had intimated
that they could not consistently restrain their nationals
from lawful fishing on the high seas; and that if anything
in the way of an understanding were necessary to conserve
our salmon industry now was the time to attempt to reach
such an understand ing - if indeed it were not already too
late.
It was therefore suggested (1) that if Mr. O'Malley
were not himself able to go to Tokyo some other qualified
representative of the Bureau of Fisheries be sent to dis-
ouss the matter with the Japanese, together with the
Embassy, with a view to ascertaining what the situation
actually was, from a practical standpoint, and what
measures were necessary and feasible; (2) that, as an off-
set to the restriction of their fishing activities in
other lines, all possible encouragement should be given to
the Japanese crab industry in preparing and marketing
their products at a profit; and (3) that, as a step to
that end, the Bureau of Fisheries endeavor to get in touch,
through its agencies on the Pacific coast, with the repre-
sentative (Mr. Booth) of the combined Japanese crab sel-
ling agencies, who was reported to be now in the United
States

=== Page 121 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
States,to disouss this matter with Amerioan packing
interests.
It is not now clear whether the Bureau of Fisheries
does or does not consider that our salmon industry is at
present, or may be in the future, menaced by the Japanese
fishing activities; nor what measures, if any, they con-
sider necessary and practicable to meet such a menace,
should it exist; nor what, if anything, might be offered
to the Japanese by way of compensation in return for the
voluntary restriction of their right to fish outside of
territorial waters.
The solution of these questions seems to call for
the combined special and technical knowledge and experi-
ence of the officials of the Bureau of Fisheries and the
special knowledge and experience of the officers of the
Embassy at Tokyo, who are familiar with the Japanese
officials and interests. The step suggested by Mr. Dooman
to send a qualified representative of the Bureau to Tokyo
to join with the Embassy in discussing the question with
the Japanese appears to be the only practicable way (other
than by rendering such encouragement to the Japanese crab
industry as may be possible) in which a satisfactory solu-
tion may be found, if such is desired.
Mr. Dooman has intimated his willingness to return to
Washington

=== Page 122 === (confidence 0.95)
-4 -
Washington toward the middle of June if requested to do so;
and it is regommended that he be asked to return to the De-
partment, on assignment, for a period of a wek or ten days
in order to bring the matter to the point of a definite de-
cision by the Bureau of Fisheries, which is primarily con-
oerned.

Mr. O'Malley, Mr. Radoliffe and Mr. Bowers,
of the Bureau of Fisheries;
Mr. Miller and Mr. Dooman.
894.628/71
8
Sub je ot :
Japanese fishing activities in Alaskan
waters.
Mr. Miller and Mr. Dooman called yesterday morning by
appointment on Mr. O'Malley, Commissioner of Fisheries,
Mr. Radcliffe, Deputy Commissioner of Fisheries, and
Mr. Bowers, Chief of the Alaskan Division, also being
present. Mr. Dooman related at some length the disoussions
whioh had taken place by the officers of the Embassy at
191T193
Tokyo and the Japanese offioials and representatives of
Japanese organizations interested in the fisheries re-
sources of Alaskan waters. He tried to make it olear that
these discussions had been initiated upon instructions from
the Department issuing out of wishes of the Department of
Commerce to procure adequate protection of the Alaskan
salmon industry. He said that the Embassy, whichwas unable
to indioate to the Japanese what measures of proteotion are
desired, had given the Japanese to understand that we
wished to come to some understanding by which the Japanese
fishing

=== Page 125 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
fishing interests might be restrained from interfering
with the fishing in Alaskan waters; that the Japanese had
shown a willingness to join in such an understanding but
that they were unable to restriot the lawful activities
of their nationals without some compensation. He said
that the Japanese Government had informally given its
assurances that it would instruct all Japanese fishing
vessels approaching Alaska not to interfere in any way
with the salmon. In this conneotion he pointed out that
the initiative had been taken by us and that, while the
Japanese were apparently cooperating on the proteotion of
the salmon industry, it would be unreasonable to expect them
to maintain their present attitude indefinitely without
further evidence of our appreciation of their attitude.
Mr. O'Malley appeared to be thoroughly alive to the
possibilities of serious injury being done to the Alaskan
salmon industry by the Japanese and he expressed entire
sympathy with the view of Mr. Neville that an understanding
should be reaohed before the Japanese had made a substantial
investment in the Alaskan fisheries. He said, however, that
he was reluctant to follow Mr. Neville's suggestion of going
out to Japan and disoussing the question with the Japanese.
He was unable to see what compensation could be offered the
Japanese in return for an undertaking on their part not to
engage

=== Page 126 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
engage in fishing for salmon and other forms of edible fish
unless it were to give Japan more favorable terms than
those granted under the present fur seals treaty. He re-
marked, nevertheless, that the Alaskan salmon industry is
of such great importance that he would be prepared to make
substantial concessions in respect of the killing of seals.
Mr. Miller remarked that, as the questions that had
arisen concerned our fishing industries, it seemed to be
quite in order for us to take the initiative in case those
interests appeared to be menaced. As to the advantages of
a visit to Japan by Mr. O'Malley or one of his representa-
tives to disouss this question, three things might be said:
first, that it would afford an opportunity both to give
and to get information which would lead to a better under-
standing of the situation by both sides; second, that such
an exohange of information would enable us to deal with
the matter in a spirit of mutual understanding and confi-
denoe, rather than of suspicion and distrust; and that,
third, in so dealing with the questions that might arise,
there was a good prospect that some arrangement might be
arrived at for the conservation of our salmon resources,
in the conservation of which the Japanese as well as our-
selves might reasonably be expected to be interested.
Mr. Radoliffe expressed the opinion that the Japanese
might

=== Page 127 === (confidence 0.97)
-4 -
might consider any effort made on our part to obtain an
understanding as a sign of weakness in our position. He
believed that the situation as it exists today might be
left alone until further developments take place. However,
Mr. O'Malley, as well as Mr. Miller and Mr. Dooman, be-
lieved that the situation with which the Japanese were
faced in Siberian waters, together with the evident
sincerity of the Japanese in their expressed desire for
some form of cooperation, made it desirable for us to
explore the possibilities of reaching an understanding
without undue delay. After some further discussion
Mr. Radoliffe appeared to concur in the latter point of
view.
Mr. O'Malley said that he was prooeeding shortly to
Alaska in company with members of the Senate Committee on
Wild Life Resouroes and that he thought he would have a
very favorable opportunity to conv lce the Committee.
whose support would of course be ne ssary in any measure.
that might be taken hereafter, of th: serious possibilities
inherent in the present situation. He *aid that after his
return from Alaska in the autumn he would be prepared, if
ciroumstances were favorable, to proceed to Japan with the
purpose of disoussing the fisheries with the Japanese.

The report stated also that Mr. Hendrickson of the War Depart-

alongside the Nagato Maru and that men were engaged in transferring

from the lighthouse at Cape Sarichef on the west end of Unimak Island.

=== Page 144 === (confidence 0.99)
6GO P Y
ONFIDENTIAL
TREASURY DEPARTMENT
UNITED STATES COAST GUARD
BERING SEA PATROL FORCE
1 October, 1931.
From:
Commander, Bering Sea Patrol Force.
To :
Commandant.
Subject:
Japanese activities along the Aleutian Islands.
During the current season an andeavor has been made to ob-
tain information relative to the activities of the Inperial Japanese
Fisheries Steamer HAkUHo LARt along the Aleutian Islands. This vessel
has not been seen nor heard from durin the present year. Also, infcrma-
tion was obtained relative to the activities of the Japanese who visited
the Islands in connection with proposed "Good-Will" flight across the
Pacific. The information obtained is put down in the forn of notes as
obtained from the persons interviewed.
2.
Mr. Fred Schroeder, trader and fur rancher of Attu Island,
states that the HAkUHO  ARU came into Attu Harbor from the westward on
June 29, 1930 and 1eft the following day. They came in for water and
said they were coming back at a certain time. The officers and crew of
the ship did not as: permissio to land and seemed to do about as they
pleased. They wanted to fish but were refused permission, Mr. Schroeder
telling them that there were very few fish and these were needef for
the natives. In spite of this refusal, they went to the n tive gill
nets and helped themselves to the fish and made no offer to pay for the
fish taken. Mr. Schroeder states that this ship always left about a
day or so before a Coast Guard Cutter arrived. The HAkUHO :ARU visited
▲ttu twice in 1929, arriving: ther first on June 24 of that year. They
sent boats ashore for vater and landed again July 13. The Attu natives
said that at the first landing on June 24 the bottoms of the boats were
in good condition, the natives particularly noted this fact when the
Japanese were cleaning out their boats for boating off water. On the
second arrival, however, the natives noticed that the boat bottoms were
all chewed up, showing evidence of landing on beaches. The native and
Mr. Schroeder thought the Japanese were taing live foxes on the various
Fox islands as that was just the right time of year to catch foxes, and
it is known that Japanese are startin blue fox farns somewhere in
Northern Japan. On West Semichi, an island near Attu, the natives found
a target made of driftwood during the winter of 1929 and 1930, and
alongside the target were several used cartridge cases from pistols. They
also found a Japanese tea cup. Mr. Schroeder reports that in the winters
following the summers that the Japanese visited the Aleutians, the catches
of foxes on the different Islands were distressingly small. In view of
this fact, white men were brought up from San Francisco as watchmen, but
they stayed only one year. This vessel, the HAYUHO :ARU visited along
-1-

=== Page 145 === (confidence 0.99)
the Islands during 1929 and 1930 and both natives and whites were very
much distrubed about the Japanese activities along the Aleutians. Attu
is a native community Fox island, but as stated before, lir. Schroeder
says the Japanese Government vessel never requested permission to land.
3. During the present season, the Japanese trawler KOKUSAI MARU
No. 100 visited Attu, Atka and Unalaska, in connection with the proposed
"Good-Will" flight across the North Pacific and Mr. Schroeder reports
the trawler as reaching Attu on May 2. They showed great familiarity
with the water around Attu and came in the narrow entrance to Attu harbor,
which has a depth of only fourteen feet, on a dark night at good speed.
This trawler landed three Japanese, two of whom said they were Staff
correspondents for the Japanese newspaper HOCHI SHIBUN of Torio. The
names of the correspondents are: E. Sugimoto and S. Kondo. The third
Japanese landed at Attu, named Imarnura, said he was the cameraman of
the party. He had a regular camera and a movie camera. The cameraman
took pictures, so many that he finally ran out of films, both still and
movie. These Japanese conducted theaselves in a very proper manner and
did nothing all summer but wait for the return of the trawler. They were
very liberal with money at Attu and seemed to have plenty.
4. The foregoing information was furnished by  r. Fred Schroeder
who visits Attu each year and remains about six months. r. Schroeder's
address is: 345 - 3lst Ayemue, San Francisco, California.
5. Captain A. M. Nelson of the gas screw EUNICE, belonging to the
Alaska Commerttal Company of Unalaska, heard various reports about the
Japanese during his cruises between Unalaska and Attu, at which times
he touched at the various Islands. The cook on the EUICE, a native of
Attu, told Captain Nelson that in 1929 when the Japanese Government boat
HAKUHO MARU was in Attu, he, the cook, told the Japanese that the Coast
Guard cutter would be in the next day. The Japanese aslted him how he
know, and he said the news had come by radio. Then, he said, the Japanese
ship left the following day and before the cutter arrive . This native
cook could talk some Japanese and so was able to converse with them. The
Japanese asked the cook about what season the fish ran out along the
Islands, the locations of the crmeks where the fish would be running, the
kinds of fish, etc. Also, they asked how the foxes were trapped along
the Island and the season for trapping, and where the best trapping was
to be had along the Islands.
6. Captain Nelson stated in regard to the movements of the
trawler KOKUSAI MARU during the present year, in connection with the
proposed "Good-Will" flight, that this vessel proceeded to Atka from Attu
and put three men ashore on the latter island, the party consisting of
one correspondent, one botanist and one cameraman. The botanist was busy
all the time collecting specimens while at Atka, but he went on to Dutch
Harbor later on and joined the trawler at Unalaska. During the first night
of the trawler's visit to Atka, May 7, all of the native men in the village
were drunk on liquor obtained from the crew of the Japanese vessel. The
correspondent got free with the native women, especially the young girls,
and a seventeen year old girl named Amastasia Sokolnikoff is reported
-2

=== Page 146 === (confidence 0.99)
pregnant by the cameraman. The cameraman at Atka took pictures from
all angles and from the mountains on either side of the bay.
7. Mr. E. K. Pedler, Agent in Charge of the Alasla Comaercial
Company's station, Unalaska, Alaska, reports that the Japanese trawler
KOKUSAI MARU No. 100 arrived Unalaska May 9, 1931, after having placed
parties on Attu and Atka. Four men from the trawler came ashore and took
up quarters at the Alaska Comnercial Company's house at Unalaska; H. Kimura,
T. Tanaka, Araio Toichiro and I. Aoki. H. Kimura is correspondent for
the HOCHI SHIMBUN, daily paper of Tollo. T. Tanaka represented himself
to be an aeronautical expert. Tanaka stated that he had over 3, Oo0 hours
in the air and was a Captain in the Japanese Navy, having been detached
from the Bureau of Comminications of the Imperial Japanese Navy for the
purpose of observing the "Good-Will" flight. Araio Toichiro said he was
a scientist and was connected with Japanese Bureau of Fisheries. Toichiro
was particularly interested in the activity of all vessels at Unalaska and
spent his time where here in hunting for fish scales which he claimed to
be able to read. He was also very much interested in nets, size of the web,
and other fishing gear. He also had considerable curiosity in regard to
the halibut boats, the amount of fish they had aboard and their gear. The
fourth member, I Aoki, as a professional cameraman of the ShoCHIKU Moving
Picture Corporation. This man came well prepared with supplies of films
for both the still camera and moving picture camera and took pictures dur-
ing his entire stay, of all activities. The Unalaska dock was under con-
struction at that time, and this, as well as the arrival or departure
of every vessel, Coast Guard, Navy, or merchant was photographed. He also
climbed the high mountains in the Unalas a locality, to take panoramic
views. The Captain of the vessel, who did not stay ashore, was H. Honda.
This man had the rank of lieutenant conmander in the Japanese Naval Reserve.
The chief officer was on detaded duty from one of the large mercantile lines
of Japan, and they also had aboard the vessel two radiomen, who stood con-
timual watches. These radiomen were of a higher type than is ordinarily
found on a trawler.
8. The KOKUSAI lMARU, Mr. Pedler stated, stayed only a few days at
the Dutch Harbor dock when she first arrived. Afterwards, she moved into
the bay and anchored a few hundred yards off shore, in front of the town
of Unalaska. Shore liberty was given to the crew of the bot, and they
spend considerable time ashore. The habits of the crew were objectionable
to the people of the town, due principally to the fact that the Japanese
custom of urinating wherever they stood was kept up. A protest was made
about this, and the Company permitted the KOYUSAI  ARU to tie up at the
Dutch Harbor dock, which is a more isolated place, and the men stayed aboard
the vessel for longer times. The crew had free access to the village at
all times, spent money freely here and made many friends anong the native
Aleutians.
9. The fish expert, Mr. Toichiro, after a comparatively short stay,
left Unalaska under orders from his home office, bound for Japan on the
Steamer STARR to Seward, and thence to Seattle. From statements made just
before his departure, he said that he did not think that the flight would
-3

=== Page 147 === (confidence 0.99)
take place, and that as he had gathered all the information we could
furjish about the fishing industry, he was pleased to be ordered home.
T. Tanaka, who was somewhat of a pessimist in regard to the flight and
thought it never would take place on account of the small size of the fly-
ing ship, left on June 29 for Seattle and thence home. Tanala had re-
ceived instructions from the Imperial office to report back to Japan as
quickly as possible. Kimura and Aoki left on the KOkUSAI LiARU when she
left here, bound for Japan via Atka, Attu and Petropavlosk on August 3rd.
The activity of the Japanese representatives in inquiring into the fish-
ing industry here was very noticeable. In fact, at a dinner given in the
Company's house, Captain Honda made a statement that the fish did not have
any flag, and speaking on behalf of his Company, the International Fish-
ing Corporation of Japan, stated he thought that some arrangement should
be made for the conservation of this food supply.
11. The Japanese were all well supplied with American money, some
of which they had brought with them from Japan, and during their stay in
Unalaska, several deposits were made by the Hewspaper to the credit of
H. Kimura. The Japanese were particularly anxious to acquire American
footwear, but did not purchase any woolen clothing. We had no trouble with
them whatsoever in regard to breaking our laws, and outside of their
personal habits, which I mentioned before, their visit could not be talten
exception to.
12. The Japanese crab-canning ship, MAGATO MARU FUCHU, arrived
Bristol Bay in the spring of the present year and left during the month
of August. This ship repeated her operations of last year and renained in
practically the same locality.
13. The Coast Guard cutter CHELAlr visited At a and Attu later on
in the summer and interviewed the natives relative to the Japanese activ-
ities this year. It was ascertained that at Attu the three Japanese took
pictures and gathered rock specimens, but tool no soundings so far as was
known. Last year the Japanese sounded out Holtz Bay, according to the
native chief. At Atka the three Japanese stayed at the chief's house.
There were two scientists (one botanist and aoparently one marine biolog-
ical) and a reporter. They were said to have taren a reat many pictures,
many of them distant views. They were active in their variois pursuits
during their stey, and the marine man may have taren soundings, although
nothing definite is kmown. Both scientists were graduates of Tokdo Uni-
versity. This information was obtained from Mr. Brown, storeleeper of
Atka.
(Signed) L. C. COVELL.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

JAN 22 1932
DIVISION OF
894.628
Dear Bill,
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
When I got here last year it seemed to me that
the only real thing pene ing between our Govern-
ments was the matter of settlin: the Alaska fish-
eries question. It is ver important to do this
F/LS
while there is no important controversy pending
and while times are dull and the fisheries not
active. The minute they all begin making money
S
and operating off the coast of Alaska then the
question gets vital, large investments are apt to
be involved which require pro ection, or proposed
investments headed off. Now is the strategic time
to settle the thing.
894.628/80
Referring to the Department's telegran 92,
I understood that Commissioner o'Malley was com-
ing out about this time; but accoraing to the
h9
Department's telegram 4,79received today, in
reply to #287, this trip is indefinitely post-
poned on the ground of "economy". It is mighty
poor economy to fail to settle something wich
may involve many millions of dollars and good
relations between Japan and the United states,
just to save the cost of a trip of one agent out
here who is on the pay-roll already. Cannot you
stir this thing up and get action?
Of course the Manchurian situation has developed
since that time and everybody's attention has been
so focussed on it that there doesn't seen to have
been room to think of anything else. But I believe
in watching both the little and big things, and have
PUHD
1932

=== Page 155 === (confidence 1.00)
-2-
seen too many people, while looking at the big
thing afar off, trip on some little thing which
they might have avoided by lifting the foot and
looking the right way, and get a nasty fall. I
have done it myself, and do not doubt the rest
of us have. So I do not want to let the fisheries
thing go because Manchuria happens to be the bigger
issue at present.
Cordially yours,
Ambassador.
Hon. William R. Castle, Jr.,
Department of State,
Washington, D.C.
