=== Page 157 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
AFFAIRS
Memorandum
Dewartmont of State
Subjeot: Japanese Fishing Aotivities in
Alaskan Waters.
F
In a letter from the Aoting Seoretary of Commerce
R
dated July 8, 1930, the Department of State was informed
894.628/82
that certain Japanese fishing vessels had been operating
off Port Moller, Bering Sea; that if the Japanese ex-
tended their activities to salmon fishing in the Bristol
Bay region of Bering Sea, they would no doubt work havoc
with the salmon industry of that region; and that it was
hoped that the Department of State after consideration
of the matter, miht suggest some course of aotion to
be pursued in the event that the Japanese operations
under consideration should become a detriment to the
American fishery industries of the Pacific Coast.
PU HD
The matter was at once taken up with the American-
1032
Embassy in Tokyo, since which there have been numerous
exchanges of correspondence between the Department of
State and the Embassy in Tokyo and between the Depart-
ment of State and the Department of Commeroe. There
have also been a number of conversations between offi-
cials of the Department of State and the Department of
Commerce,

=== Page 158 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Commerce, at some of which there were present officers
from the Embassy in Tokyo. The last communication ex-
changed between the Department of State and the Depart-
ment of Commeroe was the letter from the Department of
State dated November 13, 1931. The last conversation
on the subjeot was that between Mr. Miller of this
Department and Mr. Bowers of the Bureau of Fisheries
on January 2, 1932.
The Embassy in Tokyo reported that the Japanese
were prepared to consider favorably an understanding
which would restriot their activities in the waters
in question, provided compensation could be provided.
The Embassy suggested that, as it was the opinion of
the Bureau of Fisheries that the interference of Japan-
ese fishing with the salmon industry might subsequently
beoome a matter of considerable importanoe, and as it
was possible that by that time the Japanese interest
in the fishing industry in the Bering Sea might have
become greater on account of the investment of capital,
it would probably be easier to reach an understanding
now rather than later. However, the Embassy was unable
to proceed further in the matter without specific sug-
gestions from the Bureau of Fisheries concerning the
nature

=== Page 159 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
nature of the restriotions desired and the concessions
whioh could be offered to the Japanese as an inducement
to restriot the activities of Japanese fishing vessels
in the Bering Sea.
In a letter of February 24, 1931, to Mr. O'Malley,
Mr. Neville, Counselor of the Embassy in Tokyo, suggested
that Mr. O'Malley, or someone in his organization go to
Japan to go over the question with the Japanese. At
one time it was the understanding of the Department of
State that Mr. O'Malley, Commissioner of Fisheries,
was inclined to agree with this suggestion, and that
he contemplated making a trip to Japan with a view to
disoussing the question with the appropriate Japanese
offioials.
On Deoember 30, 1931, the Department received the
following telegram from the Ambassador in Tokyo:
"May we expeot O'Malley soon? It would be advisable
if anything is to be aooomplished before the
next boat season."
Mr. Miller of the Department of State made inquiries
concerning this matter of Mr. Bowers, 'Chief of the Alaskan
Division of the Bureau of Fisheries, on January 2, 1932,
and asoertained that Mr. O'Malley's proposed trip to Japan
has been abandoned and that the Department of Commeroe
contemplated

=== Page 160 === (confidence 0.94)
-4 -
contemplated taking no aotion in regard to this matter
in the near future. Consequently, the following telegram
was sent to the Embassy in Tokyo on January 5:
"Commissioner O'Malley informs the
Department that for reasons of economy and
other considerations no further aotion in
this matter is contemplated at present."

My dear Mr. Secretary:
231
The Commissioner of Fisheries has received a telegram sent recently
by J. J. O'Donnell, master of the Bureau of Fisheries vessel Crane, en
route between Seattle, Washington, and Bristol Bay, Alaska, reading as
follows:
Large steamer apparently Japanese anchored at approximately
fifty five degrees thirty three minutes north latitude one
894.628/84
sixty three degrees thirty minutes west longitude smaller
tenders setting fishing gear approximately seven miles off
Amak Island.
Upon plotting this position on Coast and Geodetic Survey chart No.
8802 it is noted that the steamer is anchored approximately 13 miles north-
west of Amak Island off the Alaska Peninsula coast in Bering Sea.
As the smaller tenders are setting fishing gear approximately seven
miles off Amak Island, it is apparent that their operations are between the
place where the ship is anchored and the nearest part of Alaska, which is
Amak Island.
This steamer is anchored only a few miles from the place where the
Japanese vessel Nagato Maru and fishing launches were observed on August 1,
1931, by the master of the vessel Chirikof operated by the Alaska Packers
Association, lll California Street, San Francisco, California. The cir-
ment of Commerce under date of October 21, 1931. /.
AD
5
C0
The substance of the information contained herein-above has been 
communicated by the Commissioner of Fisheries to the Commandant of the
United States Coast Guard, with a request that vessels of that service as-
signed to the Bering Sea patrol make observations and check up from time
to time in respect to Japanese fishing activities off the Alaskan coast
this season.
It is suggested that the Department of State obtain a statement through
the Embassy of the United States at Tokyo 'regarding the names and dates of
departure of Japanese vessels which may operate in waters of the high seas

=== Page 164 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
off the coast of Alaska, together with a general report concerning the
character and extent of their activities.
It will be remembered fron previous correspondence which this
Department has had with the Department of State that this Department is
not concerned particularly with regard to the activities of Japanese
vessels which may be engaged in the taking and canning of crabs off the
coast of Alaska, but it is much concerned as to the possibility of such
vessels engaging in salmon fishing. Great damage to our highly valuable
salmon fishery might result through the operations of Japanese vessels
in waters of the high seas adjacent to Alaska. The correspondence in-
dicates that the Japanese do not intend to undertake any such salmon
operations off the Alaskan coast, and it is sincerely hoped that there
will be strict observance of this policy in the future.
The Department of Commerce will be interested in any further in-
formation on this subject which may be secured by the Department of State.
In the meantime, it is the purpose of this Department to inform the De-
partment of State of any important developments coming to attention on the
sub ject.
Very truly yours,
Adtine Secretey of Commerce.

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
With reference to the Department's instruction No. 28
of July 1, 1932,(File No. 894.628/), it has been ascertained
from the Foreign Office that the boats referred to in the
Department's instruction under reference must have been
the so-called "Kawasaki-sen" attached to the NAGATO-MARU,
as she carried eight boats of that category in addition to
two auxiliary motor launches. The Foreign Office likewise
stated that a " Kawasaki-sen" is a small fishing boat,
about 30-40 feet in length, generally having a capacity
of 6-7 tons, and 10-15 h.p. if equipped with a motor; it
is

=== Page 185 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
is this kind of craft that actually carries on fishing
operations on the spot, and, therefore, at least eight
or nine "Kawasaki-sen" are deemed natural and indispensable
accessories to a floating cannery.
Apparently these "Kawasaki-sen" are considered as
normal equipment and consequently were not included in
the previous mention of "two auxiliary motor-boats".
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

This letter will serve to introduce r. Kenikichi
Ueki, Managing Director of the Japan Floating Canneries
Corporation and Mr. T. Takasaki, Managing Director of the
Tokyo Seikan Kaisha, Ltd., who desire to seek your counsel
and advice in the preliminary organization work l oking
towards closer cooperation between Japan and America in
the Japanese Crab Meat Industry.
I have told these gentlemen of your deep interest
in establishing cordial and friendly reletions in inter-
national fishery projects and vour deep sympathy with the
Japanese efforts in this direction. Mr. Ueki and .r.
Takasaki will be in Tashington on October 17th and 18th
en route to the Pacific Coast to embark on a steamer to
Japan. If you will be good enough to drop me a line here
at the New York office of the Cou cil which of those two
days would be most convenient for you, and name the hour,
I will pass on the information to them and have them con-
form with that schedule.
I have & general idea of the outline of their
program and feel that you would be interested to hear their
story and to give them the benefit of your advice in the
sarly stages of organization.
Any courtesies extended to them by you will be
considered a personal favor to me.
With kindest personal regards, I am
Sincerely yours,
(Sgd.) Maurice Holland

Mr. Secretary:
The Secretary of War sends, for your
information, a copy of a radiogram from
the Governor General of the Philippine
Islands which consists of a statement
for the local press (Manila) which will
be released in Manila on December 7.
The account relates to the arrest
and detention of the crews of two Japanese
vessels found fishing unlawfully in
Philippine waters. The Governor General
reports that he will handle this matter
as any other routine violation of
Philippine laws.
Opinion of and/or action by this
Department are not requested; and this
Division perceives no reason for other
than a negative attitude on he part of
this Department.

fish, and that such cooperation would rest entirely with
Amerioan private business interests. Mr. O'Malley added
that it was his understanding that two lawyers in San
Francisoo had first suggested the possibility of cooperation
in Alaskan waters of Amerioan private interests with
Japanese fishing interests.
With regard to Japanese crab fishing in Alaskan waters,
where it is confined to Bristol Bay, the Japanese visitors
gave the following information. Several Japanese crab
companies were amalgamated last year into the Japan United
Floating Canneries Fishing Company (Nippon Godo Kosen Gyogyo
Kaisha), which is the only Japanese crab company now licensed
by the Japanese Government to catoh crabs in Alaskan waters.
By this license the catoh is limited to ll0,Ooo cases. The
catoh this year was estimated at 35,000 oases but actually
33,000 cases (of 96 one-half pound tins) had been packed.
An annual deorease in the amount of crabs caught is
anticipated. A deorease in the large-sized orabs is indicated
by the faot that whereas formerly a case averaged fifty orabs
the average is now more than one hundred crabs. In this oon-
neotion it was stated that by license the size of the orabs
whioh may be caught by this company is limited to orabs with
shells of at least six inches in diameter and that it takes
seven

=== Page 201 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
seven years for the shell of a spider crab, whioh is the
kind this company packs, to attain this size. Mr. Takasaki
said that in a fishing area near Hokkaido 100,000 cases of
orabmeat had been packed in one season but that the supply
of orabs had been so depleted that crab catohing in that
area had been prohibited for a period of six years. The
industry has now been resumed there with the limit of catoh
fixed at 50,O00 cases. In Soviet waters the large crabs
have been very greatly reduoed in number and small crabs
are now being caught. At Bristol Bay the Japanese receipts
have been slightly larger than expenditures.
Mr. Takasaki said that unless some arrangement for co-
operation with American interests were arrived at, which
would considerably lower the cost of production by making
possible the landing of the oatoh at an American port and
the purohase of tin and miscellaneous supplies from
Amerioan ports, Ja anese fishing vessels might undertake
the oatohing of other kinds of fish with resultant injury
to the salmon of Alaskan waters. He said that Japanese re-
cently had been fishing close to the three mile limit in
Soviet waters in.the path of a salmon run as a result of
whioh the Japanese had paoked 25,000 oases of salmon while
Soviet establishments along the river whioh opened into
this part of the sea had paoked only 5,000 oases.

My dear Mr. Secretary:

Forwarded herewith are excerpts fron reports received by this depart-
ment which indicate that Japanese subjects, some of then officials of the
Japanese Government, have been enguged in the gathering of informution and
894.628/94
the taking of photographs in United States harbors in the leutian Islands
for purposes of military intellicence.
So far as this department is awure, there is no international lav or
agreement, and no domestic law or regulation which would prohibit Jupanese
subjects or vessels from ent ring United States harbors in the Aleutian Is-
lands other than iska, which is by Executive urder a closed harbor under
the jurisdiction of this derartment. It is, however, manifestly unethical
and contrary to international custom und usage for the agents or subjects of
one nation to enter the territorial jurisaiction of another nation for the
obvious and manifest purpose of cathering military intelligence. In so far
as these acts were committed in connection with the praparatious for a trans-
Pacific flight by a Japanese mane, he i ht of which within merican
jurisdiction was authorized by this covernm nt, they constituted a breach
of good faith. There is no doubt thut the Japunese Government  ould actively
resent any similar actions by citizens of the United states in harbors and
territory under Japanese jarisdiction.
This information is furnished your depurt ent for such use as you
may choose to make of it.

Inclosure - A -
Excerpts from a report submitted by the Commander, Bering Sea Patrol
Force, Coast Guard, dated 1 October, 1931:
"During the current season an endeavor has been made to obtain
information relative to the activities of the Imperial Japanese
Fisheries Steamer HAKUHO MARU along the Aleutian Islands. This ves-
sel has not been seen nor heard from during the present year. Also,
information was obtained relative to the activities of the Japanese
who visited the Islands in connection with the proposed 'Good-will'
flight across the Pacific. The information obtained is put down in
the form of notes as obtained from the persons interviewed.
"Mr. Fred Schroeder, trader and fur rancher of Attu Island,
states that the IAkUHO MARU came into Attu Harbor from the westward
on June 29, 1930 and left the following day. They came in for water
and said they were coming back at a certain time. The officers and
crew of the ship did not ask permission to land and seemed to do
about as they pleased. They wanted to fish but were refused permis-
sion, Mr. Schroeder telling them that there were very few fish and
these were needed for the natives. In spite of this refusal they
went to the native gill nets and helped themselves to the fish and
made no offer to pay for the fish taken. lir. Schroeder states that
this ship always left about a day or so before a Coast Guard cutter
arrived. The HAKUHO MAHU visited Attu twice in 1929, arriving there
first on June 24 of that year. They sent boats ashore for water and
landed again July 13. The Attu natives said that at the first land-
ing on June 24 the bottoms of the boats were in good condition, the
natives particularly noticed this fact when the Japanese were clean-
ing out their boats for boating off water. On the second arrival,
however, the natives noticed that the boat bottoms were all chewed
up showing evidence of landing on beaches. The natives and lr.
Schroeder thought the Japanese were taking live foxes on the various
Fox islands as that was just the right time of year to catch foxes
and it is known that Japanese are starting blue fox farms somewhere
in northern Japan. On west Semichi, an Island near Attu, the natives
found a target made of driftwood during the winter of 1929 and 1930
and alongside the target were several used cartridge cases from pis-
tols. They also found a Japanese tea cup. Iir. Schroeder reports
that in the winters following the summers that the Japanese visited
-1-

=== Page 209 === (confidence 0.99)
the Aleutians the catches of foxes on the different Islands were
distressingly small. In view of this fact white men were brought
up from San Francisco as watchmen but they stayed only one year.
This vessel, the HAKUHO MARU visited along the Islands during 1929
and l930 and both natives and whites were very much disturbed about
the Japanese activities along the Aleutians. Attu is a native com-
munication Fox island but as stated before, Mr. Schroeder says the
Japanese Government vessel never requested permission to land.
"During the present season the Japanese trawler KOKUSAI ARU
No. lo0 visited Attu, Atka and Unalaska in connection with the pro-
posed 'Good-will' flight across the Torth Pacific and Lir. Schroeder
reports the trawler as reaching Attu on Liay 2. They showed great
familiarity with the water around Attu and came in the narrow en-
trance to Attu harbor which has a depth of only fourteen feet on a
dark night at good speed. This trawler landed three Japanese, two
of whom said they were Staff correspondents for the Japanese news-
paper HOCHI SHI.BUI of Tokio. The names of the correspondents are:
E. Sugimoto and S. Kondo. The third Japanese landed at Attu, named
Imarnura, said he was the cameraman of the party. He had a regular
camera and a movie camera. The cameraman took pictures, so many that
he finally ran out of films both still and movie. These Japanese
conducted themselves in a very proper manner and did nothing all sum-
mer but wait for the return of the trawler. They were very liberal
with money at Attu and seemed to have plenty.
"The foregoing information was furnished by Ir. Fred Schroeder
who visits Attu each year and remains about six months. Lir. Schroeder's
address 1s: 345 3lst Avenue, San Francisco, California.
"Captain A. M. Nelson of the Gas-screw EUNICE belonging to the
Alaska Commercial Company of Unalaska heard various reports about the
Japanese during his cruises between Unalaska and Attu at which times
he touched at the various Islands. The cook on the EUNICE, a native
of Attu, told Captain Nelson that in 1929 when the Japanese Govern-
ment boat HAKUHO MARU was in Attu,he, the cook, told the Japanese that
the Coast Guard cutter would be in the next day. The Japanese asked
him how he knew and he said the news had come by radio. Then, he
said, the Japanese ship left the following day and before the cutter
arrived. This native cook could talk some Japanese and so was able
to converse with them. The Japanese asked the cook about what season
-2-

=== Page 210 === (confidence 0.98)
the fish ran out along the Islanus, the location of the creeks where the
fish would be running, the kinds of fish, etc. Also, they usked how the
foxes were trapped along the Islands and the season for trapping, and where
the best trapping was to be had along the islands.
"Cartain Nelson stated in regard to the mov ments of the trawler
KOKUSAI MARU during the present year in connection with the proposed
'Good-will' flight, that this vessel proceeded to Atka from Attu and
put three men ashore on the latter island, the party consisting of one
correspondent, one botanist and one cameraman. The botanist was busy
all the time collecting specimens while at Atka but he went on to Dutch
Harbor later on and joined the trawler at Unalaska. During the first
night of the trawler's visit to Atka, May 7, all of the native men in
the village were drunk on liquor obtained from the crew of the Japanese
vessel.* The cameraman at Atka took pictures from all angles
and from the mountains on either side of the bay.
"Mr. E. K. Pedler, Agent in Charge of the Alaska Comnercial
Company's station, Unalaska, Alaska reports that the Japanese trawler
KOKUSAI MARU No. 100 arrived Unalaska May 9, 1931 after having placed
parties on Attu and Atka. Four men fron the trawler came ushore and
took up quarters at the Alaska Commercial Company's house at Unalaska:
H. Kimura, T. Tanaka, Araio Toichiro and I. Aoki. H. Kimura is cor-
respondent for the HoCHI SHIMBUN, daily Paper of Tokio. T. Tanaka
represented himself to be an aeronautical expert. Tanaka stated that
he had over 3,Oo0 hours in the air and was a Captain in the Japanese
Navy having been detached from the Bureau of Conmunications of the In-
perial Japanese Navy for the purpose of observing the 'Good-will'
flight. Araio Toichiro said he was a scientist and was connected with
the Japanese Bureau of Fisheries. Toichiro was farticularly interested
in the activity of all vessels at Unalaska and spent his time while
here in hunting for fish scales which he claimed to be able to read.
He was also very much interested in nets, size of the web and other
fishing gear. He also had considerable curiosity in regard to the hal-
ibut boats, the amount of fish they had aboard and their gear. The
fourth member, I. Aoki, was a professional cameraman of the SnocHIkU
Moving Picture Corporation. This man came well prepared with supplies
of films for both the still canera and moving picture camera and took
pictures during his entire stay of all activities. The Unalaska dock
was under construction at that time and this as well as the arrival or
departure of every vessel, Coast Guard, Navy, or Merchant was photo-
graphed. He also climbed the high mountains in the Unalaska locality
-3 -

=== Page 211 === (confidence 0.99)
to take panoramic views. The Captain of the vessel who did not
stay ashore was H. Honda. This man had the rank of Lieutenant Com-
mander in the Japanese Naval Reserve. The Chief Officer was on de-
tached duty from one of the large mercantile lines of Japan and
they also had aboard the vessel two radiomen who stood continual
watohes. These radiomen were of a higher type than is ordinarily
found on a trawler.
"The Fish expert, LIr. Toichiro, after a comparatively short
stay left Unalaska under orders from his home office bound for Japan
on the Steamer STARR to Seward and thence to Seattle. From state-
ments made just before his departure he said that he did not think
that the flight would take place and that as he had gathered all the
information we could furnish about the fishing industry he was
pleased to be ordered home. T. Tanaka, who was somewhat of a pessi-
mist in regard to the flight and thought it never would take place
on account of the small size of the flying ship, left on June 29 for
Seattle and thence home. Tanaka had received instructions from the
Imperial office to report back to Japan as quickly as possible.
Kimura and Aoki left on the KOKUSAI MARU when she left here bound
for Japan via Atka, Attu and Petropavlosk on August 3rd. The Activity
of the Japanese representatives in inquiring into the fishing industry
here was very noticeable. In fact, at a dinner given in the Company's
house, Captain Honda made a statement that the fish did not have any
flag and speaking on behalf of his Company, the International Fishing
Corporation of Japan, stated he thought that some arrangement should
be made for the conservation of this food supply.
"The Japanese were all well supplied with American money, some
of which they had brought with them from Japan and during their stay
in Unalaska several deposits were made by the Newspaper to the credit
of H. Kimura. The Japanese were particularly anxious to acquire Amer-
ican footwear but did not purchase any woolen clothing. We had no
trouble with them whatsoever in regard to breaking our laws and out-
side of their personal habits which I mentioned before their visit
could not be taken exception to.
"The Japanese crab-canning ship NAGATO MARU FUCHU arrived Bristol
Bay in the spring of the present year and left during the month of
August. This ship repeated her operations of last year and remained
in practically the same locality.
4

=== Page 212 === (confidence 0.98)
"The Coast Guard cutter CHELAN visited Atka and Attu later on
in the summer and interviewed the natives relative to the Japanese
activities this year. It was ascertained that at Attu the three
Japanese took pictures and gathered rock specimens but took no sound-
ings so far as was known. Last year the Japanese sounded out Holtz
Bay according to the native chief. At Atka the three Japanese stayed
at the chief's house. There were two scientists (one botanist and
apparently one marine biological) and a reporter. They were said to
have taken a great many pictures, many of them were distant views.
They were active in their various pursuits during their stay and the
marine man may have taken soundings, although nothing definite is
known. Both scientists were graduates of Tokio University. This in-
formation was obtained from Mr. Brown, Storekeeper of Atka."

Inclosure - B -
Excerpts from a report submitted by the Commanding Officer of a survey-
ing expedition sent by the Navy Department to Alaska during the summer of
1932.
** * * During the sojourn of this Expedition along the Aleutian
Islands, the report of Commander Bering Sea Patrol, U.S.Coast Guard,
was checked and verified in every detail. The reports submitted be-
low are therefore, for the most part, repetition of information al-
ready on file in the Navy Department. In some cases, however, ampli-
fication is indicated and in others new details are brought out. The
reports are therefore submitted as received.
"Ir. G. R. Gardner of Unalaska submitted the following report:
Unalaska, Alaska, June 20,1932.
"The Kokusai Maru anchored close to the blank buoy Unalaska harbor
at 9 p.m., May 9, 1931. She was the tender ship for the Japanese Good-
will Flight of the Hochi Shimbun Newspaper of Tokio. The Kokusai Maru
is owned and operated by the International Fishing Company, Showa Bldg.,
Marnnouchi, Tokio, Japan. Captain I. Honda was in command of the ves-
sel.
"Captain Honda was fishing in Bering Sea with the Kokusai Maru
in 1930 and came into Dutch Harbor for water at which time Captain Shan-
ley in Command of Coast Guard Cutter Haida, arrested the crew and de-
tained the ship, for illegally enterin: an American Port. The vessel
and crew was released in about twenty four hours on orders from Washing-
ton, D.C.
"The Kokusai Maru, en route to Unalaska, left three men at Attu
and three at Atka, reported to be Press representatives, engineers and
photographers. The personnel stationed ashore at Unalaska were: Toichiro
Sari, an engineer with the Hokkaido government; Rear Admiral Tanika, auro-
nautical expert; H. Kimura, News Reporter, representing the Hochi Shimbun
newspapers and Isamo Aoki, professional photographer, all well educated
men, including Captain Honda.
"Toichiro Sari, the engineer left here on the 'Starr' for Seattle,
via Seward on June 29, 193l. Rear Admiral Tanika left here for Seattle,
on July 'Starr' via Seward. Kimura and Aoki remained here until August
3, 193l, when they left on the Kokusai Maru, stopping at Atka and Attu
to pick up their representatives.
-1-

=== Page 214 === (confidence 0.98)
"During the visit of the Unalaska delegation I saw the engineer
Sari, taking pictures of the waterfront. That is: he would take a
picture then walk about one hundred yards up the street, take another
picture and continued in this manner until he had covered the full
distance of the waterfront.
"Aoki took pictures of this harbor, the Taval Radio Station, Dutch
Harbor docks and oil tanks and of the Unalaska village and docks and
of the adjacent bays and mountains from every angle possible. Aoki
was in no hurry -- he had plenty of time and repeatedly went on picture-
taking trips to the mountains in this vicinity. Ir. Farley, Chief Radio
man at that time, (since retired) caught Aoki taking moving pictures of
the station. Farley stopped him and demanded the film. Aoki hurriedly
cut the film and gave it to him only to find he had received the unex-
posed end of the film! Aoki was still in possession of the pictures he
had taken."
The Commanding Officer of the surveying expedition further states:
"Mr. G. L. Devenny, Master of L..S.ALAsco IV at Adak Island, reported
on 12 June, 1932, the following:
"Captain Devenny was given passage on the MOlUsAI  ARt from Atka to
Unalaska during the summer of 193l, having lost his owm ship, the Isc0,
by stranding on south side of Atka Island. The Captain of the KOkUSAI
refused passage to other applicants, but granted it to Captain Devenny
and his engineer as shipwrecked mariners.
"Captain Devenny said that he believes every officer on the ship
was a naval officer, most of them wearing uniforns, an unusual thing on
trawlers. He was unable to read their charts, as they were marked in
Japanese, but they seemed more in detail and larger scale than the cor-
responding C. & G. S. charts. Both he and the engineer were questioned
as to details of the waters of the Aleutians with which they were fam-
iliar.
"Thile entering Unalaska Bay, Captain Devenny warned the Captain of
the KOkUSAI that he was running into danger. Phis caused creat consterna-
tion, the engine was stopped and there was a good deal of confusion in
the wheel house. Captain Devenny was finally requested to conn the ship
clear and pilot it into the harbor which he did. lotion and still pictures
of entrances of both Nazan Bay and Dutch Harbor were taken from the vessel.
-2-

=== Page 215 === (confidence 0.98)
"Captain Devenny gained a strong impression that the trans-Pacific
flight for which the KOKUSAI MARU was supposed to be the advance base
was a blind to enable the ship to operate in the islands, it having
been ordered away by the Coast Guard on a previous trip. He believed
that there was never any intention of actually completing the fliht.
"Mr. W. C. Brown of Atka Island submitted enclosure (B), croup
pictures of the three members of 'KOKUsAI I ARU' that were left at Atka
during the absence of that ship at Unalaska - Dutclh harbor area. Ir.

ity at Atka.
"On 23 June at Unalaska, Alaska, during the course of conversation
with Doctor Eric Hulten, Sweedish Botanist, of Stockholm University, he
produced two pamphlets, Vol. I and II (translations from the Japanese)
Notes on plants of Western Aleutian Islands, collected in l929, by L.
Tatewaki.' These pamphlets dealt with the plant life at Chichagof Harbor,
Attu; Constantine Harbor, Amchitka and lazan Bay, Atka.
"Doctor Hulten had but one each copy of the above mentioned pamphlets
and desired to used them in connection with his study of plant life among
the Aleutian Islands, but stated that duplicates would be readily obtained
from the author, c/o Imperial University, Sotanical Department, Sapporo,
Japan.
"According to the best information available, no Japanese botanist
was kmown to be operating in this group of islands during the year 1929.
"Note: Doctor Hulten is a very interesting character who has
spent years in the Kamchatka Peninsula area, and ad-
jacent islands, in connection with his studies of plant
life of that region and which he is now ostensibly fol-
lowing in the Aleutian group. He apparently has en-
joyed many privileges of the Japanese while operating
in the Kamchatka area, having been decorated by them
for heroism displayed some years ago when a Japanese
man of war was wrecked near Petropovlavsk. He frankly
admits the greatest esteem for the Japanese, and quite
as frankly displays malice toward the United States, as
a result of treatment received at the hands of the U.
S. Customs officials at San Francisco. He also severely
criticized the U.S. Coast Guard Service, because, it is
understood, he was refused admittance to Kiska in con-
nection with his studies. Captain Boedecker, Comnander
Bering Sea Patrol, U.S.C.G. did not trust Doctor Hulten,
-3-

=== Page 216 === (confidence 0.98)
believing that he might be in the service of the In-
perial Japanese Government. Captain Boedecker did
not state upon what grounds he based these suspi-
cions, except lulten's insistance upon visiting
Kiska and his long association with the Japanese.
Doctor Thulten, due to his long association with
the Japanese, can not be placed above suspicion. It
was the concensus of opinion of officers of this Ex-
pedition that he is a scientist whose whole interest
in life is to carry on his botanical studies, and that
he would undcubtedly make creat sacrifices in order to
carry on these studies. His personal finances, from
observation based upon the way he lived in Unalaska,
were, apparently, quite limited."

THE RUSSO-JAPANESE DISPUTE OVER EXCHANGE RATE FOR
FISHERY RENTALS.
From the nature of the dispute, the issues
involved and the acerbity of the argument it would
seem that history were repeating itself in the matter
of the current Russo-Japanese altercation over the
exchange rate at which Japanese fishing interests
shall pay for the use of Soviet salmon grounds. It
sounds much like the difference of opinion in l931
over the same issue and serves to show that the
Shidehara-Troyanovsky ▲greement of that year was but
a palliative and not a permanent solution of the
exchange question.
Treaty Rights.
The right of Japanese to fish in the territorial
waters of the Maritime Provinces and Kamchatka derives
from the provisions of the Treaty of Portsmouth of
1905. Since 1928 this right has been interpreted and
more clearly defined by the Russo-Japanese fishing
convention of that year, which was successful in
clarifying most outstanding issues but was silent on
the since exacerbating question of the exchange rate
at which Japanese operators were to pay in Yen for
fishing grounds rented in Rubles. The treaty of 1928
is to expire in l936 and negotiations for the agreement
which is to replace it will commence in 1935 if not
sooner. Within the general framework of the existing
treaty the fisheries of the Siberian marginal sea have
been apportioned between Russian and Japanese operators.
In 1933 Japanese fished 52.7% of the grounds and
Russians

=== Page 239 === (confidence 1.00)
-4-
Russians 47.3%, the Japanese catch amounting to 58%
of the total for the season and the Russian to 42%.
These figures do not include the less important crab
(1)
fisheries.
The Exohange Problem.
Between 1928 and 1931, when conditions became so
involved as to require the temporary alleviation
provided by the Shidehara-Troyanovsky Agreement, there
was no definite arrangement as to the payment of
fishery rents in rubles by Japanese operators.
Auctions were held every year in Vladivostok, at which
time the aggregate of Soviet fishing grounds was put
up for bidding, the highest offer taking the districts
in question. In 1928 the Japanese fishers secured
85.7% of the coastal grounds, in 1929 65.2% and in
1930 55.2%, since which time the apportionment of the
(1)
areas has been at about that ratio.
There was no
official rate of exchange between Yen and Ruble,
al though there was worked out a de facto rate of
(2)
around 40 sen per Ruble during the period.
However,
the Bank of Chosen in its Vladivostok branch, purchased
"bootleg" Rubles at a rate under that which was regard-
ed by the Soviet officials as more or less the going
figure and the Japanese fishing companies naturally
purchased their Rubles from the Bank of Chosen at a
more advantageous rate, reported to be at from 27 to
(2)
28 sen per Ruble.
 This activity on the part of the
Bank of Chosen resulted in its closure by the Russian
authorities and was the immediate issue which called
into being the negotiations between Foreign Minister
Shidehara

=== Page 240 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
Shidehara and Ambassador Troyanovsky.
The outcome of these negotiations was the
Shidehara-Troyanovsky Agreement of April 26, 1931,
which provided, in substance, that in the payment of
fishery rentals the exchange rate should be 32 1/2
sen per Ruble, pending a final settlement of the
question. The Russian and Japanese Governments would
continue negotiations with a view to such a permanent
(3)
settlement.
This protocol governed the si tuation until
January 1, 1934.
The Present Dispute.
On the first of January, 1934 the representatives
of the Japanese fishing interests in Vladivostok were
informed by the Soviet authorities that the State Bank
had announced that the exchange rate for the forth-
coming fishery auction in February would be 75 sen per
(3)
Ruble instead of 32 1/2 sen.
In response to a protest
from the Japanese fishing companies, the local Russian
authorities declared that the matter was out of their
hands and that they were acting upon instructions from
(4)
Moscow.
On February llth, nine days before the annual
auction was to be held in Vladivostok, the Japanese
Ambassador, Mr. Ota, drew the attention of the
Commissar for Foreign Affairs, Mr. Litvinov, to the
question of the alleged violation by Russia of the
Shidehara-Troyanovsky Agreement, receiving a polite
but non-committal reply. The efforts of the Japanese
Embassy at Moscow to address protests to high officials
in the Commissariat of Foreign Affairs met with little
success

=== Page 241 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
success, as the officials became suddenly indisposed
or unavailable. An under-official finally informed
the Embassy that the matter of the exchange rate was
a concern of the Central Commercial Commissariat, to
which the protests of the Japanese Government had
(5)
been referred.
Impatient, the spokesman of the Foreign Office
in Tokyo informed the press on February l9th that
"the attitude of the Moscow Governnent is making a
very bad impression on the Japanese Government".
He
went further and was reported to recall in this
connection the statement by Viscount Kikujiro Ishii
in his recent diplomatic memoirs that "the Russians
do not observe treaty commitments when matters of
(6)
importance to them are involved."
The following day, on February 20th, the auction
of fishing grounds was held in Vladivostok. One
hundred and seventeen grounds were presented for lease
(7)
during 1934, of which all but six were salmon fisheries.
The other half-dozen precincts were for crab. Japanese
operators bid for 84 of the grounds. Seventy-four
were bid for by the one Nichiro Fishery Company, the
great monopoly in which the majority of the Japanese
fishers in Russian waters is amalgamated. The Showa
Fishery Company bid for 9 grounds and there was one
individual bid. All the Japanese bids were accompanied
by certified checks for Ruble amounts at the old exchange
rate of 32 1/2 sen per Ruble. Russian representatives
(8)
bid for 69 fishing grounds.
The Soviet authorities at Vladivostok threw out
the

=== Page 242 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
the Japanese bids on the ground that the exohange rate
should have been 75 sen per Ruble instead of 32 1/2
sen. Sixty fishing grounds were awarded to Russian
(8)
bidders.
The auction had been preceded by an
unsuccessful three day campaign on the part of the
Japanese Consul General at Vladivostok, who conferred
with Russian officials on the 17th, 18th and 19th and
was still attempting to win his case at 12:30 on the
(9)
morning of the 2oth when bidding officially commenced.
The next step of the Tokyo Government was to
instruct its Ambassador at Moscow to request that the
Soviet Government either recognize as valid the
Japanese bids made on the 20th or hold an entirely
(10)
new auction.
Mr. Ota called on Mr. Litvinov on the
23rd, presenting this view and indicating that the
Japanese Government regarded the Shidehara-Troyanovsky
(11)
Agreement as still controlling the exchange rate.
Mr. Litvinov was reported to have proposed that
a second auction be held at Vladivostok, but only for
the 57 fishing grounds remaining after the Russian
bids for 60 grounds had been accepted on the 20th at
the first auction. Japanese bidders at a second
auction might present checks for Rubles calculated at
the 32 1/2 sen rate, but these would be considered
only as payments in part, to be followed by additional
payments after the exohange rate had been finally
determined by negotiation. The second auction might
be held on February 28th. The proposal of the Foreign
(10/11)
Commissar was rejected by the Japanese Ambassador.
Representatives of the Japanese Foreign Office,
the

=== Page 243 === (confidence 0.99)
-8-
the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry (in direct
control of fisheries) end the fishing interests were
reported to have conferred on the situation on
February 27th, after which the Japanese Ambassador
at Moscow and the Consul General at Vladivostok were
instructed to inform the Russian authorities that the
Soviet proposal for a supplementary auction on the
(12)
28th was rejected.
The month closed with the stalemate persisting.
Russia insisted that, although the exchange rate might
be open to negotiation, it must be higher than the
old rate of 32 1/2 sen per Ruble. Japan was firm in
its stand that the provisions of the Shidehara-
Troyanovsky Agreement still controlled the fishery
auction, and made not the slightest suggestion that
the rate fixed in 1931 might be subject to negotiation.
FISHERY

=== Page 244 === (confidence 1.00)
-9-
FISHERY MERGERS.
Renewal of Talk re Merger of Nichiro and Kyodo Fishery
Companies.
There was an echo in February of the loud rumor
and speculation in December over the prospects of a
merger of Japan's two largest fishing enterprises, the
Nichiro Fishery Company and the Kyodo Fishery Company.
Nichiro, in which the Mitsubishis are interested,
already has a practical monopoly of Japanese operations
in Russian waters, while Kyodo, a subsidiary of the
Kuhara interests, specializes in pelagic fishing. In
December Kyodo made a determined effort to acquire
control of the Nichiro company and was only defeated
when the Government indicated that it regarded the
Kyodo project as inimical to the best interests of the
(13)
fishing industry.
During February there was renewed talk of an
amalgamation between the Kyodo and Nichiro companies.
Nichiro was still hostile to the Kyodo overtures, but
appeared willing to listen to its representations.
The ostensible advantage of a merger was stated to be
that of presenting a united front against Russia in
regard to the current troubles over fishery auctions,
but since the Kuhara company is engaged in high seas
fishing and does not have to secure Russian permission
for its activities, this excuse was nothing but a
pretext. The real reason was the one so attractive
to large Japanese capitalists: the securing of an
absolute monopoly and the profits to be derived from
a monopoly.
The

=== Page 245 === (confidence 0.99)
-10-
The month closed with the negotiations between
(14)
Nichiro and Kuhara still in progress.
More Kuhara Mergers.
February was a month of mergers for the Kuhara
dynasty of Tokyo. Not only were the initial steps
taken for the acquisition of the great Osaka Iron
Works, but in addition the Kyodo Fishery Company made
arrangements to absorb the Toyo Whaling Company, while
at the close of February it was announced that the
greatest Kuhara firm, the Japan Industry Company,
would take over both the Kyodo Fishery, the Toyo
Whaling Company and the Dai Nihon Ice Manufacturing
Company. The Kuharas were integrating their industrial
empire with a particular view to the rationalization
of their fishing interests. They already control the
Daido Fishing Company in the pelagic industry and were
reported in December to be organizing thirty small
independent high seas operators into the Kuriles New
(13)
Development Company.
On February 5th the Kyodo Fishery Company signed
a contract with the Toyo Whaling Company of Osaka for
the taking over of the latter firm on the basis of an
exchange of stock: one share for Kyodo for two and
one-half of Toyo. Toyo Whaling has a nominal capitali-
zation of ¥6,000,000 with paid up capital of ¥3,750,000.
The acquisition of the whaling enterprise by Kyodo
would have given the latter company a nominal capital
of 16,600,000 of which 14,185,000 would be paid up.
The contract specified that Kyodo would take over all
the assets and liabilities of Toyo Whaling as of
January

=== Page 246 === (confidence 0.99)
-11-
January 1, 1934, the actual merger to take place on
(15)
June 5, 1934.
However, at the close of the month it was announced
that the Kyodo Fishery Company itself would be
amalgamated with its parent firm, the Kuhara-controlled
Japan Industry Company. In this case, the Toyo Whaling
Company would be taken over directly by the Japan
(16)
Industry Company. The Japan Industry Company later
indicated that it had decided to absorb in addition
the great Dai Nihon Ice Manufacturing Company, which
(17)
has a paid up capital of 28,670,000.
It was clearly evident that the Kuharas were
reorganizing their fishing enterprises so as to
incorporate them under one direction. The result will
be one of the mightiest fishing enterprises in Japan
and one that may well bid for the control of the
heretofore supreme Nichiro Fishery monopoly. Should
the proposed mergers go into effect, the capitalization
(17)
of the Japan Industry Company will exceed ¥100,000,000
and the Kuharas will enter the ranks of the other great
family dynasties of Japanese industry, the Mitsuis and
Mitsubishis.
It would appear that internal developments in the
Japanese fishing industry are fraught with almost as
much potentiality for conflict as the international
questions raised in the northern seas.
GOVERNMENT

=== Page 247 === (confidence 1.00)
-12-
GOVERNMENT CONTROL OF FISHERIES.
The Japanese Government has long exercised control
of the national fisheries with a view to the development
of this highly important industry. Dating from 1897
and before, legislation has continually been passed with
a view to the subsidizing of fishing enterprises, the
creation of harbors for fishermen, the provision of
storage facilities, the protection of vested fishery
rights and the organization of fishery guilds. The
fishing industry presents the anomaly of being at once
the enterprise most carefully encouraged and supervised
by the Government and the industry most dominated by
the monopoly of private capitalism. The question of
State control of fishing was again raised in February.
The Government was interpellated in
the Diet on
the matter of whether closer supervision would be
exercised over the north-seas fisheries.
Premier Sai to
replied that he saw the need of State control and that
the Foreign Minister was already looking forward to the
revision of the Russo-Japanese fishery convention.
The
Minister of Agriculture and Forestry, Mr. Goto,
expressed similar views and added that his Ministry
was investigating the possibility of creating harbors
for Japanese fishing craft in the Kurile Islands. He
thought that the Government would not sanction the
establishment of any new Japanese fishing companies
this year. Foreign Minister Hirota was reported to
have suggested that it would be advisable for the
Japanese fishing industry to station a permanent
representative in Moscow who, although not a Government
official

=== Page 248 === (confidence 0.99)
-13-
official, could conduct negotiations with the Soviet
authorities looking to the revision of the fishery
(18)
treaty in 1936.
Draft Marine Products Export Control Bill.
▲ repercussion of the recent instances in the
United States of the condemnation of Japanese exports
of decomposed tuna may be seen in the draft of a Marine
Products Export Bill which has beenprepared by the
Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry for submission
to the Imperial Diet.
The Ministry is reported to regard the unfortunate
cases of shipping inferior products abroad as resulting
from the chaotic condition of uncontrolled competition
which prevails in the Japanese export trade for marine
products. In an endeavor to slash prices exporters
have neglected to maintain minimum standards for the
products sold abroad.
A summary of the draft control bill indicates that
no exports of marine products will be permitted without
official inspection by the Ministry of Agriculture.
The bill confers on the Government authority for the
supervision of manufacturing processes and special
treatment for export lines. No one may manufacture
for the foreign trade without a Government permit.
The Government will have the power to order fishing
guilds and organizations to comply with certain
standards for the export trade. The exports to
Japanese colonies will be considered as in the category
of foreign trade for the purposes of the bill.
It is understood that the Government plans to
apply

=== Page 249 === (confidence 0.99)
-14-
apply the new legislation first to the tuna industry,
which was the subject of recent complaint in the
United States, extending it later to the exporters
(19)
of salmon, trout and crab.

Subject:
Japanese fishing operations in Bristol Bay, Alaska.
The Secretery of Commerce:
The Bristol Bay region of westem Alaska is the chief producer
of the world's supply of red salmon. In the season of 1934 over
1,700,000 cases of 48 one-pound cans ecch were packed by Anerican
canneries operating in these waters.
There is a creat concentration of salmon in the offshore waters
of this area. The sulmon, at the age of four or rive years, are re-
turning from their abode in the sea to the streams tributary to iristol
Day. Here they will spawn and complete their life cycle.
More than a thousand Anerican fishermen operate gill net boats on
the high seas waters of Bristol Bay in the annual catching of the
15,000,000 or more salmon which, on the averae, make up the pack from
this region. These boats for the most part operate in waters beyond
the three-mile limit.
A little farther offshore for a nuaber of years several Japanese
floating canneries have been engaged with their tenders and small boats
in the packing of crabs. This industry has not attracted merican
capital or fishermen, probably because or the high labor costs involved.
There is nothing to prevent Japanese interests from enga in: in
commercial fishing operations for salmon in these waters, if they so
desire. It is true that in the period of five yeers since floating
crab canneries have operated in this region there is no authentic record
of the taking of salmon for thhis purpose.
In former discussion with the Department of State re arding the
matter, officials of the Department of Commerce have pointed out the
great danger to the American salmon industry if the Japanese should un-
dertake the packing of salmon. They would not be bound by the regula-
tions and restrictions as to time and method of fishing, which are
applicable to those operating from the laska canneries.

=== Page 254 === (confidence 0.97)
-2 -
It is understood that tnere has heen more or less of a gentle-
man's agreement upon the part or the Japanese not to invade the
salmon fisheries of Alaska. They have wanted to continue our good
will in connection with the operation of the floatin crab canneries,
as the United States is the chief market for the Japanese pack of
canned crabs. It is understood that the Japanese have asserted their
intention to refrain from any operations in the offshore waters of
Alaska that would result in the capture of salmon.
The Department of State hes had advices from the Japanese in-
terests urging marketin and shinping concessions in connection with
their crab produets. They have wanted to n otiate an a reement with
the United States whereby the canned crabs packed on Japanese float-
ing canncries off the Alaska coast could be marketed directly in the
United States without being returned to Japan. This would be of real
advantage to the Japanese inasmuch as the United States is the chief
market for the Japenese pac: o: crabs.
Hothing of a definite nature nus ever come about alon t is line,
so far as this o fice is awar . It is understood that the matter is
simply coing along fron season to season, - the Japanese ve sels con-
tinuing to pack crabs and, as rar as kiowm, not molestin the runs of
salmon.
The point of ti is co munication, mowever, is that the Japanese
vessels constitute a threat that might seriously interfere it or
even practicaily ruin an important merican inlustry.
This leads to the thought of considerin: the wisdo. of takins u
with the Depurtment of State the matter of negotiating a treaty with
Japan, Whereby all nutionals of t at country would rofrain from en-
gaging in any salmon packing in the vaters of Terin Sea and ristol
Bay east of a line fron. Cape Avinof near the west entrance to Kuskokwim
Bay to Cape Sarichef at the west end of Unimak Island. This proposed
line is shown in red on the accompanyin up of Alusku.
of course, this matter must be approuched with caution because of
the possibility of arousing Japanese interests and causing thom to de-
mand so much in return that this nation would be the loser.
If the matter is taken up with the Japanese Government, it may
be that they will ask for elimination of the tariff on canned crabs,
or for the privilege of direct shipment of canned crabs from Bering
Sea to ports in the United States.

=== Page 255 === (confidence 0.97)
-3 -
An important point also is that the Japanese mitht ask for a
revision of the fur-seal treaty of l9ll, particularly to the end
of securing an increase over the 15 per cent of sealskins from the
Pribilof Islands, or the proceeds thereof, which they now receive.
As a matter of fact this has been taken up by Japanese authorities
in recent years, but the matter was quieted by officials of the
State Department.
It is understood that when discussion of the matter of Japen-
ese vessels in Bering Sea cane up a rew years ago, the Japanese
made representations to the Department of State that they would
seek definite advanta es not enjoyed oy them, in return for a
guarantee that they would not interfere with our salmon fisheries
in Alaska.
This office also understands that when the matter was under
consideration previcusly, officials of the Department of State felt
quite certain thet it would not be to the advantage of the Govern-
ment of the United States to raise any issues alon this line.
It was the view of these o ficials that the United States was
better off us lon as it did not actually develop that the Japanese
were interfering with American industry. So far there hus been no
such interference in respect to the salmon fishery of westemm Alaska.

CONFIDENTIAL.
My dear Mr. Secretary:
I refer to your letter of August 24, 1934, the re-
ceipt of which was acknowledged by my letter of August 31,
894.628/1.
1934. The question has been raised of the advisability of
seeking to negotiate a treaty with Japan, whereby all Japa-
nese nationals would refrain from engaging in any salmon
packing in the waters of Bering Soa and Bristol Bay east of
a line from Cape Avinof near the west entrance to Kuskokwim
.05
Bay to Cape Sarichef at the west end of Unimak Island.
It is believed that a brief review of previous de-
velopments in conneotion with the question of Japanese
fishing operations in Alaskan waters may serve to set
forth the oiroumstanoes which appear to be involved in the
suggestion under reference.
In 1930, the Department of Commerce expressed to this
Department interest in the operations of certain Japanese
vessels in Alaskan waters and, 1n its letter of October 4,
1930, the Department of Commeree pointed out that a
diffioult
The Honorable
Daniel C. Roper,
Secretary of Commeroe.

=== Page 261 === (confidence 0.98)
一8-
diffioult situation would be presented if Japanese vessels
were to undertake the capture and utilization of salmon in
international waters off the coast of Alaska. It suggested
that perhaps the only possible remedy would be the conolusion
of a treaty whereby Japanese vessels would be restrained
from such operations.
Through a series of disoussions carried on by the
American Embassy at Tokyo with various Japanese officials
and persons interested in the fishing industry, it was
ascertained that the Japanese Government could not invoke
any legislation to prevent Japanese ships from carrying
on lawful fishing operations in non-territorial waters,
and that as a practical measure the only means whereby any
restraint could be imposed would be the conclusion of a
treaty between the interested countries. It was pointed
out by the Japanese that fish 1s an important: item in the
Japanese food supply; that in proportion as difficulties
are encountered in fishing in Soviet waters the Japanese
would be forced to seek other sources of supply; and that
in the oiroumstanees then existing the Japanese Government
could not consistently restrain its nationals from lawful
fishing on the high seas.
A suggestion was made by representatives of the Japa-
nese crab packing interests that if some provision were made
by private Ameriean interests to facilitate the packing of

=== Page 262 === (confidence 0.97)
-8.
erab by Japanese floating canneries operating in waters off
the coast of Alaska, and if some concession were made by way
of redueing or removing the duty on canned orab imported into
the United States, the Japanese Government would take suitable
measures to prevent Japanese fishing vessels from operating
in those waters and from thus injuring the Alaskan salmon
industry.
It was believed by the Embassy at Tokyo, and in that
belief this Department conourred, that it would be the part
of wisdom to provide adequate restraint against operations
by Japanese fishing vessels injurious to the Alaskan salmon
industry before substantial sums were invested by Japanese
in suoh operations, but that the ciroumstances would not
warrant expectation that the Japenese Government would
participate in a treaty restraining its nationals from
operating on the high seas off the coast of Alaska unless
compensatory privileges could be offered to the Japanese.
In view of certain administrative measures which the
Japanese Government was understood to have provisionally
taken to restrict operations of Japanese trawlers off the
coast of Alaska, pending the presentation by this Government
of some definitive proposal, officers of this Department and
of the Department of Commeroe tentatively agreed in l931
that it would be desirable to detail to Tokyo a representative
of the Bureau of Fisheries in order that the possibilities of
aa

=== Page 263 === (confidence 0.98)
an arrangement being made with Japan might be explored by
informal discussion with the Japanese. It may be recalled
that for reasons of economy and other considerations it was
later found impraotioable to detail to Tokyo an officer of
the Bursau of Fisheries.
It contimes to be the view of this Department that a
satisfactory arrangement with the Japanese Government look-
ing toward the regulation of fishing operations by Japanese
in waters off the coast of Alaska should be effected as soon
as practioable. Careful consideration has been given to the
suggestion that there be proposed to the Japanese Government
the conelusion of a treaty along the lines suggested in the
memorandum of the Commissioner of Fisheries whioh is enclosed
with your letter under reference, but the Department peroeives
no ciroumstance warranting expectation that the Japanese
Covernment would undertake in the absence of some compensatory
privilege to restrain its nationals from engaging in lawful
activities on the high seas.
The Department is furnishing to the Tariff Commission
a copy of this letter with the request that the Tariff
Commission render an opinion in regard to the practicability
of reducing or eliminating the duty on canned orab. If the
opinion of the Tariff Commission should be of an affirmative
character, and if no objection should be perceived to a re-
dustion in or elimination of the duty on sanned erab from the
pos

=== Page 264 === (confidence 0.88)

oims of view of poliey, it is the opinion of this
Department that the greatest possibilities of effeoting
an agreement with the Japanese Government would be offered
by diseussions at Tokyo between officers of the Ameriomn
Embassy and a representative of the Bureau of Fisheries,
on the one hand, and the appropriate Japanese authorities
and vested private interests, on the other hand.

l4 cents per pound, come mainly from Japen and consist of smell dried fish
consumed by Orientals principally in Hawaii. In 1933 the imports of these
from Japan amounted to 1,201,991 pounds, valued at $104,775. Domestic
production is negligible.
Imports of canned oysters from Japen over the 8 cents per pound
duty (equal to about 50 percent ad valorem) are negligible, but & reduction
of 25 or 50 percent in duty might lead to a small trade. Large imports
would be unlikely at the lower rate because there is a substanti:l donestic
supply, part of which is exportec.
Canned fish specialties, such as fish cakes, pastes, and sauces,
are importec in rel&tively large quantities from Japan for consunption by
Japanese. As there is no domestic production of some of these products
and no domestic production of competitive products, a reduction in the dutics
would have no effect on the domestic fishery. The present cuties on canned
fish specielties range from 25 to 30 percent ad valorem.
Consideration might also be given to a reduction in the duty on
canned tuna and on canned clems, but as these tro products are importec from
Japan in substantial quantities and are directly competitive vith the domestic
pack a concession might not be warranted. Then, too, if excessive concessions
are grantec to Japan for the purpose of excluding Japanese vessels from the
salmon fisheries of Alaske, such concessions might become & precedent should
the vessels of another country, such as Canade, enter the Alasken fishery.

/108
ing the advisability of seeking to negotiate a treaty with Japan which
would prohibit all Japanese nationals from engaging in salmon packing
operations in the waters of Bering Sea and Bristol Bay east of a line
extending from Cape Avinof to Cape Sarichef.
It is noted that previous inquiry by the Department of State has
developed that the Japanese Covernment could not invoke any legislation
to prevent Japanese vessels from carryin on lawful fishing operations
in non-territorial waters, and that such restraint would be possible
only through the conclusion of a treaty. It is noted further that the
State Department is of the belief that it would be advisable to provide
adequate restraint a ainst operations by Japenese fishing vessels in-
jurious to the Alaska salmon industry as sooi as practicable, and before
substantial investments are made by Japanese .Iterests in such operations.
This Department is entirely in accord with these views and is of
the belief that progress toward effectin: such an agreement would result
from informal discussions at Tokyo between officers of the American
Embassy at Tokyo and a representative of the Bureau of Fisheries and
Japanese authorities and vested private interests of Japan. It is under-
stood that arrangements for such informal discussions should be held in .
abeyance pending & report from the American Embassy at Tokyo as to the
advisability and timeliness of approaching the Japanese Government in
the matter of effecting such a treaty.
AR
It is to be expected that the Japanese Government will not enter
FILED
into an agreement which will restrict the fishery operations of nation
als of that country on the high seas off the Alaska coast without re-
ceiving certain compensatory privileges. In this connection the Tariff
Commission has stated that there would be no serious injury to American
industry resulting from the removal of import duties on canned crab,
dried unsalted fish (except stockfish), canned oysters, and various
canned fish specialties now being imported from Japan.

- 2 -
It is the opinion of the Bureau of Fisheries that assured
protection of the valuable Alaska salmon fisheries from threatened
interference by Japanese cannery vessels operating in non-territorial
waters would be far more important to the United States than the
small revenue derived from import duties on the products mentioned
above.×
This Department will await with interest the report of the
American Embassy at Tokyo as to the propriety of approaching the
Japanese Governm nt in the matter at this time.

=== Page 288 === (confidence 0.99)

History of the Japanese Whale Fishery.
The seas surrounding the Japanese islands have
long been known as the habitat of whales, although
it was not until the nineteenth century, with the
advent of the Yankee whaling ship, that the fishery
was developed in those waters.
Prior to that time
Japanese fishermen occasionally brought in small
cetaceans, but there was no regular industry. The
proscription imposed by the Tokugawa Shogunate against
the building of vessels capable of deep sea navigation
was also a factor which for almost three centuries
made a Japanese whaling industry impossible.
The New Bedford and Nantucket whalers came to
Japanese waters primarily in search of the sperm
whale, then the most valuable of the species; and for
a while the Japanese fishery rivalled that of the
Seychelles in the number of sperm taken. The Bering
Sea whalers, however, working to the westward along
the Aleutian fringe, soon discovered that baleen whales
were also to be taken in Japanese waters and came in
search of right whales, humpbacks and grey whales.
Whether the American whalers of that day attempted to
take the great blue whale or fin back, the writer is
not aware, although it is certain that until the advent
of the explosive harpoon this most enormous of living
things was relatively immune from human attack.
It will be recalled that almost the first direct
interest of the United States in Japan developed from
the activity of American whalers in waters contiguous
to the coasts of the Empire. It was the necessity of
American

=== Page 289 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
American whalers securing supplies from Japanese
harbors and the difficulty to which shipwrecked
American whalers had been subjected by the laws of
the Tokugawa regime which formed one of the principal
reasons for the intervention of Commodore Perry; and
one of the chief aims of Townsend Harris in negotiating
the first commercial treaty of a western power with
Japan was to provide for the assistance of the
American whale fishery in the seas adjacent to Japan.
Following the decadence of the New England whale
industry the waters of Japan lay fallow for almost a
generation. It was not until the Norwegians and
others had perfected the technique of the small killer
vessel armed with an explosive harpoon and serving a
shore station or floating factory that there developed
a domestic whaling industry in Japan.
. Early in the
present century the Toyo Whaling Company introduced
the new technique, which was speedily adopted by three
other small enterprises. The four little firms were
amalgamated in 1909 by the Toyo Whaling Company and
from that year may be said to date the inception of
(1)
modern whaling in Japan. Toyo Whaling itself was
founded in 1899.
Since 1909 the Toyo Whaling Company has followed
a policy of acquiring smaller enterprises until the
point has been reached where the parent firm practically
controls the whaling industry of Japan. Today there
are five independent companies engaged in whaling, but
of the total fleet Toyo Whaling owns 25 out of 3l killer
vessels, representing 2,898 gross tons of the 3,705
tons

=== Page 290 === (confidence 1.00)
-4-
tons of all Japanese whalers. Similarly, of the 38
(1)
whaling stations in Japan, Toyo owns 30.
The story
of Japanese whaling, therefore, is practically the
history of this one firm.
Government Aid.
Mention should not be neglected of the role of
the Japanese Government in the development and
regulation of the Japanese whaling industry.
Under the heading of development, the principal
work of the Government has been in the application of
the Act for the Encouragement of the Pelagic Fishery.
This legislation dates from 1897, but has been subject
to numerous revisions.
As now in force, the law
provides for two types of subsidy to high seas
fishermen; one for the construction of vessels, and
one for the maintenance of actual operations. The
whaling industry benefits only from the former type
of assistance, receiving no state aid to defray cost
of operations.
The effect of the Act for the Encouragement of the
Pelagic Fishery upon the whaling industry of Japan has
been the construction of special killer vessels under
terms of a Government bounty. The little ships must
be built in conformity with the Regulations for the
Inspection of Vessels Engaged in Pelagic Waters in
order to receive the subsidy. The construction
(2)
bounties granted are as follows:
For

=== Page 291 === (confidence 1.00)
-5-
For Vessels Under 60 Gross Tons.
Per ton of vessels constructed of steel - ¥60 maximum.
Per ton of vessels constructed of wood --
¥45 maximum.
Per horsepower of steam engines
¥22 maximum.
Per horsepower of diesel motors
¥40 maximum.
These rates of subsidy may be made less than the
maximum, depending upon types of vessels built, need
for aid, and other factors.
Vessels of Over 60 Gross Tons.
Vessels of over 60 gross tons are constructed with a
bounty of not exceeding 20% of the total cost of
(2)
construction.
Another provision of the Act stipulates that under
certain circumstances the cost of repairs or special
improvements for vessels engaged in the open seas
fishery may be defrayed by the Government up to one-
(2)
third of the total cost of such repairs or improvements.
Government Regulation.
As a counterpart to Government aid, the Japanese
whaling industry is subject to Government regulation,
under the Ministry of Forestry and Agriculture, which
supervises all the fisheries of the Empire. The
authorities have been quick to recognize the danger of
unrestrained whaling and in order to preserve the
fishery have placed the industry under a system of
lioenses. The total number of killer vessels which
are allowed to operate in Japanese waters is set at
thirty. At the present time some twenty-seven such
craft are in operation. Similarly, there is a limita-
tion on the number of whaling stations. Whaling
oompanies may not operate without licenses specifying
the

=== Page 292 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
the period during which operations may be conducted,
the areas to be worked and the types of cetaceans
(1)
which may be taken. It is understood, however, that
this supervision applies only to contiguous waters and
not to open ocean activity in the North Pacific or in
(3)
the South Pacific.
In spite of these restrictions on operations, the
number of whales in Japanese waters has been steadily
diminishing. Part of this diminution has been due to
gradual extermination and part to the fact that the
animals are moving off the coasts to the greater safety
(1)
of the open sea.
. It is likewise possible that a shift
in the supply of food, such as plankton and cuttlefish,
has had effect upon this movement.

The distribution of the whale fishery is wide,
ranging from the Kuriles in the far north, where right
whales are taken, to the semi-tropical waters around
Formosa. Sperm are found principally on the Pacific
coast of the main island off Sendai, about a hundred
miles to seaward of Kinkazan Point, although they are
sometimes encountered as far north as Hokkaido. The
wide geographical distribution of the industry is
instanced by the location of the stations of the
principal operator, the Toyo Whaling Company, compris-
ing 30 of the 38 stations under license from the
(1)
Government:

Whaling Operations.
It is not necessary to describe at great length
the methods of the Japanese whale fishery, as they were
brought over in toto from the technique of the Norwegians
and British as prinoipally developed in the Ross Sea and
the

=== Page 295 === (confidence 1.00)
-9-
the other Antarctic grounds. The small killer ships,
most of them averaging around 120 tons, slip up on the
prey while it is breaching or basking and discharge
into it a bomb harpoon. The dead whale is reeled in
by winch and either inflated with air to await the
return of the killer boat or is at once towed into
the shore station. There are at present no floating
whale stations, although recently the Toyo Whaling Co.
bought the old Norwegian factory ship ANTARCTIC and
four killers of Norwegian owners, for use in the South
(4)
Pacific.
. At the shore stations the whale is hauled up
on the beach, the carcass is flensed and the blubber
tried out. The various uses to which the products of
the whale are put will be discussed below.
Whaling Companies.
In addition to the great Toyo Whaling Company
there are four other firms in the Japanese fishery.
(1)

Production.
The number and kinds of whales caught in Japanese
waters within recent years have already been indicated.
The sole remaining question is the utilization of the
animals caught.
Whale Oil.
It is, of course, obvious from the entire history
of whaling that the principal use of the whale is as a
source of animal oil, rendered from blubber. Of the
whales oaught in Japan the best from the point of view
of the quality of the oil are the Greenland and
Southern Right whales and the sperm. The humpback,
grey and rorqual whales do not yield oil of such
richness

=== Page 297 === (confidence 0.99)
-11-
richness. That special oil tried from spemicetti
comes solely from the brain case of the sperm whale.
The uses to which whale oil is put in Japan are
manifold. It is devoted to the making of soaps,
artificial butter, candles, hardened oil, as a
substitute for vegetable oils, for lamp oil, for
machinery and leather grease, and for the special
greases used in weaving.
In l929 the total production of whale oil in
Japan amounted to ¥704,925. During the following year
the total declined to ¥517,520, and in 1931 it slumped
(5)
to ¥168,92l. Statistics on production are not available
for later years, but the data for whale oil exports
indicate that there was a considerable increase in
production from this very low figure.
Exports of
(6)
whale oil have been as follows:
Year
Value
1928
¥220,000
1929
43,000
1930
361,000
1931
146,000
1932
465,000
1933
131,000
The bulk of this exportation goes to Germany and
Great Britain, although Denmark took a large quantity
in 1932.
Baleen_or_Whalebone.
Baleen or whalebone from all the whales caught
in Japan except sperm (which have teeth in the lower
jaw) is utilized for stiffening, although its classical
contribution to the corset is no longer of importance,
least of all in Japan where corsets are unknown.
Inferior baleen is made into bone meal or fertilizer.
Meat

=== Page 298 === (confidence 0.99)
-12-
Meat.
In Japan the whale is an important source of
food. It is sold fresh, the same as beef or horse
meat, but the principal utilization is in the salted
or preserved form. The Toyo Whaling Company salts
meat which is said to keep for one year. Other whale
meat is preserved in the lees of sake or rice wine.
Skin.
Whale skin is salted the same as meat and forms
the basis for at least nine different dishes, according
to the unique "whale cook book" compiled by the Toyo
Whaling Company.
A special kind of skin called une is that along
the ventral side of the whale, from the mouth to the
genital organs. This is prized for its culinary
possibilities, both in the raw and salted state.
Flukes.
The flukes of the whale are bottled and are eaten
in the form of yubiki: strips scalded and then eaten
when cool with vinegar and bean paste.
Irikasu.
Irikasu is a sort of hardened gelatin made from
the skin and tongue of the whale after the extraction
of oil. It is first soaked in water until it becomes
soft, after which it is used as the basis of soup or
(1)
other dishes.
Ambergris:
Ambergris comes only from the sperm whale and is
generally found as the result of stomach ulceration
caused by the beaks of cuttlefish, the principal article
of

=== Page 299 === (confidence 1.00)
-13-
of diet of this species. While it is but rarely
encountered, it may be listed as one of the products
of the Japan whale fishery.
Fertilizer.
Almost as important as the varied uses of whale
by-products for food is the utilization of by-products
in the form of fertilizer. Every vestige of the whale
not devoted to the uses above described is made into
fertilizer: inferior meat, viscera, skin and bones.
There are three principal types of whale fertilizer:
bone meal, flesh fertilizers and blood fertilizers.
Steamed bone meal contains an average of 3.19%
nitrogen and 23.75% phosphate. It is principally of
value for fruit orchards. It is claimed that whale bone
meal is superior to other bone meals because it is less
soluble in water. It is said to be superior to
superphosphate on account of the fact that it does not
oxidize the soil. It is understood to be especially
effective when used in conjunction with whale flesh
(1)
fertilizers.
Whale meat meal contains an average of 1l.49%
nitrogen and 0.79% phosphate. It is used for mulberry
(1)
trees and other plants requiring nitrogen in abundance.
Whale blood fertilizer is composed of nitrogen
10.52% and phosphate 0.90%.
Its uses are similar to
(1)
those of meat meal.
According to the Toyo Whaling Company, an average
rorqual of a length of 75 feet will be utilized as
follows:
For

The writer would state his frank suspicion of the
above statistics with regard to the low proportion
TION
of "fat" (presumably blubber) and the high proportion
of edible meat. It would seem more logical if the
proportions were reversed.
Such in brief is the whale industry of Japan.
It is not a large industry, nor an important one, nor
does its production at all compare with the vast output
of the Antarctic grounds. At the same time, the whale
fishery of Japan is of interest as the principal
industry of this character in an ocean once haunted
by many whaling ships; and as the residuary legatee
of the fishery first conducted in Japanese waters by
the whalers of New England.
