DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
November 8, 1934.
Division of
CONFIDINTIAL.
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
DEC 1- 1934
The Aleutian Islands: Japanese
Espionage Activities Therein.
Department of State
94.6
During the past few years there has come to the
0
894.628
notice of the Department a number of reports indicating
that visits of Japanese vessels to the Aleutian Islands
8
have given rise to statements in American quarters that
in connection with such visits there occurs a certain
y
amount of Japanese espionage activities.
The information available to the Department indicates
that the number of Japanese vessels which visit the Aleutian
Islands is not great (though probably increasing); that the
visits of Japanese vessels do not occur with any great
frequency; that the visits are made for the declared purpose
of fishing, observing and investigating the migration route
of fur seals, and engaging in general experimental work con-
nected with fishing operations. Some of the Japanese
vessels which visit the Aleutian Islands are Japanese public
vessels. When a Japanese public vessel desires to visit
the Islands, it is customary for notice to be given this
Government through diplomatic channels with request that
117
FILED
the usual courtesies and facilities be granted. This
一
Department does not make reply to such communications from
9
the Japanese Government until there have first been con-
F
sulted the Navy Department and the Department of Commerce.
G
For

=== Page 316 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
For the past eighteen months, we have, at the instance of
the Navy Department, informed the Japanese Government that
there is no objection to Japanese public vessels visiting
ports in the Aleutian Islands where there are stationed
American officials. This is in line with the reply usually
made by the Japanese Government to requests that American
public vessels be permitted to visit ports in the Japanese
Mandated Islands. At times Japanese public vessels have
put in at ports other than those where American officials
are stationed and in such instances this Department has
called the matter to the attention of the appropriate
Japanese authorities.
In addition to Japanese public vessels, it is under-
stood that Japanese private fishing vessels also visit the
waters of the Aleutian Islands. In connection with the
visits of Japanese fishing vessels, it needs to be remembered
that Japanese fishermen are as a class of a rather truculent
and adventurous type. In connection with the visits of
Japanese public vessels, it needs to be remembered that
Japanese military and naval personnel seems to be afflicted
with almost a mania for espionage activities.
The query has been raised whether it might be desirable,
with a view to preventing Japanese espionage activities in
the Aleutian Islands, for the American Government to declare
the Aleutian Islands a closed area not open to the visits
of foreign vessels or nationals. In case the competent
military

=== Page 317 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
military and naval authorities of the United States should
be of the opinion that such action is necessary from the
point of view of national defense, there would seem to be
warrant for considering the taking of such a step. However,
insofar as Japanese espionage activities in the Aleutian
Islands are concerned, the information available to this
Department does not indicate that any such activities are
of a particularly serious character. The establishing of
the Aleutian Islands as a closed area not open to the visits
of foreign vessels or nationals would be open to objections
as follows:
1. Such action would create in Japan
ill will toward the United States and would
be interpreted in Japan as a military act
directed solely against Japan. Aside from
American vessels, Japanese vessels are the
only ones which visit the Islands;
2. Such action might be considered by
Japan as an unfriendly act not in conformity
with the provisions of the Fur Seal Treaty
of 19ll, Article VIII, which provides that
"All of the High Contracting Parties agree
to cooperate with each other in taking such
measures as may be appropriate and available
for the purpose of preventing pelagic sealing
in the prohibited area mentioned in Article I";
3. Such action might cause Japan to
seek to modify the Fur Seal Treaty of 19ll.
For a number of years there have been indica-
tions that the Japanese desire to obtain a
revision of the Treaty which this Government
has consistently desired to avoid. Abrogation
of the Treaty would result in a return to the
destructive practice of pelagic sealing, no
doubt chiefly by Japanese vessels, and the
great fur-seal herd now numbering about
1,250,000 animals would shortly be reduced to
comparatively

=== Page 318 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
comparatively insignificant proportions, probably
fewer than the 135,000 animals in 191l when the
Treaty became effective;
4. Such action by the United States would
probably influence adversely any prospect of
working out with Japan means for protecting the
Alaskan salmon industry. For several years this
Department has been in communication with the
Bureau of Fisheries in regard to this question
and a formula has just been worked out by the
competent American authorities which affords some
basis for hope that an approach may be made to
the Japanese Government and an agreement worked
out which will accord protection to this important
American industry.
MMH/REK

the Department of Commerce with a view to

attached).

TREASURY DEPARTMENT
United States Gustoms Bervice

CONFIDENTIAL
November 26, 1934.
CONFIDENTIAL
The Commissioner of Customs,
Treasury Department
Washington, D. C.
Sir:
Referring to my letter to the Bureau dated November 5, 1934,
relative to the employment of nonresident fishermen, I have the honor to
inform the Bureau that an American attorney representing certain Japanese
commercial interests has made inquiry to this office regarding a matter
which I believe to be very significant. He stated that the interests he
represents have important lobster fishing concessions in Mexican waters
along the coast of Lower California.
He asked whether there would be any objection by the Government
of the United States should the parties concerned desire to bring across
the Pacific their own fishing fleet, which it is planned would bring the
lobster catch to Los Angeles for transshipment to Japan on the regular
trans-Pacific liners. Apparently, there is no legal bar to operations of
this kind, provided that the Customs regulations and those of the Commerce
Department are observed. The attorney was so informed.
It is my personal opinion that this matter would be regarded by
the Navy and State Departments as one which would require very close
scrutiny.
Respectfully,
(Sgd. Alfred A. Cohn
Collector of Customs

=== Page 322 === (confidence 0.99)

December 21, 1934.
CONFIDENTIA
The Collector of Customs,
Los Angeles, California.
Sir:
Roference is made to your letter of November 26, 1934 (45), o∞n-
cerning an inquiry of an attorney representing Japanese commercial interesta
as to whether it is permissible for these interests to bring their own
fishing fleet across the Pacifio, with which it is planned to bring the
lobster catches to your port for transshipment to Japan on the regular
trans-Pacific liners.
There appears to be no objection so far as Customs laws and regu-
lations are concerned to the transshipment at your port of fish from Japanese
fishing vessels to regular trans-Pacific liners for shipment to Japan, and the
procedure in Article 912 of the Customs Regulations of 193l governing entry
for immediate exportation should be followed.
Copies of your letter are being sent to the Navy Department and State
Department for their information. A copy of your letter is also being sent
to the Bureau of Navigation and Steamboat Inspection so that it may advise
you as to whether the proposed action is permissible under the navigation
laws.
Respeotfully,
Commissioner of Customs.

) DATED

REGARDING: Commercial fishing in Japan. Legal provisions
restricting the exploitation of natural resources
by foreigners-.
Copy of Tel. attached.
2
IN
Jw

=== Page 341 === (confidence 1.00)
FE
GRAY
MP
Tokyo
Dated March 29, 1935
Rec'd 12:05 a.m.
Secretary of State,
Washington.
68, March 29, 10 a.m.
Department's 42, March 10, 8 p.m.
Aside from the mining law there is no specific
legislation restricting the exploitation of natural
resources by foreigners. The benefits of subsidies
are restricted by law in each case to Japanese subjects.
There are a number of administrative policies which in
practice would probably prevent foreign exploitation
of resources. In the case of water power rights an
628
application by a foreigner to dam streams and build
power plants would probably be rejected, although there
is no law which forbids foreigners to go into the
business. A number of foreigners have been interested
in these enterprises as investors, but the authorities
would very likely insist that the concession be issued
in the name of a Japanese. The same applies to com-
mercial fishing, which requires a license. It is
extremely improbable that an application by foreign
interests

=== Page 342 === (confidence 0.98)
MP
2-#68 From Toky0, March 29, 10 a.m.
interests would be considered favorably, despite the
absence of legislation on the subject.
Agriculture is not restricted to Japanese.
There are a number of foreigners, mostly mission or
missionary institutions, who conduct farmin: enterprises
without interference. There is no legislation, regu-
lation or restrictive policy in vogue in this matter.
It would probably be impossible to import labor to
operate an agricultural establishment, although there
is no specific legislation on the subject. Immigration
is controlled not by law but by administrative ruling
of the Home Department, by which the police keep out
persons whose presence is considered undesirable. As
habeas corpus proceedings are unknown in Japan there
is no way to interfere with administrative rulings in
this connection.
GREY
HPD

My dear Mr. Secretary:
894.623/124
The receipt is acknowledged of the Department of State's letter of
March 30, 1935, file No. FE 894.628/122, enclosing a copy of dispatch No.
1190 dated March 4, 1935, from the American Ambassador at Tokyo in regard
to the advisability of negotiating a treaty with the Japanese Government
to prohibit Japanese nationals from salmon fishery operations in Bering
Sea and Bristol Bay east of a line from Cape Avinof to Cape Sarichef,
Alaska.
It is noted that the Japanese Government is preoccupied with forth-
coming negotiations with the Soviet Government in regard to fishing by
Japanese nationals in Soviet territorial vaters, and that the American
Ambassador therefore recommends no approach in regard to the regulation
of fishing operations by Japanese nationals in the waters of Bering Sea
and Bristol Bay until the end of the early summer or early autumn of this
year, when in all probability the new Japanese Fishery Control law will
be in operation and current fishery difficulties with the Soviet Govern-
ment will be concluded.
The Department of Commerce has the highest regard for the opinion of
the American Ambassador at Tokyo and would not suggest any overruling of
his recommendation in the matter. In view, however, of the importence of
the situation and its effect upon the American salmon fishing industry, it
is the opinion of this Department that we should enter into such a treaty
with the least possible delay consistent with diplomatic considerations.
It is observed from the dispatch of the Ambassador at Tokyo that
recent improvements in technique have made it possible for Japanese con-
JUN 10 1935
cerns to engage profitably in deep-sea salmon fishing. It is noted that
FILED
one effect of this has been serious interference with salmon runs in
Siberian rivers where Japanese interests have been operating.
Notwithstanding assurances conveyed by the note that the Japanese,
as far as can be learned, are not interested in operating floating canner-
ios for salmon off the Alaskan coast, and that the floating canneries en-
gaged in handling crabs and fish meal will not be equipped to take salmon,
almon fishery.
this Department realizes the potential danger
TREATY DIVISIUN
MAY 2 - 1935
1.0
G
DEPARTMENT OF STATE

=== Page 344 === (confidence 0.99)
343
- 2 -
The undesirable situation which has grown up on the Siberian
coast might be duplicated on the Alaskan coast, particularly in the
waters of Bering Sea and Bristol Bay eastward of a line from Cape
Avinof to Cape Sarichef. This is especially true in view of the
improvements which enable Japanese operators to fish successfully
in deep-sea waters. Therefore, it is hoped that as prompt action
as possible may be taken to initiate a treaty that will protect
American interests.
Sincerely yours,
Secretary of Commerce

Sir:
Roference is made to your despatch No. ll90, dated
March 4, 1935, in regard to the advisability of the Japanese
Government being approached with a view to there being con-
2
cluded between the United States and Japan a treaty to regulate 4
salmon fishing in certain waters of Bering Sea and Bristol
Bay. The Department made available to the Department of
Commerce a copy of your despatch under reference, and there
is enclosed a copy of a letter, dated April 16, 1935, from
that Department, in which there are set forth circumstances
which, it is believed, make it important that a treaty such
as that under contemplation be entered into with the least
possible delay consistent with diplomatic considerations.
The Department shares your view that the Japanese Govern-
ment should be approached informally, that it should be in-
formed of the desires of this Covernment in connection with
the preservation of the fishery resources of Alaska, and that
the Japanese Government should be asked to indicate whether
it would be prepared to cooperate in the matter.
The

=== Page 348 === (confidence 0.98)
347
● 2 ●
The Department associates itself with the Embassy in the view
that the results of projected negotiations between the Jape-
nese and Soviet Government in regard to fishing by Japanese
nationals in Soviet waters would have important bearing on
the attitude which the Japanese Goverrment may take in the
matter of salmon fishing off the coast of Alaska, and it
believes, therefore, that no approach should be made to that
government until there has been presented to the Japanese
and Soviet Goverrments reasonable opportunity to conclude
between them an agreement.
The Department does not, however, concur with the
Embassy in the view that an approach to the Japanese Govern-
ment should await the enactment and enforcement of legislation
by Japan to regulate fishing in northern waters by Japanese
ationals. It is realized that the enactment of such legis-
lation would place the Japanese Government in relatively
favorable position to cooperate, if it should so desire,
with the American Government to preserve the fishery resourees
of Alaska. In view of the fact that the Japanese Government
was reported in l951 by the Embassy to be favorably disposed
toward cooperative regulation of salmon fishing off the coast
of Alaske and of the fact that operations by Japanese would
seriously affeot the American salmon fishing industry, it is
requested that the Embassy attentively follow the fishery
negotiations between the Japanese and Soviet Governments, and
that the Embassy report to the Department as soon as the
bemey believes that it would be epportume, in the light of

=== Page 349 === (confidence 0.94)
348
the views set forth in the present instruction and in the
enclosure thereto, to approach the Japanese Government.
Very truly yours,

that Government in keeping in hand those
who would fish off Alaska. As you know,
Japanese fishing vessels are required to
obtain a license, and it would be practica-

of iicenses, for the Japanese Government
to insure the observance by its nationals
of the contemplated United States-Japan

I have the honor to report that among the im-
portant bills introduced by the Government before the
Diet which were not passed was the "Law for the Control
of Fishery in Northern Waters".* Although the House of
Peers passed this bill, it was not acted upon by the
Lower House before the regular session of the Diet ended.
In
*Embassy's despatch No. 1190 bf March 4, 1935.
894.628/122

=== Page 352 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
In this connection the NICHI NICHI reported on
April 1 that Mr. Ohta, the Japanese Ambassador to the
USSR who is now on leave in Tokyo, will return to his
post on April 1o with instructions to take up the
matter of the revision of the Russo-Japanese Fishery
Convention of 1928 when he arrives in Moscow. It is
rumored that even before his arrival the subject will
be broached by the Japanese Chargé d'Affaires.
It will be recalled that the Japanese Government
had undertaken two general steps in anticipation of
these negotiations, first, the amalgamation of the
northern fishing interests and, second, the bill which
has just failed of passage. The importance of the
fishery bill lies in the fact that it would legalize
strict regulation of certain irresponsible fishing
concerns which have tended to endanger the coastal
fisheries (as opposed to the off shore fisheries) and
to divide the Japanese interests. The situation was
confused and, it was thought, would handicap Japan in
negotiating with the USSR.
Despite the failure of the bill it is widely
believed in Tokyo that the authorities are prepared to
take strong measures against the undesirable elements
in the fishing industry. The unfortunate aspect of
the situation is that it tends to prolong indefinitely
the confused state of affairs which produced violent
disagreements

=== Page 353 === (confidence 0.94)
-3-
disagreements and uncertainty among the Japanese
concerned with this industry.
Respectfully yours,
Noseb
Joseph C. Grew.

Sran
May 9, 1935.
I telephoned Dr. Bowers of the Bureau
of Fisheries and stated that we did not feel
quite sure that we understood the purport of
the second paragraph of Commerce's letter of
May 7, 1935, attached. I said that our view,
as expressed to the Embassy at Tokyo, was as
follows:
The Embassy should follow closely the
Soviet-Japanese negotiations on fish-
eries, and should report as soon as
the negotiations had been developed to
a point'whieh created a favorable
opportunity for this Government to
approach the Japanese Government in
regard to Alaskan fisheries. We did
not, however, feel that such approach
should be withheld until the Japanese
had enacted their bill for the control
of fisheries in the North Pacific, for
the reason that the Japanese Government
had at present sufficient power to
regulate the fishing operations of
their nationals on the high seas, as
well as in Japanese waters.
Dr. Bowers stated that the above expres-
sed his conception of this Department's view,
with which Commerce is in entire accord. It
was, therefore, agreed that the letter
attached required no action by us.

now preocoupied with the Russian fishery question, an early
settlement of the matter could not be anticipated.
It would be appreciated if the Department of Commerce
could at its early convenience indicate to this Department
its views in regard to the character of the reply which
should be made to the Ambassador.
Sinoerely yours,
Enclosure:
Cordell Hull

Sir:
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
I have the honor to refer to this Eabassy's despatch
127
No. 1298 of May 14, 1935, in regard to Japanese pelagic
1/
.fishing in Bering Sea, and to enclose a memorandum of
JAN 14 1936
conversation which the Counselor of the Embassy had with
the Chief of the European Bureau of the Foreign Office.
The memorandum indicates that the Foreign Office is oc-
cupied with the Russian fishery question and would not,
at the moment, be able to take up effectively the making
of a fishery agreement with the United States.
According to newspaper and other reports which seem
to be authentic, the Japanese hoped to confine the dis-
cussion

=== Page 362 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
cussion to such items as they wished altered, particularly
questions growing out of the auction of fishery lots. The
Japanese feel that the auctions are a farce because there
is no private enterprise in Russia and the local people
who bid at the sales are nothing but creatures of the
Soviet Government. They wish to pay a fixed sum over a
period of years and do away with the auctions. The
Russians, however, were reported to be unwilling to ne-
gotiate until the Japanese gave notice that they would
denounce the treaty. This has now been done, which means
opening up the whole fishery question. This is apparently
what Mr. Togo had in mind when he said that there was a
year in which negotiations could be carried on.
I shall continue to watch the situation and report
to the Department. As soon as there appears to be an
easing of the present tension in the Russian negotiations
it may be worth our while to ap roach the Japanese. I do
not believe it would expedite matters to do so before that
time.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

=== Page 363 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to despatch
No. 1318 of May 29, 1935 from the
Embassy at Tokyo.
Conversation
May 21, 1935.
Mr. Neville and Mr. Togo,
Chief of the European Bureau
of the Foreign office.
Subject:
Bering Sea Fisheries.
I called on Mr. Togo at 3:00 P.M. I thanked him
for his letter giving us information as to the vessels
proceeding to Bering Sea for the purpose of taking crabs
and fish. I asked him whether the information we had
received that these ships were not equipped to take salmon
was correct. He said that it was. He said that these ships
would not be nearer than twenty miles or so to the Alaska
coast. I said that I should appreciate learning more about
the places in which the vessels going to Bering Sea would
operate, and he promised to get the information as soon as
he could.
I then asked him what proress was being made with the
Soviet fishery negotiations. He smiled and said that the
preliminaries had begun; that the present treaty had a year
to run, during which time negotiations could be carried on.
I asked him how long he thought it woulā take, to which he
replied by reminding me how long the railway negotiations in
Manchuria had taken.
After some discussion of the defunct fishery law, Mr.
Togo said that the authorities here realized our interest in
Japanese fisheries in Bering Sea and our fear that open sea
operations might affect the shore canneries. He said there
was no fear of it at present. I said I should appreciate
going over the whole situation with him at his leisure with
officials of the Fishery Bureau. He said that he would be
glad to do so, but he could not promise to do much in that
direction while the Kamchatka fisheries are unsettled.
From

=== Page 364 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
From his manner I gathered that both he and the
fishery officials are pretty well occupied, and would
not welcome additional problems just now. He also gave
me the impression that the alterations desired in the
Soviet fishery treaty are not extensive, but that he did
not anticipate a speedy settlement.
E L N.

In a memorandum left by Mr. Dooman, before departing

send an expert to Tokyo, my recommendation would be to t ele-

The Department's letter to the Secretary of Commerce

of course seems advisable to have the necessary arrangements
completed in advance.
The question of negotiating a fishery convention with
Japan has been pending several years, and has grown out of
the wishes of the Department of Commerce to secure adequate
protection of the Alaskan salmon industry. Naval Intelligence
reports, and reports reaching the Department from other
sources, indicate not infrequent approaches of Japanese
fishing craft to this area. The Japanese Government has
cooperated

=== Page 371 === (confidence 0.95)
- 3 -
cooperated informally to prevent Japanese fishing vessels
from interferring with the Alaskan salmon industry, but as
protection by this means is not expected to be procurable
indefinitely, the Departments of State and Commerce have
long had under consideration the conclusion of a formal
agreement. It has seemed highly advisable to negotiate
an agreement before the Japanese will have made substantial
investments in the Alaskan fishing industry. As to
advantages of a convention, past consideration of the
matter, and correspondence on file, make it appear that
American interests distinctly have more to gain than do
the Japanese.

My dear Mr. Secretary:
The receipt is acknowledged of your letters of June 7, June 24, and July
12, 1935, file FE 894.628/129, together with enclosures mentioned in the first
two named communications, in regard to the matter of the conclusion of a treaty
between the United States and Japan, which would prohibit Japanese nationals
from engaging in fishing operations in certain waters of Bering Sea and Bristol
Bay off the coast of Alaska.
If not inconsistent with diplomatic and other considerations, the Depart-
ment of Commerce suggests favorable action upon the expression of the Ambassador
that an informal approach be made to the Japanese Foreign Office in Tokyo, as
soon as possible, concerning the wishes of the American Government in regard to
activities of Japanese fishing interests in the waters of Bering Sea eastward
of a line from Cape Avinof to Cape Sarichef, Alaska.
In any such presentation of the matter, it is this Department's belief
that reference to American wishes should be confined to the subject of preser-
vation of the salmon fisheries. There are no other extensive fishery opera-
tions in the waters of the area specified, nor are there likely to be any such
Cv
operations of particular value to American interests that would be harmed by
Japanese activities.
It is further suggested that in any approach in this matter made by the
Embassy at Tokyo, it would be appropriate to indicate, with due discretion,
readiness on our part to make some concessions in re ard to crab fishing in
the waters of this area, recalling in this connection informal suggestions
made several years ago by Japanese crab-canning interests.
In regard to the desire of the State Department that adequate technical
assistance be made available to the Ambassador at Tokyo, so that occasions of
reference to Washington for decision may be reduced as far as practicable, I
wish to say that when conversations regarding the treaty have progressed to
the extent that the services of one of our experts is needed in Tokyo, this
Department will be glad to have Mr. Ward T. Bower, Chief of the Division of
Alaska Fisheries of the Bureau of Fisheries, who has had many years of experi-
ence regarding the fisheries of Alaska and who has been in Japan and along the
Siberian Coast, proceed to Japan to render assistance in regard to the propoed
F/FC
Alaska fishery convention.
The Department of Commerce wishes to renew expressions of appreciation of
G
the active interest taken by the Department of State in this matter.
Sincerely youra
Secretary of Commerce.

-2 -
Sea eastward of a line from Cape Avinof to Cape Sarichef,
Alaska.
During the course of my conversation with the Vice
Minister for Foreign Affairs on the subject I received
the impression that he was interested in the matter and
that the authorities concerned would probably recommend
an affirmative reply. The Embassy will not fail to
communicate the reply by cable as soon as it is re-
ceived.

telegram under reference, I assume that the government

No. 1425 of August 9, 1935, from the
Embassy at Tokyo.
C O P Y
Conversation
June 14, 1935.
Mr. Neville and Mr. F.S. Booth
of the Nichiro Fisheries Company.
Fisheries.
I met Mr. Booth, whom I have known for a number
of years, at lunch at the Tokyo Club. I asked him
how serious a menace he thought the off shore fish-
ing of salmon was likely to prove for the shore
fisheries. He said that the situation was very com-
plicated, so far as Kamchatka was concerned, but in
his judgment, the nuisance value was greater than the
profit. I asked him what he meant by that statement.
He said that there are now two kinds of pelagic fish-
ing for salmon. One consists of small boats that
operate with the Kurile Islands as their base, and
bring their catch to canneries there. These ships have
a sort of mother ship that receives and ices the fish
that are caught, and then makes off with a full cargo
to the canneries in Japan. They cannot work too far
from their base, or the fish will spoil if kept too long.
The other type consists of floating canneries. The ship
sets out to the northern seas with motor boats in at-
tendance, and cans the fish that are gathered by the
small boats. They all operate off the Kamchatka coast
with gill nets. These are floating nets that drift
with

=== Page 388 === (confidence 0.99)
344
-2 -
with the tides or currents, and as salmon always swim
against the current, they are caught in the drifting
nets.
He then told me that the Nichiro had absorbed
the open sea fisheries, and it was his judgment that
when the fishery negotiations with Russia were over,
the company would allow the floating canneries to die
out; that is, as the vessels become too old to operate
they would not be replaced. I asked him why. He said
that, compared with land stations, they are too expen-
sive to operate.
I then asked him about crab fishing in Bering Sea.
He said that it really was not profitable, but as the
fishing companies had a number of ves els that could
be used for the purpose, it was better to put them to
work than to have them idle. He said that the London
market for crab meat had held up well, which made it
possible to continue the crab fishing. I asked him
whether vessels equipped to take crab could interfere
with the salmon runs. He said they could not; that
salmon is a surface fish. He then told me that there
was, in his judgment, absolutely no probability of
Japanese taking salmon in Alaska waters. The Japanese
Government objects to it, and besides, said Mr. Booth,
there would be no profit in so long a voyage because
the catch would have to be very large and the market
very good to warrant it. Nichiro, which is controlled
by Mitsubishi, would never countenance trying to establish
a

=== Page 389 === (confidence 0.96)
65
-3 -
a nuisance value, because their trade relations with
the United States are too important. He said that
salmon packing would have to become much more profitable
than it is before floating canneries would be sent to
take Alaska salmon.
E.L.N.

Kemorandum for the Commissioner
of Fisheries:
The Bureau has given careful constderation to your memoran-
91.628
dum of the 9th instant in regard to the entry into and clearance
from ports of the United States of foreign vessels engaged in
fishery operation on the high seas.
Replying to question mumber 1, if any foreign vessel on en-
tering our ports presents a proper clearance from a foreign port.
there is no navigation law which would prevent her enterin: with
a cargo of fish taken on the high seas and landing: the same, the
lending of the cargo being subjeot of course to the requirements
of the customs laws.
Answering question mumber 2, there is no provision of statute
for elearing a vessel either foreign or American for the "high
seas". Sections 4197-4201 R.S. preseribe the procedure for clear
ing a vessel to a foreim port. The procedure for "permitting"
a foreign vessel to proceed coastwise from one United States port
C
to another is found in sections 4367-4369 2.5. and sect1ons 442
446 of the Tariff Act of 1930. Thore 1s no provision in our law
4
for granting a permit to a vessel to proceed from a United States
port to the "high sers" and return to the seme port of clearance.
The permits authorized by law contemplate either a designated
foreign port or another desi mated domestic port as the destina-
tion.
As you are aware, the "high seas" are not foreign within the
meaning of our navigation laws. As stated by Justice  tory in
Taber et al, vs United States, ederal Case no.13722. "he ocean
1s deemed the common highway of all nations and foreign to none".
There is likewise no provision ir he law for entering a
vessel at a United States port from t "high seas". Vesgels
are entered from a "foreign port or place": sections h33-136
Tariff lot of 1930.
Answering question mmber 3. we have no law which would pre-
vent an American vespel from receiving and transporting ashore.
the output of a floating plant stationed on the "high seas". You
are of course familiar with the provisions of seotion 43ll ..
F/F
which reads as follows!
"Vessels of twenty tons and uxard, enrolled in pur-
muance of this Title (R.s. 4311 to 4390: U8c title
G
46, se0s.251 to 336), and having e l1conse in force.

=== Page 392 === (confidence 0.97)
or veesels of less than twenty tons, which.
although not onrolled, have & license in
foree, as required by this Title, and no
others, shall be deemed vessels of the United
States ontitled to the privileges of vessels
employed in the coasting trade or fisheries."
You also are aware that there is no ponalty for violation
of section 4311 . S.

One. I have just been orally informed by
the Foreign Office that the Japanese Government
would prefer not to enter into negotiations on
the question of salmon packing and crab fishing
35
at the present time. The reasons given are as
follows:
(a) Japanese fishing interests in the Bristol
Bay region are limited. The crab fishing is of
no great importance although it does provide a
livelihood to some of the fishing population.
At present there are no Japanese authorizod to
engage in salmon fishing in the open sea anywhere
except around Kamchatka. It appears unlikely that
Japanese interests in this industry will develop
to an extent that would be detrimontal to
American interosts and that consequently thore
seems

treaty as the matters involved have too little immediate
value.
(b). The Japanese Government would in any event
prefer to reserve its liberty of action in regard to an
area as large as that indicated and while the authorities
have no present intention to authorize Japanese floating
canneries to take salmon in those waters the Government
would prefer not to enter into negotiations for a fishery
treaty which present conditicns do not seem to require.
Two. I replied that my Government would be dis-
appointed and inquired whether the compensation offered
seemed inadequate; that 1s, whether they felt that salmon
fishing had greater possibilities for development than
crab fishing. I asked whether there were any possibility
of the authorities reconsidering their decision; that my
Government regarded the question of open sea salmon fish-
ing very seriously; that our land canneries represented
a large investment which might well be impaired by open
sea salmon fishing; and that finally it needed little
imagination to picture the situation which might be
created if Japanese fishing vessels came to be regarded

to prevent such a situation arising by taking suitable

A telegram (No. 208) of November 12, 1935, from the
Ameriean Chargé d'Affaires ad interim at Tokyo, reads
substantially as follows:
One. The Foreign Office has just informed me orally
that the Japanese Govermment would prefer not to enter at
the present time into negotiations in regard to orab fish-
ing and salmon packing. The following reasons were given:
(a) There are no extensive Japanese fishing
interests in Bristol Bay. Although crab fishing provides
a livelihood to some fishermen, it is of no great
importance. There are no Japanese licensed at present for
salmon fishing in the open sea anywhere except in the
vicinity of Kamohatka. It does not seem liksly that
Japanese interests in salmon fiahing will so develop as
to be detrimental to American interests, and therefore
there does not appear to be adequate reason for the
negotiation of a treaty, the matters involved not having
sufficient immediate importance.
(b) In any event the Japanese Government prefers
to reserve its liberty of action in any area as large as
that under reference. Although the Japanese authorities
have no intention at the present time to permit Japanese
floating sonneries to pask salmon in that ares, the
Japanese Government prefers not to enter into negotiatioms
im regard to a fishery treaty not required by existing
conditions.

ay Government; that open sea salmon fishing might well

MR. SAYRE
Tokyo Embassy's No. 1558, November 20,
1935:
This despatch confirms the Embassy's
telegram No. 208, November 12, 1935, stating
that the Japanese authorities are reluctant
to enter into discussion of a convention
whereby Japanese would be restrained from
engaging in salmon packing off the coast
of Alaska.
Although the Foreign Office seems
disinclined to enter into commitments be-
fore the situation shows definite need for
action, Mr. Neville thinks that the
authorities intend to continue to refuse
applications for open sea salmon fishing
in the eastern part of the Bering Sea.
Mr. Neville thinks that if after a brief
interval we press the matter further the
Japanese authorities may be willing to
reconsider their decision.
It will be recalled that on November 27
Mr. Sayre discussed this matter with the
Japanese Ambassador and requested that the
Ambassador lay before his Government the
benefits

=== Page 409 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
benefits which would result from the con-
clusion of a treaty to regulate fishing
by Japanese on the high seas off the coast
of Alaska. The Ambassador agreed to
submit the matter to his home authorities
in the effort to make them "think" some
more about it.

said the authorities did not like to idea of entering into
commitments before the situation showed definite need for
action.
I left with the impression that the authorities in-
tended to continue to refuse aplications for open sea
salmon fishing by floating canneries in the eastern part
of the Bering Sea but that if the pressure for permits
should become too strong they would then notify us. It
is possible that if after a brief interval we press the
matter further the Japenese authorities may be willing to
reconsider their decision. For the immediate present, how-
ever, there appears nothin more that can be done.
1/
A memorandum of my conversation with Mr. Horinouchi
on November 12 is enclosed herewith.
Respectfully yours,

CONVERSATION HELD ON NOVE.BER 12, 1935
BETWEEN
MR. NEVILLE AND LR. HORINOUCHI, CHIEF
OF THE ALERICA BUREAU OF THE FOREIGN
OFFICE.
Salmon Fishing in the Bristol Bay Region.
I called on IIr. Horinouchi at ll A.. I told him
that I had called to ask him whether he could cive me
some indication of the Fisher Bureau's views on the
proposal to restrict prospective open sea salmon fishing
by Japanese interests in the Bristol Bay region. He told
me that he had just been in communication with the Depart-
ment of Agriculture and Forestry and that their reply
had been that they would prefer not to enter into nego-
tiations on this subject at the present time. The reasons
civen for this decision were two:
1. Japanese fishing interests in the Bristol Bay
region are limited. The crab fishing there and even else-
where is of no great importance to Japan, although it does
provide a livelihood to some fishing population. Still,
it has relatively limited possibilities in the way of
future development, and cannot be considered as of suffi-
cient importance to be the subject of treaty negotiations.
At the present time there are no Japanese authorized to
engage in fishing salmon in the open sea anywhere but
in the seas around Kamchatka. It seems unlikely that
Japanese interests in this industry far away from Japan
will develop to any considerable extent, certainly not to
an

=== Page 414 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
an extent that would be detrimentel to American interests,
and that consequently there seems to be insufficient reason
for negotiating a treaty, as the matters involved have too
little immediate value.
2. In addition to the reasons given above, the
Japanese Government would prefer to retain its liberty
of action in regard to an area so large as that indicated.
It is several hundred miles from Cape Avenof to Cape
Sarichef, and while the Japanese authorities concerned
have no present intention to authorize Japanese floating
canneries to take salmon in those waters, the Government
would prefer to enter upon no negotiations for a fishery
treaty which conditions just now do not seem to call for.
I told Mr. Horinouci that my Government would be
disappointed. I asked him whether the decision was caused
by the belief that the compensation offered was inade-
quate - that salmon fishing had greater possibilities for
development than crab fishing. He said that while that
may have entered into the picture, he thought that it
was a definite decision on its merits; that the subject .
was not of enough importance from the Japanese point of
view (the point of view of the Department of Agriculture
and Forestry) to be the subject of treaty negotiations,
and they would prefer to retain their liberty of action
in regard to granting or refusing licenses for open
water salmon fishing over so large an area.
I then asked Mr. Horinouchi if there was any
possibility of the authorities' reconsidering their
decision

=== Page 415 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
decision. I said that while I was not authorized to
speak, I felt sure that my Government would be glad to
listen to any counter proposal which the Japanese might
care to make; that my Government regarded the possibility
of open salmon fishing very seriously; that our land
canneries represented a large investment which might
well be impaired by open sea salmon fishing, and that
finally it needed no great intellect to picture the
situation which might be created if Japanese fishing
vessels came to be regarced as a menace to American
shore canneries, and that we desire to prevent diffi-
culties arising by taking suitable measures beforehand.
He said that he fully undcrstood my point of view; that
he would take the matter up again, and ask the fishery
authorities to reexamine it. He then said that there
had been two or three applications for licenses to fish
for salmon in the Bering
Sea area which the Fishery
Bureau had, by direction of the Iinister of Agriculture
and Forestry, refused to grant.
He said that they had
told him that they would consult the Foreign Office
before granting any such licenses; that he did not
believe there was any present intention to permit that
type of fishing by Japanese vessels in waters adjacent
to Alaska, but the authorities preferred to make no
definite commitments in the matter. He said that there
seemed no probability that Japanese would be engaged in
such fishing, but the authoritics do not like the idea
of making international commitments before the si tuation
shows definite need for action.
Presumably

=== Page 416 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
Presumably what we may count on is that the authori-
ties will refuse applications for open sea salmon fishing
by floating canneries in the Eastern part of the Bering
Sea for the time being; and that they will notify us if
the pressure for permits becomes too strong. I cannot get
more than this at the present time, but they may come for-
ward with some scheme of their own, depending on the
amount of rersistence they meet.

=== Page 418 === (confidence 0.95)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
of State
19355C 4 PM 3 49
MR. SAYRE
PROPOSED CONVENTION WITH JAPAN TO REGULATE
FAR EASTIRRA
FISHING BY JARANTSE HN SBRISTOL BAY, ALASKA
ANDDS
In 1928, it was noticed that there was an increasing
number of Japanese fishing vessels operating in the non-
territorial waters of Bristol Bay, off the coast of
Alaska. The apparent growing interest of the Japanese in
894.628
this area created considerable apprehension among the
American fishing interests in Alaska, particularly among
salmon fishers and salmon canners. The common Alaska
salmon is the red salmon, which is the most valuable of
/1
the salmon family. This type of salmon breeds almost
3
8
entirely in the rivers flowing into Bristol Bay. At the
end of the second year the fish leave fresh water and
Weot
migrate to waters off the coast of the continental United
states, and at the end of the fifth year, the fish return
to Bristol Bay to spawn in the rivers, the salmon running
season being from June 25 to the end of July. It has been
established that the fish return to Bristol Bay through a
passage between two of the Aleutian Islands known as
FN 14136
Unimak Pass. It has been stated by experts of the Bureau
CET
of Fisheries of the Department of Commerce that intensive
fishing operations, during the period above mentioned, in
open waters in the vicinity of Unimak Pass over a period
of
G

=== Page 419 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
of a few years would result in the complete destruction
of the Alaska red salmon.fDuring the past seven or eight
years, Japanese vessels equipped to pack the giant crab
which is also to be found in Bristol Bay have been operating
in this area. In 1930, there were informal conversations
in Tokyo between representatives of the Japanese crab
canners, representatives of the Japanese Government and
officers of the American Embassy in regard to the
possibility of making some arrangement whereby the Japanese
Government would undertake to forbid Japanese fishermen
from fishing for salmon in Bristol Bay in return for the
American Government lowering the tariff on canned crab,
the entire arrangement to be conditional upon the formulation
of a working agreement between Japanese crab packers and
American distributors of canned crab (the United States
being the most important market for Japanese canned crab).
This arrangement fell through, due to doubt whether it
would be practicable.
In the summer of 1934, Mr. Roper, the Secretary of
Commerce, visited Alaska and was strongly impressed by
the increasing apprehension felt by American salmon
interests in Alaska in regard to the activities of Japanese
fishermen. Upon Mr. Roper's return to Washington, the
Department of Commerce requested that the Department of
State enter into negotiation with the Japanese Government
looking toward the conclusion of a treaty whereby Japanese
nationals

=== Page 420 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
would be prohibited from fishing for salmon in that part
of Bristol Bay lying east of a line drawn from Cape Avinof
in Alaska to Cape Sarichef, Unimak Island, which is one
of the Aleutian Islands. The Department replied that it
would be prepared to propose to the Japanese Government
the conclusion of such a convention if something in the
way of compensation could be offered by this Government
to the Japanese Government for renouncing its right to
permit its nationals to fish in what ere open waters.
The matter was referred to the Tariff Conmission, which,
after completing a study of the question, concluded
that duties on canned crab and certain other types of
sea products could be reduced without American interests
being adversely affected. Accordingly, on August 3, 1935,
the Department instructed the American Embassy at Tokyo
to inquire whether the Japanese Government would be
prepared to enter into negotiations with a view to
the conclusion of a convention whereby Japanese nationals
would refrain from engaging in salmon packing in the waters
Embassy reported to the Department that the Japanese
Government expressed reluctance at this time to enter into
such negotiations. The report indicated that the Japanese
Government is not permitting Japanese nationals to fish
for salmon in the Bering Sea and that it has no present
intention of authorizing Japanese to take salmon in those
waters.

=== Page 421 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
waters. The opinion was expressed by the Embassy that,
if the Japanese Government were sufficiently pressed, it
might present a counter proposal. The substance of the
135
Embassy's report of November 12 was communicated to the
Department of Commerce, and that Department, in its
13L
letter of November 19, 1935,/expresses the hope that
"the Department of State will find it consistent to again
approach the Japanese Government on this subject".
It is to be emphasized that the principal consideration
which makes desirable the conclusion at this time of the
proposed convention is not the existence of salmon packing
by Japanese in Bristol Bay, but the fear that Japanese may
at any time operate in those waters -- it is the fear that
Japanese may operate in an improvident manner and thus
destroy a valuable natural resource and thus render
valueless the large investment of American capital in
salmon canneries and other equipment, to say nothing of
depriving a large number of American fishermen of a live-
lihood. These circumstances make the Alaska salmon situa-
tion analogous to the various trade problems arising out
of Japanese imports which the American and Japanese Govern-
ments have been endeavoring to adjust by means of "gentle-
men's agreements". In the case of most of these trade
problems, the principal factor has been the fear of
increased

=== Page 422 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
increased importations from Japan at low prices, rather
than the quantities actually imported. The Japanese
Government on several occasions requested that this
Government consult the Japanese Government before taking
any unilateral action designed to restrict imports from
Japan, and it is on the basis of these requests that
this Government has proposed the conclusion of the various
gentlemen's agreements that have already been entered
into.
It is recommended that a new approach be made to
the Japanese Government through the Japanese Ambassador
at Washington along the lines above suggested. The
Japanese Ambassador might be informed briefly in regard
to the salmon situation, the analogy as above suggested
between the salmon situation and the various trade
problems covered by gentlemen's agreements might be
emphasized, and in conclusion the Ambassador might be
requested to urge upon his Government the desirability
of entering into an arrangement with this Government
whereby a source of apprehension to an important
American industry would be permanently removed.
ZHD/DLY

=== Page 450 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to despatch
No. 1764 dated April 4, 1936
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
Conversation
March 30, 1936.
Mr. Neville and
Mr. Horinouchi.
I called on Mr. Horinouchi about 1l:30 and invited
his attention to the reports which had reached the American
Government that Japanese vessels have obtained licenses
for salmon fishing in waters adjacent to Alaska and are
now engaged in such fishing. I told him that my Govern-
ment believed that the circulation of such reports might
most effectively be discouraged by making known to Alaskan
salmon fishery interests the assurances given last year
by the Japanese Government. (Department's telegram No. 37,
March 28, 3 p.m.).
Mr. Horinouchi said that he did not believe any li-
censes had been issued or that Japanese vessels were en-
gaged in open water salmon fishing in the Bering Sea. To
begin with, the Fishery Bureau had promised to inform the
Foreign Office before issuing licenses of this character,
and in the second place, from all that he knew of northern
waters, it seemed improbable that Japanese fishermen would
be operating there at this time of year. He promised,he
said as a matter of form, to make inquiry of the Fishery
Bureau and to inform me of their reply, which he said he
was quite sure would be to the effect that no licenses
have been issued or are con templated.

=== Page 451 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2 to despatch
No. 1764 dated April 4, 1936,
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
Conversation
March 31, 1936.
Mr. Neville and
Mr. Horinouchi.
Mr. Horinouchi called me on the telephone and
stated that the Fisheries Bureau had just informed
him that no licenses to take salmon in waters adjacent
to Alaska had been issued to Japanese fishing in terests,
nor is such action contemplated at present, and that the
Japanese Government has no knowledge of any Japanese
fishing vessels in the Bering Sea at this time. I
asked him if there was objection to making this informa-
tion available to interested persons. He said we were
at liberty to do so.

forth in the story prepered by Mr. Cray. In any event, it would need
some revision or verification es to the proposal that the Japanese are
going into the salmon fishing business in these waters.

activities. I wish to assure you our Government is keenly alive to the
situation and there has been extensive corres, ndence, through official
channels. with the Japanese Government. The metter is not dead and buried
by any manner of means so far as we ar concernei.  are not siving any
publicity to exchanges between the two governments concerned; to do so
might defeat or interfere with our objectives.

Mr. Frank T. Bell,
U. S. Commissioner of Fisher es,
Washington, .. C.
Dear Ar. Bell:
With reference to the letter addressed to you arch 17 by
S. H. Gray. oditor of PACIFIC FIsH RMAN, you may be nterested
in a history of the Jupanese relations in the fisheries of the
Pacific Coast based on experiencesof the writer for more than
thirty years:
In 1905 the late J. T. Barron, then operating the Thlinket
Fackin: Company, came to my office with a deleg tion of fisher-
mon with the information thet two Japanese schooners hed entered
Funter Bay, erected camps on shore, and were engaged in cutch-
ing and salting salmon. Mr. Barron an: associates wanted to
know what ought to b done about it. e ot up a memoriul
addressed to the Department of Commerce and the Deportment of
State reciting the facts. The then president was Theodore
Roosevelt, who wasted no time in note writing, but sent up a
gunboat. The Jap vessels "ere seized, the camps on shore
burned, nd the crews deported back to Japen.
As a sequel to this incident e reguletion sas promulgated,
presumably prompted by the.. ureau of isheries, that
aliens ineligible to citizens: ip ahould not be allowed to fish
in the territorial waters of lask..
We followed this with legislat ion enucted by the states of
Oregon and Nashington identical in charucter and intent as
the Alaska regulation. There were no commerciel fisheries of
importance in California at the time, and no effort was made
to secur the adoption of like legislation in that state.
At the outbreak of the world War I entered the U. .
Navy in the rank of Commander of the Navel Reserves. uring
the war the Culifornia fish ries were suddenly greatly ex-
panded, a considerable number of expert Jepanese fishermen being
imported. At the end of the war my attention was called to the
situation on the California coust. An effort was made to secure
the passage of an uct similar in its effect to that of the rest
of the coast, but by that time the Japanese had become entrenched
so strongly with thoir financial and business inter-relations with
American operators that the bill was beaten at Sacramento; it is
said by the use of a large sum of money.

DEPARTMENT OF COMMERCE
BUREAU OF FISHERIES
WASHINGTON
March 25, 1936.
17
Proposed article concerning Japanese salmon
fishing in Bristol Bay.
Mr. Stedman H. Grey,
Editor, Pacific Fisherman,
71 Columbia Street,
Seattle, Washington.
Dear Mr. Gray:
I have your letter of March 17, enclosing copy of an item which you
are considering for publication in the April 1ssue of Pacific Fisherman
on the subject of Japanese operations off the Alaska Peninsula.
I am considerably concerned about the printing of any such article.
I feel that it would be unwise at this time and that it might embarress
officials of this Government in approaching Japanese authorities to re-
strain Japanese activities so that American int rests long established
in these waters will not suffer.
Certainly before printing any article of this nature, consideration
should be given to the accuracy of your source of information that Japa-
nose are entering, or may enter, waters in the Alaska Peninsula region
to engage in salmon fishing. I think it highly important to secure con-
firmation of any such information for th reason that it is not in accord
with the latest official information available here. I shall be glad if
you will let me know how vou learned of the matter.
Through the State Depertment we have been kepping in close touch
with the situstion and, as a result, it s ems clear that the Japanese
Government has not licensed any vessels to engage in salmon fishing in
waters along the Alaskan coast. It further seems clear that there is no
likelihood of the isauance of any auch licenses in the near future.
This statement is based upon reliable official sources.
My best judgment is to let the matter rest for a while, for I feel
that by so doing we shall avoid any controversy that will rise up to
plague us when, a little later on, we have hopes of making formal arrange-
ments with the Japanese authorities whereby our salmon fishery interests
along the Alaskan coast may not be harmed as a result of Japanese offshore
salmon fishing activities.

=== Page 458 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
I think we must take a sufficiently long-range view of the
situation to avoid embarrassment in the future in connection with
negotiations which are likely to develop. It does not seem the
course of wisdom to publish aäything which may stir up the Japa-
nose along this line.
It must be remembered that the Japanese for a number of years
have been sending their floating crad canneries and subsidiary
fleet to the waters of Bristol Bay. Japanese officials have re-
peatedly asserted that there was no intention of licensing these
vessels to engage in salmon fishing. To date, so far as this
office has been able to ascertain from official sources, there hes
been no infringement of this agreement. of course, there may be
a few stray salmon picked up during the progress of their activi-
ties, but they have made no definite or concerted effort to can
salmon in these waters.
Under the circumstances, I think that you should withhold
publication of the proposed article. I appreciate very much your
thoughtfulness in iving me an o portunity to review the item,
and likewise I shall esteen it a privilege if you will give me a
chance to pass upon sny similar item which you mey think of pub-
lishing in the future. I mention this hecause we have sources of
official information here thet may put a very lifferent face upon
some item which at first might appear suitable for publication.
Very truly yours,
Bli
Commissioner.
TB:FJH

=== Page 459 === (confidence 0.99)
CFISHERMAN
(C O P Y)
bia Street
Washington
March 17, 1936.
F. Frank T. Bell,
U. S. Commissioner of Fisheries,
Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. Bell:
We would ask your attention to the attached item, concerning Japanese salmon
fishing in the vicinity of Bristol Bay, on which we have just received some new
information. No doubt you have observed the item in our January issue (page 21)
on the demand of Japanese companies for licenses to pack salmon in Bristol Bay.
For many years we have felt uneasy over the danger of the Japanese invading the
Bristol Bay salmon fishery, but have carefully refrained from any publicity on
the subject, in the view that any comment might hasten the trouble. It now
seems, however, that the invasion has already comnenced, so there is no apparent
reason for avoiding the subject.
It will be greatly appreciated if you will inform us whether the American Gov-
ernment has taken cognizance of this matter, or done anything or formulated any
policy in that connection.
I thought it best to write you about this matter rather than take it up direct
with Secretary Roper, but would like to suggest that you discuss it with him,
and would urge him to place it before the State Department for any action that
can be taken. We consider it of vital importance that our government take
action. and that a definite policy be developed t rough collaboration of the
best ability and knowledge in the Commerce and State departments.
While we cannot lawfully extend any control over Japanese activities in extra-
territorial waters, we believe that our government should stand firmly on the
principle that the Bristol Bay salmon runs, spawning in American waters and built
up and maintained by Americans, are listinctly an Anerican resource; that we
should be able to find some diplomitic means of obtaining Japanese recognition of
this principle, so that their nationals vould be required to keep their salmon
gear out of those waters.
We also consider it of the utmost importance that some branch of our government
maintain en active, thorough patrol of those waters during the packing season,
to check up on what the Japanese are doing and see that they do not invade our
territorial waters.
The preservation and upbuilding of the Bristol bay salmon resources have cost
our government and our packers a great deal of expense and trouble, and are of
enormous value. However, if they are now to be invaded by the Japanese, it is
doubtful whether any further effort at conservation would be warranted -- we
might about as well suspend the regulations and grab what we can in the five
years or so before the run is exterminated. But we earnestly hope that a bet-
ter way can be found.
Other Bristol Bay questions have no doubt already given you plenty of concern.
We have your letter about the number of boats, which will be used in our April
issue. It is unfortunate that this Japanese trouble has come up, but the issue
is apparently one that cannot longer be avoided, and the time has come for our
government to take a firm and vigorous stand.
Very truly yours,
S.H.Gray
PACIFIC FISHERMAN
D
(Sgd.) Stedman H. Gray, Editor
Eno

=== Page 460 === (confidence 0.99)
(C O P Y)
FOR APRIL PACIFIC FISHERMAN
Japanese Operations
Off Alaska Peninsula
(12)
From Japanese sources, presumably authentic, comes a report that three or
four Japanese "mother ships" operating last season in the vicinity of Bristol
Bay, produced, in addition to canned crabmeat, large amounts of salt codfish,
frozen fish, oil and meal, cod liver oil, and a substantial quantity of canned
sa lmon.
This is the first time that any salmon pack has been reported on the part of
Japanese outfits operating in the vicinity of Alaska, although they have been
working in thet district for a number of years -- originelly fishing only for
spider crabs, which have not been utilized commercially by Americans. In the
last few seasons, however, it has been known thet some of the Japanese "mother
ships" were accompanied by extensive trawling outfits, working over grounds
commonly frequented by American cod fishermen; although Americans have not been
permitted to use drag trawls in these waters.
These vessels, as far as is known, have not ettempted to fish within American
territorial waters, but little is known in this country about their operations.
Americans, whether of private or official status, have renerally been refused
adm1ttance to their shins. In 1934, however, it was reliably reported that
they were actively trawling an: takinz a heavy tonnage of bottom fish -- mainly
Alaska hake, but including also considerable yuant ities of codfish and halibut,
If they are now seeking to intercept the salmon run bound for the streams of
Bristol Bay -- the greatest source of Red salmon und the most valuable fishing
area of North America -- it will inevitably raise questions of unusual gravity,
even though the Japanese vessels remain in extra-t-rritorial waters.
The Bristol Bay salmon fishery is distinctively an American resource, even though
the fish at some stages of their life pass outside of territorial waters. The
American government, and American packers through many years by rigorous regula-
tion and other measures of protection, and at great expense to themselves, have
succeeded in maintaining and building un the Bristol Bay salmon resources. Any
relaxation of effective control would result in their rapid destruction, and it
is up to our government to see that they are adequately protected.
What policy or course of action the United States government will adopt in this
connection is unknown at this writing, but it is not to be expected that a matter
of such importance will be allowed to pass unnoticed.

AIR MAIL
Honorable Frank T. Bell,
U. S. Commissioner of Fisheries,
Washington, D. C.
Dear Frank:
Doctor Thompson called me yesterday
morning to tell me that a Professor Konda, a Japanese
professor from some fisheries college in Hakodate,
was here in towm looking into the possible fishery
difficulties between Japan and the United States.
I endeavored to contact him but found he had already
left for Bristol Bay. Professor Konda purports to
be making this trip for the purpose of promoting a
peaceful adjustment between Japan and the United
States of fishery matters. It is to be hoped that
he is sincere in this regard and that his government also
is sincere. You may draw your own conclusions. In any
event I thought you might like to have this information,
if you did not already have it, so as possibly to com-
municate with your representative in Bristol Bay.
Very truly yours,
(Sgd.) EDWARD W. ALLEN

DIVISION OF
June 4, 1936.
NOTE
Mr. Bower of the Bureau of Fisheries
adled this afternoon to furnish me with
copies (attached) of correspondence between
the PACIFIC FISHERMAN and the Department
of Commerce with regard to Japanese fishing
operations in the Bering Sea.
We discussed Tokyo's telegram No. 120,
June 4, 10 a.m., and we agreed that there
appeared to be little prospect of persuading
the Japanese Government to enter in the
Co
near future into any agreement which would
have the effect of prohibiting Japanese
nationals from fishing in Bristol Bay.
Earlier today, I discussed the questi
with Mr. Metzger of Le. Mr. Metzger said
C8
that, if the Japanese engaged in fishing
in the open waters in Bristol Bay, this
Government would have no basis for protest
5
to the Japanese Government until or unless
4
the Japanese operations were such as to
do serious injury to the salmon resources
of the United States. He thought that it
might be possible to invoke one of the
principles underlying an argument success-
fully used by this Government in the 
Alabama Claims against the British Govern-
ment -- a government renders itself liable
7
to damages if it should permit its
6
territory to be used as a base for
Ti
operations injurious to the interests
G
of another government.
I

=== Page 474 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2 -
I repeated to Mr. Bower that which
Mr. Metzger had previously said to me.
We agreed that nothing further should be
done in the matter for the time being
and until there had been further develop-
ments.
Mr. Bower and I agreed that Tokyo's
telegram No. 120 contains information of
a disquieting character. The telegram
indicates, in our opinion, that the salmon
resources of Bristol Bay are being explored
with a view to the use by Japanese fishermen
of that area should the time come when it
will not be possible or profitable for
Japanese to fish for salmon in Russian
waters.

Japanese vessels in Bering See.
Mr. Stedman H. Gray,
Editor, Pacific Fisherman,
71 Columbie Street,
Seattle, Washington.
Dear Mr. Gray:
894.628/1
Note is made from your letter of May 25 that you will make the in-
formation conveyed in the Bureau's letter of May 20, 1936, the basis of
an 1tem regarding Japanese vessels in Bering Sea for the June issue of
Pacific Fisherman.
It seems clear you do not have much faith in assurances of the
Japanese Government that its vessels are not permitted to engage in any
line of fishery operations except those for which specifically licensed.
In this connection may I remind you that in the past six seasons opera-
5
tions by Japanese floating fishery plants north of the Alaska Peninsula
4
there is no evidence that they have interfered with the runs of salmon
passing through those waters en route to the spawning grounds of Bristol
Bay er olsewhere. It may be that a few salmon have been picked up in-
cidemtally but, as stated by you, such number probably is not large.
I agree with you in regard to the necessity of doing all that we
can to safeguard the salmon fisheries of Alaske from exploitation by
foreign vessels. Along this line, hewever, I have complete confidence
N17 1936
in the high ability and fine judgment of officials of the State Depart-
ment who have this subject under close observation. If there are any
indications that Japanese vessels may engage in the salmon fisheries of
Alaska, the State Department will ascertain that fact promptly and ac-
tion will be taken to protect our fishery resources.
Until there is evidence of any overt act by Japanese fishing ves-
sels, or unfriendly intention upon the part of the Japanese Government,
we must assume that their assurances along this line are valid. There
is nothing to indicate disturbance: of the present friendly relations of
the two governments in regard to the salmon fisheries of Alaska.
F/
Very truly yours,
F

Boll

=== Page 476 === (confidence 0.99)
(COPY)
PA C I F I CF I S H E R M A N
May 25, 1936.
71 Columbia Street
Seattle Washington
Mr. Frank T. Bell,
U. S. Commissioner of Fisheries,
Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. Bell:
Yours of May 2Oth, with notes about Japanese vessels for the Bering Sea
fisheries in the vicinity of Aleske, is greatly appreciated and will be
used as the basis of an item for our June issue.
I note your remark that the Japanese government does not permit its
vessels to engage in any type of fishery operations other than that for
which they are licensed. That, no doubt, is theoretically true. However,
I am pretty well convinced that salmon from the Bristol Bay runs have been
taken and processed by such vessels, in quantities not known to us (prob-
ably not large), incidentel to other operations. My anxiety is based on
the thought that this may just be the thin edge of a wedge.
I personally have little confidence in any official assurances that the
Japanese may offer. I feel that the whole record of their international
relations shows a policy of encroachment, accompanied by reassuring state-
ments, until such time as their activities are entrenched, when they come
out flatly with declarations of what they will, and will not do. For that
reason, I feel that our government should seek to establish some definite
principles concerning the salmon fisheries off Alaska, and see that they
are adhered to.
The question in my mind as to the taking of Alaska salmon by Japanese, is
not what the Japanese government tells us is being done, but what actually
is being done; and I feel very strongly that our government should insist
on maintaining a fairly close scrutiny of such operetions. Assurances
from the Japanese government are not worth the paper they are written on.
Very truly yours,
PACIFIC FISHERMAN
(Sgd.) Stedman H. Gray
Editor
S.H. Gray

From - PACIFIC FISHERMAN
June - 1936
p. 37
JAPANESE FLOATERS START FOR BERING SEA FISHERY
Ambezeador Grew at Tokyo was informed May 9 by the
Japanese Foreign Office of the departure from Hakodate of
two vessels with twelve accessory motor boats for Bering
Sea (presumably in the vicinity of Alaska) for crab fish-
ing. One of the vessels will be engaged principally in
making fish meal.
Commonting on the above, Commissioner Bell says:
"As crab fishing and the making of meal will comprise the
activities of these vessels, there should be no cause for
alarm that our salmon fisheries will be invaded. It must
be remembered that the Japanese government does not per-
mit its vessels to engage in any type of fishery operations
other than that for which they are specifically licensed
before leaving Japanese ports."

Dear r. ry
The receipt is acknowledzed of your letters of June 9 and 1l with
regard to the possible invasion of the salmon fisheries off the Alaskan
coast, partioularly in Bristol bay, by Javanese flosting ca neries and
accompanying fishing veseele.
The information conveyed by you is of real interest. It is evident
thet you heve an active corres ondent in Japan who kesus you wosted con-
Cerning important fishery develovmonts of int rest to American industry.
We appreciste very much the informstion thus received, as it comes from
a different source and reflects the point of view of versons not directly
conneoted with the Covernment. This is almays heluful in sheving the
polioy to be pursued.
Ageneies of our Covernment concerned in the matter have not been
ignorant of develomments pertaining to this situs ion. The probability
that the Japanese would eventually manifest interest in this industry
has been realised for many yeers end develouments ave been cerefully
followed since le29. Maturally, there are feetures of diplomatic moment
that cannot be made wublie.
Five years ago a setisfactory arrangement was on the poimt of being
consmmated but fell through. If en understanding hed been reached with
the Japamese Government at that time, it seems probable the present diffi-
culties would not have arisen, or, in any event, would have been minimized.
At that time the Jepanese Government seemed definitely committed to the
poliay of kmeping its fishing weseels entirely ewey from hish sea watere
off the const of Alaska, insofar as they might in any way affect salmon
fishing: they were content with crab fishing activities.
It promises to becosw a difficult wroblem to deel with, however, if
the Japanese Government insists upon permitting its vessels to engege in
salmon fishing n waters of the high seas off the Alaskan coast; they are
within their international legal rights in any such undertekin, unless
we can reach an agreament with them not to do se.

=== Page 488 === (confidence 0.96)
-2-
If the Japanese Government should insist upon permitting its
nationals to engage in salmon fishing in waters of the high seas,
there would be created a situation which has features of similarity
between the present salmon fishery situation and that which existed
years ago with respect to the fur seels of Alaske, ond which cul-
minated in the Bering See Award, no doubt familier to you.
We are not entirely eatisfied, however, that there would be
no legal warrent for this Government taking reasoneble and empropri-
ate measures for the conservation of an i dustry in wh ch there is
invested large sums of Americen cavital and on which the liveli-
hood of many Americans devends.
This office has thought of the feasibility of sending en
expedition o" our owm to determine how and to what extent salmon
can be caught in high sea waters of bristol bey. The experienes
of a mumber of years ago by the Carlisle Pecyins Commany, under the
immediate supervision of Mr. J. N. Gilbert, demonstrated clearly the
feasibility of operations of this character. Furthermore, e large
part of the catch of salmon in Bristol Bay is now being made on the
high seas by American fishermen. The necessity of an official in-
vestigetion of this nature now, therefore, is not clenr; moreover,
ny such demonstration at the present time mi tht ive Jauanese i-
tereste heloful ideas in reeerd to the conduct of offshore sol on
Pishinp opsretiona.
Mo doubt one of the objectives of the Tenyo Haru, which we hay
recently learned is to go to Bristol Bey this sumer and for the
next two years, is to asoertain how, when, end where salmon may be
caught in international waters. Plans are under way to have the
sctivities of that vessel cheeked closely so we may know very defi-
nitely what is done by this Japanese investigative exmedition.
In the New York Times of June 14, 1936, there was printed an
article from Sydney under the title "Japanese Pearlers Annoy Aus-
tralia." nclosed is a copy of that item. It will be noted there-
from how Javenese pearlers conducted overations just outside the
three-mile limit and in addition indulged in the practice of recondi-
tioning their vessels within territorial limits, and procured fresh
water without regard to the requirements of local lavs and regulations.
You may rest assured thet this Government will take a firm stand
in dealing with the Japanese fishery eituation along the Alaska coest
as it now exists.

June 11, 1936.
Mr. Frank T. bell,
U. S. Commissioner of Fisheries,
Washington, D. C.
Referring further to the Japenese-Alaska question, I have just received a
private letter from Japan dated May 23rd, which says:
"In the extra session of the Diet now oven, a petition was raised an
accepted by the government as for one wortn considering. Its appearance was
rather of a sudden to us. It was raised by a out 1o0 members of the lower
House, including both the Seiyukai and Minseito, the two major political par-
ties of Jauan.
.. . . The petition reads as fo lows:
"'The government should, in acccrdance with the requirements and hopes
of the civil people engaged in the industry, immediately estanlish a policy of
development of the open-sea salmon fishery industry on the northeastern waters
of the Pacific Ocean, and should endeavor to realize the scheme fruitrully."
"The reason attached to the petition rencs:
""There is little roon for the further expansion of the selmon isheries
that have been develoved by our people on and off the Far Eastern coast of
U.S.S.R., where the output is gradually dwindlin. Of late the depression of
the coastal fisheries of Japan proper, and the exhaustion of the domestic
fish:ng villages necessitate the develooment of newlv-onened fields, this nec-
essity being accelerated by the increased vooulation of fishery peoole. At
this time there exist best salnon fisheries of the world to the east of the
North Pacific Ocean.
"At present we watch the fact of canned salmon production in the anount
several times as much as ours yearly on the coast of Alaska. Thus our fishery
people are keenly hoping for fishing salmon on those waters. Tue governnent
snould make en epoch in Japen's fishing industry and correspond to the timely
need by inmeciately granting permission to the selmon fishery on the motner-
ship system to operate on the North Paciic Ocean incl ucing that cood loca+ion."
I think the above substantiates some of my former co: ments on the subject. It
reminds me of one of the early Turkish conquerors, who bequeathed to his sons,
the ermire of "Roum" or Rome -- all they had to do was go and take it away
from the Romans.
Very truly yours,
PACIFIC FISHERMAN
(Sngd.) Stedman H. Gray
S.H.Gray

Humbie Street
