June 9, 1936.
Mr. Frank T. Bell,
U. S. Comnissioner of Fisneries,
Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. Bell:
Meny thanks for your letters of June 1, end June 5tn rerarding Jananese
fishery operations in Berin Sea. Any further infornation vou can send us
on this subject will be greatly anpreciated.
I fully understand that o ficial state ents fren our gov rnment -- n the
absence of concrete evidence to the contrary -- must be based on an assumo-
tion of good faith on the part of foreign govern ents. On the other hand, I
feel that it is a duty of our government to maintain a constant watchfulness
as to what is actually bein done in matters of this kind, and I do not know
of eny way in which the State Department can maintain such a watchfulness ex-
cept through the co-operation of other agencies, such as the Coast Guard, the
sureau of Fisheries, Navy vessels, etc., end the authentic observ: tions of
private parties.
While statements fron private sorces ere, of course, not toune y such rules
of internetional courtesy or diplomacy, I do not feel thnt inaccurete or un-
duly provocative statements from any source are justiried.
The Japanese dispatcn which you quote seens to be more or less addled. It is
a matter of common opinion, and I believe, of kmowm fact, tnat many previous
Japanese expeditions to waters near Alaska, heve been accomoanied by govern-
ment officials and fishery experts -- this is by no means the first one.
Whether or not the Jaoanese government has "hitnerto refused permission to
operate in Alaskan waters", we know thet they have operated in waters closely
adjacent to Alaska for a number of years. The statement, "It is not considered
feasible to extend the Jepanese fisheries to Alaska", strikes me as extremely
raw, as neither foreign fisnery onerators, nor scientific investigators could
properly be permitted to operate IN ALASYA WATERS (that is, witnin territorial
waters of the United States).
However, the dispatch, with the further report from Ambassador Grew, will prob-
ably be of interest to our readers.
Very truly yours,
PACIFIC FISHEHMAN
S.H.Grey
(Sgd.) Stednan H. Gray
C
Editor
P.S.-- Studying over Mr. Grew's report, I an struck with the suspicion

=== Page 492 === (confidence 0.99)
Mr. Frank T. Bell, Page 2.
June 9,1936
that the scientific exoedition mentioned is carrying out the intention
reported in the January PACIFIC FISHERMAN, page 21, in regard to Bristol
Bay Salmon fishing: that the Japanese government "would never give the
license for some time, but that they would investirate the problem both
politically and scientifically", etc. It looks to me as if the present
purpose is to determine whetner t: ey can catch salmon in worth-while
numbers in the "open" waters of Bristol Eay, outside the three-mile limit.
If this proves to be physically possible, I think our government will have
to exercise a lot of firmness and diplomatic resourcefulness to preserve
the Bristol Bay salmon run as an American resource. There is no doubt in
my mind that the Japanese are preparing to demand & share of the Bristol
Bay salmon (provided they can be caught successfully more than three miles
offshore), and I feel that our government should now be layin the ground-
work for a determined resistance of such demands.
Further, it seems to me tnat it would be a very wise action on the part of
our government -- and a thing taat ought to have been done lon- ago -- to
make a little scientific investigation of its own to determine the economic
possibility of conmercial salmon fishing, with all kmovn types of gear, just
outside of the three-mile limit on Bristol Bay. Aside from providing knowledge
which our government ought to have for conservetion, regulatory and other pur-
poses, this would give an onportunity to keep an eye on what the Japanese ex-
pedition is doing, and also show then that our government is not asleeo at
the switch.
S.H.G.

of 5-20-64 from Dunsid 1. Sincn, State Dept.
Boculer Date 3///6
I understand that the Japanese Diet had presented to
1t a very strong petition urging that the Japanese fishing
interests be allowed to conduot unrestrioted fisheries on
the Alaske side of Bering Sea, particular emphasis being
laid upon the canning of salmon aboard mother ships or
floating canneries. Just what aotion 1s being taken on
this potition I do not know.
I have previously been informed that the Japanese were
sending over anothor fishery investigation vessel, and that
recently a member of the faoulty of the Fisheries College
at Hakodate who was here in Seattle left for Bristol Bay
to study the situation.
One of the 1deas expressed in this petition was that
the Siberian fisheries were playing out. The Japanese, as
you know, have for many years had speoial privileges 1n
Siberian waters, in addition to whioh they have fished
salmon extensively three miles ofr the Kamohatkan coast,
where they have operated floating canneries in these inter-
national waters. It 1s my understandiny that a fishery
of this kind is very destruotive.
Apparently the reason the Japanese wish to come over
to the Ameriean side and fish for salmon is beoause of the
inevitable effeot of their ruthless fishing methods on
their own side of the ocean. I can see serious trouble
ahead ir they should send their mother ships into Bristol
Bay, even though they remain more than three miles off
shore.

mercia! Secretary of the Canadian Legation at Tokyo, concerning
Japanase salmon fishing operations off the Kamchatkan coast, and in-
timating the possibility of extending such activities to the Alaskan
coast.
We are keeping very close watch of this situation. Officials of
the State Department, both here and in Tokyo, are prepared to take e
firm stand in dealing with the situation. Naturally, the problem is
difficult, should the Japanese Government insist upon allowing its
vessels to fish for salmon on high sea waters off the Alaskan coast.
In so doing, they would be within their international legal rights
unless some other agreement is reached with them.
Thanks for the information which you have forwarded. Should aay-
thing else along this line reach vou, your courtesy in sending it to
this office will be appreciated.

Salmon Pack Prospect: in Kamchatka
Salmon pack of the Nicniro Fishery for this season in Kanchatka,
according to the estimation of the company, is expected to amount to
360,000 cases of reds and 630,000 cases of pinks as against the actual
pack of 179,714 cases of reds and 654,658 cases of pinks for the 1955
season and also as against the actual pack of 500,oll cases of reds and
889,138 cases of pinks for the 194 season. It is expected by the
company that reds pack in the west coast of Kanchatka for this season
will show far better result then tnat shown during last season when,
despite an anticipation of good result by the fishing cycle year,
resulted in a very poor pack. Salnon fisin in Hokkaido which
commences as tne forerunner of the Kamcnatka salmon fishing is not very
good by the floating ice and cold waves while that of Saghalien shows
an activity for this year. The reneral expectation of reds pack in
Kamchatka is that the Nicniro Fisnery will nrocure a normel pack for
this year.
It is reported that the Nichiro Company's supsidiary company
by the name of the Taiheiyo Fishery chartered a floating cannery and
sent it on Mey 7th to the Gul: of Anadyr for fisning investigations.
It is said that the Gulf of Anadyr is considered to be orospective
salmon grounds owing to the fact that waters are adjoining to the
famous salmon grounds in the Gulf of Alaska. It is also reported that
if this expedition results in good catch, the Japanese company will
positively advance to these grounds.

Conversation
July 6, 1936.
Ir. Neville and r. Okamoto, Chief of the
American Bureau of the Foreign Office.
I told ir. Okamoto that the Embassy had received in-
formation from Washington that a Japanese vessel named
CHICHIBU MARU had been observed fishing for salmon with
gill nets in Bristol Bay, and asked him whether the
Foreign Office had any information in regard to this
vessel. He sent for the file and the Section Chief in
charge of it, and after some conversation with him and an
examination of the dossier, Mr. Ckamoto said that there
was no information on file at all, and that the Foreign
Office knew nothing about it. This statement was confirmed
by the Section Chief who was emphatic that the Fisheries
Bureau had given the Foreign Office no information in re-
gard to this vessel. Mr. Okamoto said that the Foreign
Office would investigate the report and give the Embassy
what information it could obtain.
Mr. Okamoto then stated that he had first become ac-
quainted with this question when he was Consul at Seattle.
He said that the Japanese Government was under some pres-
sure from time to time to grant licenses to fish in Bristol
Bay, but so far had refused, because in the first place
such action would irritate the Americans and secondly it
was doubtful whether such fishing would be profitable. He
said

=== Page 514 === (confidence 0.96)
91
- 2 -
said that one object of the investigation which the
Fisheries Bureau was making was to ascertain the com-
mercial feasibility of fishing off Alaska.
He then said that another question which was brought
up from time to time was the problem of fur seals. He
said that the Japanese Government had first raised the
question of revision of the fur seal treaty some ten years
ago, but that the American Government had been unable to
consider the matter because it had no diplomatic relations
with the Soviets at that time. I told him that I was
unable to discuss that question because I had no instructions
to that end, and asked him if the Japanese Government wanted
to raise the question now. He said that so far as he knew
the Japanese were not ready to do so now, but the treaty
had expired, was in force only from year to year and there
were certain features of it which he understood the Japanese
wish modified. He seemed unable to give more detailed
information.
E.L.N.

=== Page 517 === (confidence 0.99)
tract - from Pacific Fisherman
July 1936 -- p. 24
JAPANESE EYE NORTH AMERICAN FISHERIES
A Tokyo press dispatch of May 30, in which the facts seem to be
pretty badly garbled, said: "A Japanese trawler with experts aboard
will be sent to Alaska to investigate the prospects of open sea fish-
ing enterprises there. Hitherto the government has refused permission
to operate in Alaskan waters because of American fishing interests,
according to the newpaper "Asahi". It is now considered feasible to
extend the Japanese fisheries to Alaska, and the Tenyo Maru, a vessel
of 600 tons, will sail next month to begin scientific investigations.
A recent session of Parliament voted 89,000 yen for the inquiry, which
will continue three years."
In this connection the U. S. State Department is advised by
Ambassador Grew at Tokyo that the Japanese Bureau of Fisheries intends
to conduct certain investigations in the open waters of Bristol Bay,
and that such investigations will probably extend over three seasons.
A trawler or other ship will be chartered for this purpose. Mr. Grew
was informed that no private ventures will be authorized, and that the
Japanese government will keep him advised on the subject.
Japanese fishery operations within the territorial waters of
Alaska could not, of course, be legally permitted. Whether with or
without government authorization, it is well known that a number of
Japanese floeting fishery plants have operated in extra-territorial
waters for some years past, engaging primarily in the crab fishery and
oil and meal manufacture; and it was announced a month or two ago that
one of them was leaving for waters in the general vicinity of Alaska.
Japanese Demand Development
Private advices from Japan, dated a week earlier than the above,
state that a petition on the subject was presented in the special
session of the Diet in May, and accepted by the government as worthy
of consideration. While its appearance is said to have been unexpected
by the larger fishery operators, it was brought up by about lo0 members
of the lower house, including members of both political parties of Japan.
The petition is to the effect that "the government should, in ac-
cordance with the requirements and hopes of the civilian population en-
gaged in the fishing industry, immediately establish a policy of develop-
ment of the open sea salmon fishing industry in the northeastern waters
of the Pacific Ocean, and should endeavor to effectmatte the plan."

=== Page 518 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
The petition was based on the statement that "There is little room for
further expsnsion of the salmon fisheries that have been developed by our
people on and off the far-eastern coast of U.S.S.R., where the output is
gradually dwindling. Of late the depression of the coastal fisheries of
Japan and the exhaustion of the domestic fishing villages neoessitate the
development of new fields, this necessity being augmented by the increased
population of fishing people.
"At this time the best salmon fisheries of the world are to the east
of the north Pacific Ocean. We now observe the production of canned salmon
on the coast of Alaska to an amount several times as great as ours yearly.
Thus our fishery people are keenly hoping for the opportunity of salmon
fishing in those waters.
"The government should make an epoch in Japan's fishing industry, and
meet the present need by imnediately granting permission for the salmon
fishery on the mother-ship system to operate on the North Pacific Ocean,
including that desirable location."
It is also stated that the government had included in the budget for
1936 and for the two years following, an item entitled "Investigation of
the Fisheries of Alaska," under the Department of Agriculture and Forestry.
Japanese in Australian Waters
In this connection it is interesting to note a recent news item from
Sydney, Australia, reporting "increasing tension" and "incessant irritation"
resulting from the epparent intention of certain Japanese vessel owners
engaged in the pearl fishery "to ignore Australian laws to fish in prohibited
waters and to defy customs, immigration and quarantine acts."
Last fall, it is recounted, twenty-five Japanese pearlers beached south-
west of Bathurst Island and leisurely cleaned their vessels, and journeyed
to inland pools to replenish their water supply, in defiance of customs,
immigration and quarantine laws. "Most Japanese pearlers operating in the
Arafura Sea", it is stated, "are believed to have defied Australian law at
different times. When the ships are scraped and repainted, they move back
just outside the three-mile limit to begin diving work again." Such intru-
sions have been the subject of protests for two years.

By ASPomly 3/1/C7
My dear Mr. Jones:
The receipt is acknowledged of your letter of
August 26, 1936, in which reference is made to an
894
appropriation of the Japanese Government for conducting
investigations of the fishery resources of waters off
62
the coast of Alaska and in other parts of the eastern
8
Pacific, mention being made in this connection of an
9
article appearing in the July issue of PACIFIC FISHER AN.
The possibility that Japanese fishery interests
might operate in waters off the coast of Alaska,
especially in the non-territorial waters of Bristol Bay,
has engaged the attention for several years past of the
Department and of 1ts representatives in Japan. So early
as 193l, the desirability was suggested to the Japanese
Government of the withholding to Japanese fishing vessels
of licenses to fish in Bristol Bay, into which, as you are
no
The Honorable
Jesse H. Jones,
Chairman, Reconstruction
Finanoe Corporation.

=== Page 520 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
no doubt aware, flow the rivers where are spawned the
highly prized red salmon. Unfortunately, the Japanese
Government did not find it practicable to give this Govern-
ment a formal undertaking that it would take effective
measures to prevent Japanese fishing vessels from operating
in Bristol Bay, but informal assurances were given that no
licenses would be issued to private Japanese fishing
interests to fish for salmon in that area without previous
notification having been given to this Government.
Since 1932, the Japanese Government has each year
sent, with the previous Imowledge and consent of this
Government, one or two of its public vessels to American
territorial waters off the coast of Alaska for the purpose
of studying the fishery resources of those waters, and not-
withstanding reports which have appeared in periodicals in
this country to the contrary, this Government had no know-
ledge, until the early part of this year, that Japanese
vessels had engaged in the catching of salmon. In view of
the evidences of increasing interest of the Japanese in the
fishery resources of Bering Sea, the American Embassy at
Tokyo was instructed last year to inquire whether the
Japanese Government would be disposed to enter into
negotiations with this Government looking toward the con-
clusion of a convention whereby Japanese nationals would
be prohibited from engaging in fishing for salmon in
certain

=== Page 521 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
certain non-territorial waters of Bristol Bay. The
Embassy was informed that the Japanese Government would
not be in position to decide whether or not it would
enter into suoh negotiations until after investigations
which were being made into the fishery resources of Bristol
Bay had been concluded. However, further assurances were
given the Embassy that no licenses would be granted for the
time being by the Japanese Government to private fishing
vessels to fish for salmon in Bristol Bay. It is under-
stood, however, that one or more vessels belonging to
the Bureau of Fisheries of the Japanese Government have
recently been conducting experimental operations in
Bristol Bay.
The preceding paragraphs may perhaps evidence to you
the solicitude and concern with which this Department has
been following developments affecting the livelihood of a
large number of American nationals and the investment of
substantial sums of money in one of the most important
industries of Alaska, and which might conceivably destroy
a valuable national resource. The Embassy at Tokyo and
the Department, together with the Bureau of Fisheries of
the Department of Commeroe, are attentively following
the situation, and you are assured that no effort will be
spared to preserve the salmon fishing industry against
operations

=== Page 522 === (confidence 0.82)
operations by foreign nationals whioh may be deemed to be
injurious to it.
Sinoerely yours,

The receipt is acknowledged of your letter of August 28,
forwarding at the request of Mr. Miller Freeman a copy of his
letter of August 2, 1936, addressed to you concerning the in-
vestigations in Alaskan waters this season by Dr. Seiji Kanda,
of the Hakodate School of Pisheries, and the report which he
submitted at Yosemite, California, in August before a committee
of the American Council of the Institute of Pacific Relations.
The information thus furnished is of great interest to me.
The subject of Japanese fishery activities in the high sea waters
of Bristol Bay will require our most careful thought and consid-
eration. I plan to look into the matter further when I em on
the Pacific Coast next month.

Yosemite Lodge
Yosemite, Cal.
August 23, 1936.
Mr. Edw. Allen,
Northern Life Tower,
Seattle, Wash.
Dear Mr. Allen:
On my reaching here Aug. 15, Dr. C. L. Alsberg, Chairman
of the American Council of the Institute of Pacific Relations, in-
formed me that Seiji Kanda had arrived at Yosemite and had requested
he be heard with reference to certain proposals re the fisheries
of the North Pacific, which he wished to offer for consideration by
the Institute of Pacific Relations.
Dr. Alsberg informed the writer that Kanda had not been
invited; that he had appeared of his owm volition; and that he would
call a few men together to hear him privately, on the following
afternoon. Those present:
Dr. C. L. Alsberg, Stanford University
Dr. Chas. E. Martin, head Political Science Dept.,
U. of Washington, Seattle
B. H. Kizer, Spokane
Miller Freeman, Seettle
Dr. V. E. Motylev, Director Institute Great World Atlas;
Professor in Institute National Economy, Moscow,
U.S.S.R.
Vladimir Romm, U. S. Correspondont, "Izvestia", end
delegate to the I. P. R.
Dr. Shiroshi Nasu, Professor Agriculturel Policy, Tokyo
Imperial University
Dr. Kanda
Dr. Kanda stated he was connected with the Hakodate School
of Fisheries, and an official of the Jananese government; that the
Japanese government had conducted some research work in Bristol Bay,
including the visit during the early summer of a scientific vessel;
that he had to offer two points for consideration by the Institute
of Pacific Reletions, namely:
1. That joint research be undertaken by the governments
of the United States and Japan in Bristol Bay, and along
the Aleutian chain of islands of the salmon fisheries
of that area for the purpose of establishing a basis of
negotiations for a treaty by which the rights and in-
terests of each nation would be determined.
2. The Russian-Japanese fisheries problems of the
Asiatic Coast, including questions of administration and
means to be adopted to preserve the salmon fisheries of
that area which he feared will be depleted by over-fishing.

=== Page 526 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Mr. Freeman asked Dr. Kanda if the views and attitude of
the United States government had been solicited in advance of the
dispatch into the Bristol Bay area of the Japanese fisheries re-
search vessel. Dr. Kanda replied that he had been given a copy of
the telegram sent by Mr. Wingard from the Seattle office of the
U. S. Bureau of Fisheries to its Bristol Bey agent, instructing the
latter to extend any possible courtesies to the expedition; that he,
Dr. Kanda, had been hospitably entertained by cannery operators of
the Bristol Bay area.
Dr. Motylev stated that while he was not fully informed
on Asiatic fisheries problems, he felt that all questions in that
area were being satisfactorily dealt with by the two governments,
Russia and Japan.
While the possibility of a scientific study of the fish-
eries of the entire North Pacific area to be participated in by the
United States, Canada, Japan and Russia was briefly referred to,
it was not pursued to any definite conclusion.
During the discussions Dr. Kenda expressed his pleasure,
as well as his surprise, that the United States government should
so generously receive the visit of the Japanese fisheries vessel;
that Japan would not be as willing to allow a scientific expedi-
tion to conduct operations along its shores.
As agreed to in advance, the conference came to no con-
clusions, agreeing that the matter should not come before the
sessions of the Institute of Pacific Relations, but that the matter
would be considered by the National Research Committee of the In-
stitute, when decision would then be reached as to whether it
should be made a subject of study by the Institute.
Yours very truly,
Miller Freeman

Ralph C. Baker, Acting Assistant Agent
Juneau, Alaska
July 16 - 31, 1936
1936
July 18 - Professor Seiji Kanda of the Hakodate Fisheries
School of Japan was taken from Juneau to the
Funter Bay area on the Widgeon to study the
type of salmon traps used in Alaska. After
being shown a pile trap and a floating trap Mr.
Kanda was taken from the Alaska Pacific Salmon
trap No. 36-364 back to Naked Island where he
made connections with the commercial passenger
vessel Bstebeth and returned to Juneau. The
Widgeon then continued the patrol toward Hawk
Inlot.

=== Page 529 === (confidence 0.98)
Daily Alaska Empire
agust , 1936.
JAPAN STUDYING FISHING, BRISTOL BAY REGION, NOW
Operations Constitute Serious Threet to American Exports
Seattle, Aug. .- Scientific investiration of the possibilities
of salmon fishing in extra-territorial waters in the vicinity of Bristol
Bay has been carried on during the last two months by scientists and
students aboard the Hakuyo Maru school ship of the Imperial Japanese
Government Fisheries Institute. The Halnuyo Maru is now in Seattle pre-
paring to sail south to study fishing opportunities off the California
coast.
Carrying twenty-three members of the Imperial Institute's graduating
class the Hakuyo Maru left Japan May 1. Purros  of the institute
curricula is to fit students to command and onerate floatiny canneries.
Students are thoroughly trained in methods of findins the fish at
sea, in freezing and canning operations, and in navigation. The Hakuvo
Maru carried experimental enuipment for the packing of salmon, halibut,
tuna and shell fish.
Samples of water are also being drawn along the
route of the vovage.
These sanles will be shipped to Janan for
analysis.
Although a large part of the ponulation of Japan is encaged in
fishing, that country consunes very little of its annuel canned salmon
pack. Most of the output is shipped abroad. The success the Japanese
have had in world marketins of canned salmon, crab, and cther items is
an indication of the ability of their fisheries, with the aid of low
labor costs, to go into a market at substantially lower prices than
packs of other countries. The projected expansion of Japanese fishing
operations constitutes a serious threat in American export markets.

Cordova, Alaska, Daily Times
July 11, 1936.
STUDIES ALASKA FISHING INDUSTRY
Anchorage, July ll. - S. Korda, a student officer
of the Japanese government, is here studying the Alaska
fishing industry.
He said there was vital need for the formation of an
international committee between Japan and the United
States to study the salmon movements in the western
Bering Sea and north Pacific districts.
Korda is a professor of Hakodate College of Fisheries
in Japan. He left Friday for Seattle.

=== Page 533 === (confidence 0.99)
m Pacific Fisherman
agust - 1936 (p. 22)
JAPANESE COMMENCE INVESTIGATION OF ERISTOL BAY SALMON FISHERY
Some further details have been learned concerning the Japanese in-
vestigation of salmon fishery possibilities in extraterritorial waters
adjacent to Alaska, for which the Diet durine the soring voted an appro-
priation, as reported in these columns last month.
No time has been lost in starting the n st gation which, it is
understood, is to be continued for a period of three yenrs. The purpose
is to determine the feasibility of floating salmon cannery oneration in
those waters, in response to a demand of Jananese fishery interests, with
the aim of locating Japanese floating canneries outside the three-mile
limit in Bristol Bay.
It is now announced that the Tenyo Maru, a cs?-ton vessel of the
trawler type, and an accompanying auxiliary motor vescel of el tons, have
been assigned to this work, inv sti atinr the abundence of salmon end other
fishes and studying marine and meteorolorical conditions of the Alaskan
coast.
Aside from this, two Japanese floating factories, accompanied by
accessory vessels, have been operating this season in offshore waters of
Bering Sea adjacent to Alaska. These are the Toten Maru, 6,ooo tons, en-
gaged mainly in canning crabneat; and the Taihoku Maru, 8,253 tons, en-
gaged in fish reduction and miscellaneous operations.
TRAINING SHIP IF BRISTOL BAY
What appears to be an initial step in the salmon iavestigation was
carried out early in July, at the berinning of the Red salmon season,
through a visit to that district of the fisheries training ship Hakuyo
Maru, operated by the Tokyo Fish ng Institute. After spending several
weeks in Bristol Bay, she put in at Seattle July 24, renaining for the
week-end, and proceeded to Monterey, Calif., and the Hawaiian Islands.
The Hakuyo Maru is a good-sized modern steel vessel with diesel
power, well equipped with machinery and gear of commercial type for fish-
ing and processing experiments, and with laboratory and scientific
apparatus for oceanographic research. On the present voyage her officers
and crew, with teaching staff, number over 40, with about 30 students.
She is commanded by Capt. Jinzo Nakagawa, who is also a professor in the
fisheries school; other faculty members being Prof. Saito, marine biologist
and oceanographer; Prof. Yamasaki; Prof. Yasuhara and Prof. Dunno. The
mate also teaches navigation, while the engineer gives instruction in
diesel engine operation.

=== Page 534 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
he vessel's equipment includes a small but complete and very compact
ming line, with hand-filling tables, vacuum closing machines for talls,
etort, ete., with a daily capacity of avout 150 full cases of halves,
suitable for packing salmon, tuna, crabmeat, ete.; a reduction outfit of
about 5 tons daily fish capacity; a small freezer and cold storage room,
and facilities for salting salmon in the Japanese style. Fishing is done
by means of small boats, including two launches about 10 metres long with
45-hp. gas engines, which renain out all day with crews of 8 men each, and
are equipped with radio direction finders; also two large rowboats of whale-
boat type. None of these boats appeared to be especially designed for any
particular kind of fishing.
While in Bristol Bay, Japanese gill-nets were used for salmon fishing,
and no difficulty was found in making fair catches of Red salmon. The
catch, when canned, produced about 60o full cases of helves, which was con-
sidered hiphly satisfactory as an experiment.
FURTHER INVESTIGATION
The vessel also carried large-mesh gillnets for spider crabs; halibut
gear, somewhat resembling the American type, which is handled from the small
boats; and tuna gear of a Japanese type. In using the latter, barbed hooks
with squid as bait are hung by long, stout leaders or gangions from a long
line; the latter being suspended horizontally at considerable depth from
floats at the surface. Experiments in tuna fishing will be made during the
latter part of the voyage. It was stated that no fishing for pilchards is
contemplated on this trip, and the reduction plant has not been in operation.
The Hakuyo Maru touched at several points in the Aleutians on the east-
ward run, and proceeded direct to Seattle after completing her work on
Bristol Bay. From Monterey she is going to the Hawaiian Islands, and thence
to San Cristoval in the Solomon Islands, stopping every four hours to take
soundings and collect oceanographic data, with tuna fishing experiments
wherever conditions seems favorable.
PROF. KANDA ON TOUR
In addition to the investigations aoove mentioned, Prof. Seiji Kanda of
the Imperial Fisheries School at Hakodate has been making an extensive study
of the Pacific salmon and herring fisheries and looking into the research
and conservation methods followed in this country. During May he visited
the Seattle laboratories of the Bureau of Fisheries and the International
Fisheries Commission, and the California Fish and Game Commission and other
fishery research agencies in California. Later he went to Alaska for a
first-hand survey of fishery operations and returned the end of July, plan-
ning to attend the Institute of Pacific Relations at Yosemite this month.
He has suggested the development of an international program of scientific
research on the salmon of the north Pacific.
He has expressed particular interest in the regulatory methods of our
state and federal governments in connection with the salmon fisheries, as
the Japanese government has come to see the necessity of effective regula-
tion to preserve the salmon resources of Kamchatka and northern Japan. He
has given attention to salmon tagging operations, taking the view that such
work may be an important feature of an international study of salmon migrations.

From:

1L31
To :
Commander, Bering Sea Patrol Force.
Subject:
HAKUYO MARU and activities.
Inclosure: (1) Schedule of cruise of HAKUYO MARU, 1936.
1.
The HAKUYO MARU entered Kiska Harbor at O850, 9 June, 1936, and
anchored about five eights of a mile off shore southeast from observation station.
Shortly thereafter the boarding officer from the CYANE boarded the HAKUYO MARU and
was met at the gangway by the chief officer. He was shown to the saloon and there
was introduced to the captain, J. Nakakawa and two professors of the Tylo Fishing
Institute of the Japanese Imperial Government. He was shown around the ship
gonerally and ascertained, in general, the followin; information: The crew proper
was 29 men, 23 cadets, or students of the Tyko Fishing Institute, and 16 officers
and professors. The quarters for the cadets is separate from that of the officer
or crew. It consisted in generul of staterooms, one for every four students, a
chemical laboratory for the study of marine life and a combined study room and mess
hall. The HAkUYo MARU is a two masted diesel powered vessel about two hundred
thirty feet in length at the water line, with well deck forward and deck and pilot
house approximately amidships. The vessel is painted white all over except for a
band at the top of the dummy stack, which is painted black. The stack is just aft
of the pilot house. The vessel has about & thirty-five foot beam and draft of
between twelve and fifteen feet. The H kUYo MARU is equipped with two 7oo horsepower
diesel engines, each drivin a screw, and is capable of making approximately 12 knots;
an Aoton (British( yro canpass with  hree repeaters; electric or hand steering;
sonic dopth finder of British make accurate up to 450o fathoms; a radio direction
finder in the starboard wing of the bridge. The hold in the well deck forward con-
tained a small experimental cannery.
2.
The motor sailer of the l KUYO MARU cume alongside the CYAE at about
lo30 towing the CYAnE's surfbout, the boarding officer having accepted Captain
Nakakawa's offer to tow them back. Capt in Nakakawa and a professor came on board
to call on the commandin officer. Both understood English if spoken slowly, and
could speak it as well, and were able to read and write English. Captain Nakakawa
stated that he had made several previous cruises from Japan to the Bering Sea and
was familiar with the waters, but hed never been in Kiska Harbor before. He stated
that they were using British Admirality chiarts for the Bering Sea. Captain Nakakawa
also stated that the Japanese cannery ships TAIHOKU MARU and TOTEN MARU were fishing
in Bristol Bay and the RYOKAI MARU was crab fishing off the Komandoraky Islands and
with them would be four trawlers of about 2o0 tons. The professor stated that the
students were making the cruise to get experience in the various types of fishing
encountered in the various places. The captain asked permission for the students
to go ashore for exercise. This permission was granted. The motorboat went ashore
at about 13oo towing a small surfboat in which the men landed on the beach.
The mon, after getting on the beack, walked around for a short time and then
climbed the high hill at the head of the bay. They returned to the beach in
about an hour and a half and were picked up by the motorboat and surfboat
and roturned to the HAKUYO MARU.

=== Page 540 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
S. Captain Nakakawa stated that he planned to sail from Kiska
Harber about noon the next day. The HAkUYo MARU got underway about 1520,
10 June, and stood slowly out of Kiska Harbor going about three quarters
of a mile off shore until reaching the northern end of Kiska Island and
then steamed north northeast until about three miles north of Segula
Island and then stood east northeast (about 65 true) sailing the general
great circle course toward Bristol Bay.
4.
The CYANE got underway at 1550 and stood slowly out of Kiska
Harbor attempting to keep at the limit of visibility astern of the HAkUro
MARU averaging a speed of about 10t knots. The CYANE, on the two nights that
the HAkUYO MARU was trailed, ran dark. The trail was dropped ina position
approximately 54-51-00 N., 171-25 W.

CORDOVA Daily Times - July 20, 1936.
JAPANESE OFFICIAL VISITS
K. Okamoto, Japanese consul at Seattle, passed
through here southbound on the Yukon Saturday on the
final lap of a trip through Alaska which he stated
he has enjoyed immensely.
The visit of Mr. Okamoto marks the first time
a Japanese government attache of his rank has come
in Alaska. He has been at the head of the Seattle
consulate since June, his previous duties having
been as an attache of the Japanese embassy at Wash-
ington.
He called on Mr. Mitamura while in Cordova.

Guard Cutter
TREASURYDEPARTMENT
ALERT
UNITED
STATES COAST GUARD
27 July, 1936.
From:
Commanding Officer, ALERT.
To :
Commander, Bering Sea Patrol Force.
Subject: Japanese Motor Vessel TENYO MARU of Shomonoseki.
1. The TENYO MARU was located by the ALERT on July 20, 1936,
anchored in Latitude 57-55 North, Longitude 158-15 West. She had one
crab trawler, a vessel of about 75 feet, with her. The trawler had
completed taking in all gear prior to the arrival of the ALERT, and
no further activities were undertaken while the ALERT was in that
vicinity.
2. The TENYO is a vessel on the beam trawler type, about 200
feet long, 40 feet beam, and drew 4 meters aft and three one half meters
forward; her displacement is about 2,000 tons. She is painted white,
the cfficers on the bridge are in uniform, and all her actions and
appearance lead to the full belief that she is a Government vessel
conducting some research work for further expansion of Japanese fishing
activities.
3. The radio antennae equipment of subject vessel is very
elaborate and she unquestionably guards at least 4 frequencies at all
times. Her radio direction finders are also of the latest type, two
on the wings of the bridge. In addition she is equipped with a battle
ship type range finder.
(Signed) H. IANSON.

Bureau of Foreign & Domestic Commerce,
Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. Rawls:
There is inclosed an editorial taken from the Alaska Weekly of
last Friday, telling of the trip of the Hakuyo Meru, school ship of
the Imperial Japanese Government Fisheries Institute.
In visiting trade publicetions with Mr. Hayward while he was here,
we met and talked with Mr. Miller Freemen who mentioned the Hakuyo Maru,
and Mr. Hayward and I went down and visited the ship. So far as I can
see, the situation is pretty much as described in this editorial, and
you can readily imagine from the fact that there were some twenty odd
graduating students abroad and some 5C odd regular students of the amount
of activity. Essentially, each one of th se students is bein& trained
and equipped to operate floating canneries on the east coast of the
North Pacific Ocean outside of the three mile limit.
The question is, "Are we goir: to let our fisheries go by defeult
to the Japanese, who will not regard the restrictive meesures placed
on the American fisheries by our Bureau of Fisheries, or whether sone
action is to be taken to preserve the Alaska salmon fisheries to American
fisherman?"
I would appreciate any co ments you wish to give me on this situation.
Very truly yours,
(Sgd.) CRAWFOFD
Philip M. Crawford
Assistant District Menager.

=== Page 547 === (confidence 0.98)
aska Weekly
5th Ave.,
Washington
dy 31, 1936.
A SERIOUS SITUATION
What appeers to be the usual interesting and innocent news iten is con-
tained in the story of the scientific investigation of the possitilities of
salmon fishing in extra-Territorial waters in the vicinity of Bristol Bay
which has been carried on during the last two months by scientists and students
aboard the Hakuyo Maru, school ship of the Imperial Japanese Government Fish-
eries Institute. The Hakuyo Maru is now in Seettle preparing to sail south to
study fishing opportunities off the Celifornia cost. Let us exemine the
objectives of this little party.
Carrying twenty-three members of the Imuerial Institute's graduating
class the Hakuyo Maru left Japan May l?. The purpose of the institute curricula
is to fit students to command and operate flcetin canneries. Students are
thoroughly trained in methods of finding the fish et sea, in freezing and can-
ning operations, and in navigation. The Hakuyo Maru carries exoerimental
equipment for the pac ing of salmon, halibut, tuna, and shell-fish. Samples of
water are also being drawn along the route of the voyage. These semles will
be shipped to Japan for analysis. Floating canneries and trainint ships are
officered by naval reserve officers and trained naval observers.
Although a lerge part of the population of Janan is engaged in lish ng,
that country consumes very little of its annual canned salmon pack. Most of
the output is shipped abroad. The success the Janenese have had in world
marketing of canned salmon, crab, and other items is an indication of the
ability of their fisheries, with the aid of low labor costs, to go into a
market at substantially lower prices than packs of other countries. The pro-
jected expansion of Japanese fishing operations constitutes a serious threat
in American export markets, in the domestic markets and to the industry itself.
The Japanese have demonstrated that they can catch red selmon with gill
nets on the high seas and it appears that there is nothing we can do about it
unless international agreements can be arrived at. If they can fish outside
the three-mile limit off Bristcl Bay and take 25 per cent or more of the red
salmon before they come wi+hin the bay it presents a problem of serious and
baffling nature for the most important industry of Alaska. Salmon are, how-
ever, worthy of a slightly different classification than strictly "high ses"
fish. The salmon is born in American waters and must return to American
waters to complete his life cycle. He is, in effect, an American citizen.
pen discussions in the Japanese Diet are convincingly clear on one point -
namely that the Jaranese do not intend to leave us to the peaceful exploitation
of this great national asset. Aggressive action on the part of American in-
terests is mandatory if the salmon packing industry of this coast is not to be
seriously crippled. The Association of Pacific Fisheries has been making ex-
haustive investigations, Mr. Miller Freeman is doing valuable work, but com-
munity, Territorial, and national consciousness should be aroused to the
situation and all possible aid given the industry.

Japanese fisheries in Bristol Bay.
Mr. Stedman H. Gray,
Editor, Pacific Fisherman.
71 Columbia Street.
Seattle, Washington.
Dear Mr. Gray:
In answer to your letter of August 22, l936, wherein you ask about
a decision under which the waters within Bristol Bay, Alaska, more than
three miles from the shore line are designated as ertraterritorial waters,
I wimh to say that there is no decision which mentions specifically Bris-
tol Bay in this connection.
No doubt you are well acquainted with the long-recognized principle
that jurisdiction ertends three miles offshore. This was set forth in a
lotter written by an official of the State Department on August 10, 1936,
to Captain F. K. Dedriok, Vice-President and General Manager, California
Whaling Company, 1l90 St. Charles Street, Alameda, California, from which
I quote the following:
Jos 8' 1Be16.
Generally speaking, the rule adopted by this Government is
that which is set forth in the case of the Cunard Steamship Com-
pany v. Mollon, 262 U.S. 100. That rule is stated as follows:
"It now is settled in the United States and recognized else-
where that the territory subject to its juriadiction includes the
land areas under its dominion and control, the ports, harbors,
bays and other enclosed arms of the sea along its coast and a
margimal belt of the sea ertending from coast line outward a
mrine league, or three geographic miles."
In view of observations mede by me on my trip to Alaska this summer,
I can see no objeetion to the publicatiom in the Pacific Fisherman of in-
formation comcermimg the Japamese salmon fishery situation in the Bristol
Bay region.
In amy sueh artieles it is urged that there be a clear-cut statememt
of faets, mot mrked by vituperation or animosity. It seems to me that

=== Page 550 === (confidence 0.91)
-2
Amin statement of facts and of the coneequences to American resources
and investments will present a strong case in bringing to the people of
this country the seriousness of the situation confronting a long-established
Ameriean industry. Not only will this affect those who are ongaged ac-
tually in fishing operations, but it may have serious bearing upon the manu-
fmeturers of sin plate, cans, fiahing gear, and other iteme and comodities
of wide variety whieh are used extensively in the salmon fishery of Bristo!
Bey.
Very truly yours,
Frank T. R

Evidence that the Japanese Government has not altered
its viewpoint since that time is to be found in a brief
newspaper discussion of the salmon fisheries uestion
which appeared in the CHUGAI SHOGYO SHIPO of October 23,
1936. Referring to the fact that Canedian fisheries
interests showed concern because of the operations of
Japanese ocean-going fishery vessels off the coasts of
British Columbia and Bristol Bay, Alaska, last summer,
the newspaper remarks that Japanese fisheries operations
in those vaters are threatening to develop into a tri-
partite affair between Japan, the United States and
Canada. The following is a summary translation of what
purports to be the combined views of the Foreign Office
and the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry:
"What the Canadian fishery industry most fears
is the possibility of Japanese fishermen reducing the
quantity of red salmon in Canadian waters, the great-
est fishery resource in that region.(Whereas Japan
annually produces 500,000 cases of red salmon in the
northern industry as a whole, Canada produces 2,o00,000
cases). It is quite clear that Cenada's concern is
based on this danger.
"The authorities of the Ministry of Agriculture and
Forestry, however, have already adopted the policy of
prohibiting Japanese salmon floating canneries from
operating in Canadian and Alaskan waters. In the case
of last summer's fishery operations off British Co-
lumbia the authorities restricted the operations to
catching crabs only. Even in the future the authori-
ties have no intention of permitting fishery opera-
tions in that region.
"Supposing that the Canadian Government approached
Japan in co-operation with the United States authorities
for conclusion of an international agreement regarding
prohibition or restriction of fishery operations off the
territorial

Government should refuse to respond favorably to such
a proposal, considering that there is no reason for the
conclusion of such an agreement".

Particularly acrimonious was the century-long
dispute between England and the Netherlands over the
herring fisheries along the British coasts. This
dispute dated from the Proclamation of James I, of
May 6, 1609, prohibiting fishing by foreigners along
the British coasts except by yearly license from British
authorities. This proclamation, notwithstanding its
general terms, was, in fact, directed against the Nether-
lands fishermen, who, towards the end of the sixteenth
century had begun to designate the herring fishery on
(2)
the British coasts as their "great gold mine".
The
bitterness of the conflict may, in no small measure,
be attributed to the fact that the belief was prevalent
in England that this herring fishery along the British
coasts was the primary source of the "commerce, wealth
and naval power"(3)
of the Netherlands. Fulton, in his

The capture by the Netherlands authorities in 16l? of
one Brown sent by the Duke of Lennox, on the orders of

was criticized by Grotius in his Mare Liberum appear-

This proclamation was followed on May 10, 1635 by
that of Charles I, referring to and approving of the
position adopted by his predecessor. A fleet was sub-
sequently dispatched by Charles I to enforce the pro-
visions of this latest proclamation.
The question of the fisheries played an important
part in the negotiations preceding the outbreak of the
First Dutch War and during the ensuing peace negotia-
tions.

the Second Dutch War and following the Third Dutch

This long dispute between England and the Nether-
lands over the herring fisheries off the British coests
was accompanied by a dispute between the same two
States regarding whaling rights along the coasts of
Spitzbergen. In 1613, the Muscovy Company, chartered
by James I, sent to Spitzbergen a fleet of seven armed
vessels to defend such rights as were granted by
charter and to catch whales. These vessels proceeded
to despoil two Netherlands vessels of their catches and
fishing gear. In the following year, eighteen Dutch
whalers sailed for the Arctic under the convoy of
three States' men-of-war. However, attacks by the Eng-
l1sh upon Netherlands whaling vessels were resumed in
1617 and reprisals were taken in the next year by the
Netherlands.
A protracted dispute between the Netherlands and
Denmark concerning fishing and wheling rights off the
coasts of Greenland, Iceland, Norway and Spitzbergen
troubled the relations between these two States in the
seventeenth and eighteenth centuries. Danish decrees
of

By the mediation of Sweden and of the British and French

In their disputes with Netherlands fishermen,

Denmark, in its disputes with England, claimed

In its disputes with the Netherlands, Denmark asserted
varying claims as to the extent of its territorial
waters reserved to nationals of that State. In 1636,
a zone of sixteen to twenty-four geographical miles
was declared by the Norwegian Government to be re-
served to its nationals. In 1692, whaling was pro-

miles of the coasts. In 1733, Denmark reserved by

(3)
leagues off the coasts.

The question of the North Atlantic fisheries led
to protracted disputes between Great Britain and France
(3)
and Great Britain and the United States.
The dispute
between Great Britain and France takes its origin in the
provisions of the Treaty of Utrecht of April 11, 1713,
confirmed by the treaties of Paris (February 10, 1763),
Versailles (April 3, 1783), Paris (May 30, 1814), and

The dispute between Great Britain and the United
States originated in the provisions of the Treaty of
Versailles (April 3, 1783). On October 20, 1818, a conven-
tion was concluded on the fishing rights of American and
British (including Canadian) subjects on the Atlantic
coasts of North America. Article one of this convention
accorded rights to inhabitants of the United States to
fish along certain parts of the coasts of Newfoundland
and Labrador, and to dry and cure fish on the unsettled
parts of the southern coast of Newfoundland and of the
coast of Labrador above mentioned. Furthermore, the
United States renounced the right to "take, dry, or cure
fish on, or within three marine miles" of the coasts of
British dominions in North America not included among
those above mentioned. This article was at issue in the
North Atlantic Fisheries award of l9lo delivered by the
Hague Arbitral Tribunal.
The numerous difficulties occasioned by the enforce-
ment of this convention(1) and of the Hovering Act of
Nova Scotia of 1836(2) resulting in numerous seizures of

In 183l, a particularly bitter dispute arose between
the United States and the Republic of Buenos Aires by
reason of the capture, by authority of the latter Repub-
lic, of American vessels in the Falkland Islands on the
pretext of violation of certain laws of the latter Re-
(1)
public for the protection of the fisheries.
The
United States held such seizures to be a violation of
the acquired right of American fishermen to use these
islands for shelter and for purposes incident to the
exercise of fishing rights. An American war-vessel was
despatched to the Falkland Islands for the protection
of American commerce. On February 14, 1832, the Minister
of Foreign Affairs of the Republic of Buenos Aires in-

In 1837, a mixed Anglo-French commission was
instituted to settle complaints concerning the conduct
of British and French fishermen off the coasts of
Great Britain and France. A convention regulating
the various controversies was concluded in l839. Similar

1852.
Numerous difficulties occasioned by the close
proximity of British, Belgian, French and Netherlands
fishing vessels in the North Sea led finally to the
North Sea Fishery Convention concluded in 1882 between
Belgium, Denmark, France, Germany, Great Britain and
(3)
the Netherlands.
The claims advanced by Russia with regard to
fishing rights off the coasts of that State have led
to numerous controversies with other States and particu-
larly with the United States and with Great Britain.
In 1821 a ukase issued by the Tsar of Russia declar-

The dispute with the British Government over the
(1)
enforcement of American legislation
for the protec-
tion of fur-seals in the Bering Sea arose out of the
seizure by American authorities of British vessels and
their condemnation in the United States Court at Sitka
for violation of such legislation. Such seizures took
place in 1886, 1887, and 1839. The controversy growing
out of these seizures and condemnations and involving
the question as to the right of the United States to the pro-
tection of and property in fur-bearing seals of the
islands of the Bering Sea, was, by the Convention of
February 29, 1892, submitted to arbitraton. The regula-
tions established by the arbitral tribunal in its award
of August 15, 1893, and by the subsequent convention of
July 7, 19ll, concluded between the United States, Great
Britain, Russia and Japan for the preservation and pro-
tection of fur-seals, were effective in terminating
this dispute.(2)

=== Page 571 === (confidence 0.98)
-18-
field conflicting international claims tend to assume
very readily the character of disputes of a nature to
trouble the good relations between the interested
Statea The riparian States feel often obliged, for
the preservation of their local industries, to assert
jurisdiction over coastal waters to an extent deemed
by other States to be incompatible with their own exist-
ing rights and with the freedom of the high seas in the
matter of fisheries.
That, however, these questions are readily susceptible
of solution on the hasis of bilateral and multllateral
agreements and of arbitration is proved by the bilateral
conventions between Great Britain and France (1839) and
Great Britain and Belgium (1852), by the multilateral
convention of 1882 regulating fishing in the North Sea,
by the North Atlantic Fisheries Arbitration of 19lo, and
by the arbitral award accompanied by the bilateral (1893)
and multilateral (l9ll) regulations with respect to
the protection of fur-seals in the Bering Sea.

AR LASTLR ALTAIRS

THESECRETARY OF STATE
3mnc
WASHINGTON.COPS SENT TO
O.N.L NEED.
SIR:
I have the honor to enclose herewith for the
Department's information a copy of a memorandum prepared
CO
94.6
for this office by the Chief of the Fish and Game
Administration of the Philippine Islands on the ac-
2
tivities of Japanese nationals in the fishing industry
n that area.
7
O
It is interesting to note that the fishing industry
of Hong Kong and the surrounding South China area is
Wargely in the hands of Japanese nationals by virtue of
their up-to-date equipment and general efficiency.
The leading Japanese fishing company in Hong Kong
is the Horai Gyo Gyo (The Horai Fishing Company). The
head office of this company is at Keelung, Taiwan, and
it has a branch at Takao in southern Taiwan.
The company has been fishing off the China coast
since about 1915 but the Hong Kong office was not opened
until 1930. In 1935 the Hong Kong branch increased in
size. It now has an office staff of 18, mostly of the
Japanese race. It has one office on a main business
street of Hong Kong and a purchasing department near
F/FG
Causeway Bay
1.

=== Page 577 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Causeway Bay where its trawlers, like some Chinese
vessels, seek shelter inside the breakwater.
Usually about 12 to 16 trawlers of this company
make Hong Kong their base.
At one time most of the
trawlers were operated by steam but in recent years the
company has been inclined to favor trawlers with Diesel
engines for its fleet.
The original cost of most of the
trawlers is said to have been about Y350,000.
The trawlers
have cold storage plants on board with an average capacity
of 40 tons each. They also have radios so that they can
communicate with one another.
Occasionally some of the trawlers of the company are
detached from the Hong Kong base and sent to greater dis-
tances. During the first part of 1935 one of the trawlers
which had been operating in South China waters and which
had been transshipping its catch every two months at
Hong Kong went thousands of miles away and started fish-
ing in the waters off western Australia. This trawler
now transships its catch to Japanese vessels at Singapore
instead of Hong Kong.
. On one trip back to Singapore
recently it brought more than 2,000 cases of frozen sea
bream and red porgy with a reported weight of 83 tons.
The trawlers which still use Hong Kong as their
base usually work within a radius of 800 miles especially
in the direction of French Indo-China. The average
cruise lasts two weeks but they may stay away from Hong
Kong for periods varying from one week to six weeks or
more.
The trawlers sometimes carry a crew of as many as
25 men for fishing and preparing the fish.
During the past three months, however, there have
been

=== Page 578 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
been indications that local Chinese fishermen are
awakening to the danger of their position. On
December 14, 1936, a Guild of Fishermen and Fish
Dealers was formed for the first time in Hong Kong.
The SOUTH CHINA MORNING POST of December 15, 1936,
commented as follows on the formation of the Guild:
"This Guild is the reply of the
local dealers, whose position, in common
with other dealers in South China ports,
has been seriously challenged by competition
from Japanese interests.
In its wider aspect, the Guild is
intended to include in due course, fishing
operations carried out from Macao, Swatow,
Pakhoi, Haihow, Kwangchowwan, and other
South China centres which now import more
Japanese than Chinese marine products."
In the discussion which attended the formation
of the Guild it was pointed out that local fishermen
retain only about 30 per cent of the Hong Kong fishing
trade and it was admitted that the business had been
lost to Japanese interests due to lack of up-to-date
equipment. How to remedy this defect is a difficult
question in view of the clumsy and obsolete procedure
of Chinese junks as compared with the speedy steam
driven Japanese trawlers.
According to the SOUTH CHINA MORNING POST of the
same date a large number of the 70,000 inhabitants of
Hong Kong formerly engaged in the fishing industry have
been thrown out of employment while the circumstances
of whose who remain have been greatly reduced.
On Jenuary 14, 1936, the SOUTH CHINA MORNING POST
reported that large numbers of fishermen in the Swatow
and Chaochow area had been thrown out of employment as
a reault of Japanese penetration in the fishing industry
of that area. Commenting on the subject, the POST
said:

=== Page 579 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
said:
"The situation at Nannao, an island off
the Kwangtung coast, which is one of the most
prosperous fishing grounds in the area, is said
to be the worst, where most of the able-bodied
men, driven by poverty and starvation, have
fled from the place. Previously the total
population there was about 50,000 of whom about
70 per cent. were fishermen and 20 per cent.
farmers. After the exodus of the unemployed
fishermen, there are now only about 34,000 left."
It was reported in the local press of February 20th
that the local Fishermen and Fish dealers Guild had
presented a petition to the authorities in Nanking
requesting a subsidy of Mex$200,000 per annum and on
March 2, 1937, the local press stated that such a sub-
sidy had been granted.
The Secretary of the Guild informed the Consulate
General that such a petition had been presented but
that reports of the granting of a subsidy were in his
opinion premature.
Respectfully yours,
Howard Donovan,
American Consul.

Enolosure to despatch No. 509 dated April &, 1937

COPY
March 15, 1937.
MEMORANDUM regarding the activities of the
Japanese National Fishermen in the
Fishing Industry in the Philippines.
Under the provisions of Act No. 4003, the Fisheries
Act, which took effect on December 5, l932, only aliens
who were already operating fishing boats of more than
three tons gross and alien fishermen already engaged in
fishing business prior to the enactment of the said Act,
may be duly licensed to fish within the territorial waters
of the Philippines.
During the year 1936, 35 Japanese boats duly licensed
were engaged in the fishing business in the territorial
waters of the Philippines. During the same year, 21
fishing boats were registered in the names of corporations,
39% of the capital of which or perhaps more is Japanese.
It is suspected, in addition, that of the l28 commercial
fishing boats registered under Filipino names in the Bureau
of Customs, about 18 may be owned and operated by Japanese
nationals.
In the year 1936, 614 Japanese nationals have fishermen
licenses to work in boats of more than three tons gross.
This number is a little less than the number in 1933 (701).
No fishermen's licenses have been issued to Japane se nationals
in 1934, 1935 and 1936, except those as renewals of the 1933
licenses
Japanese fishing boats and fishermen using the beam
trawl operate mostly in Manila Bay, Lingayan and Ragay Gulfs
and market their catch in the Manila and Baguio markets in
the ieed state. A number of boats employ the muro-ami
syatem of fishing and fish around the islands north of
Palawan. These return to Manila every 10 days and deposit
their catoh in the Royal Ice Plant. ▲ few operate in
various parts of the Visayas using Iloilo and Cebu as bases.
Most or the Japanese fishermen now in the Philippines
are from the Okinawa-ken district of Japan.
HILARIO A. ROXAS, Chief,
